## 🆕 Re-measured at p717 — the 2026 global bracket, and the LATAM comparison that changes which licences matter

**Global AI-in-education market, 2026, four independent houses:**

| Source | 2026 | Horizon | CAGR |
|---|--:|---|--:|
| Precedence Research | `USD 9.58B` | `USD 136.79B` by 2035 | `34.52%` |
| Research and Markets | `USD 10.6B` | `USD 42.48B` by 2030 | `41.5%` |
| Grand View Research | `USD 11.4B` (2025: `8.3B`) | `USD 57.2B` by 2033 | — |
| Coherent Market Insights | `USD 11.40B` | `USD 57.25B` by 2033 | `16.8%` |

🟢 **The 2026 base BRACKETS tightly — `USD 9.58B` to `11.40B`, a spread of about `1.2×`** — which is the narrowest agreement this file has recorded on a current-year base. 🔴 **The forecasts do not agree at all:** the 2033-ish endpoints cluster near `USD 57B` while Precedence projects `136.79B` by 2035, and the CAGRs run from `16.8%` to `41.5%` — Coherent's `16.8%` is **arithmetically inconsistent** with its own `11.40B → 57.25B` over seven years, which needs ~`26%`. **Cite the base, never the CAGR.** Total edtech for context: `~USD 380B` (Fortune Business Insights via Emerline) to `~USD 404B` (Searchlab), growing `13`–`17%`.

🔴 **The counter-signal, and it should go in any deck that quotes the growth figures:** HolonIQ data reports **edtech VC funding fell to `USD 2.4B` in 2025, down `89%` from the 2021 peak**, with AI-enabled startups taking most of what remained. A `34%` CAGR forecast and an `89%` funding collapse describe the same industry; the reconciliation is that spend is moving from new entrants to **incumbent institutions buying services** — which is a consulting market, not a product market, and is the better reading for a studio.

⚠️ All of the above are vendor-commissioned market research. The bracket is defensible; the endpoints are not.

# 🗺️ Market map — Education

> Key players, market structure and opportunities placed by region.
> Sources this pass were predominantly vendor blogs, market-research marketing and policy trackers. Figures are **directional**; every number below names its source and flags where sources disagree.

## Market size — and why the numbers disagree

### 🆕 Re-measured at p705 — the `2026` figures finally BRACKET, and that is new

🟢 **For the first time this base can quote a `2026` range tight enough to plan against.** This pass's legs returned **`$11.40B`** for `2026` (Coherent Market Insights) and **~`$12.3B`** for `2026` (X-Pilot, attributed to **HolonIQ**) — a spread of **~`8%`** on the same year.

🔴 **The long-horizon series remains irreconcilable and is NOT merged into that bracket:** Azumo's **`$136.79B` by `2035`** is a different horizon on a different basis, and this base's own prior readings have ranged over a factor of five on a single year (`p664`) with a CAGR that `p695` showed to be arithmetically impossible. 🟡 **So: use `$11.4B`–`$12.3B` for `2026` and treat every terminal-year figure on this page as a vendor projection, not a measurement.**

🔵 **Why the `2026` convergence is plausible where the 2030s are not:** `2026` is nearly observed, and both figures are current-year estimates rather than compounded forecasts. The divergence has always been in the growth assumption, and a current-year estimate barely compounds.

| AI in Education (global) | $9.58B (2026) → $136.79B (2035), 34.52% CAGR | 2026–2035 | Precedence Research (read at p655) |
| AI in **K-12** | $0.70B (2026) → $18.47B (2036), 38.1% CAGR | 2026–2036 | Future Market Insights (read at p655) |
| AI in **higher education** | $4.09B (2026) → $13.46B (2030), 34.7% CAGR | 2026–2030 | The Business Research Company (read at p655) |

🔴 **p655 makes the disagreement worse, and that is now the most useful fact in this table.** Five further estimates were read this pass and the 2026 figure for the *same* named market — "AI in education, global" — now spans **$9.58B to $11.40B**, with CAGRs on it spanning **16.8% to 41.5%**. Two sources attach a 16.8% and a 41.5% CAGR to dollar figures two billion apart. **Stop quoting a headline number.** The two readings that are actually usable in a conversation are the *segmented* ones, because their scope is stated: **higher education at $4.09B (2026)** and **K-12 at $0.70B (2026)** — a 6:1 split that matches where this KB finds real procurement, and which no aggregate figure reveals. The **agentic** sub-segment at **$2.37B (2026)** is the one worth tracking year on year: it is ~23–25% of the broad market on any of the above readings, and it is the only slice whose definition matches what Globant actually builds.

**These do not reconcile**, and the disagreement is informative rather than noise. Note in particular that **two sources quote the same dollar figures with incompatible growth rates (25.9% and 16.8%)** over near-identical horizons, which means at least one is arithmetically wrong — a useful reminder that these are marketing documents, not research. Further: "AI tutors" ($3.6B) and "AI-powered education tools" (>$17B) differ by roughly 5× because one counts a product category and the other counts every tool with an AI feature. Do not quote a single market size to a client without naming the scope. Treat the AI-tutor figure as the addressable one for tutoring engagements.

## Global demand-side readings new at p652

| Reading | Scope | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **Only 15% of students say AI is integrated into many of their courses; only 29% believe their instructors are well equipped to guide AI use** | global higher education | Digital Education Council, AI in Higher Education Global Survey 2026 |
| **Purpose-built beats general-purpose, as a policy recommendation**: *"As general-purpose GenAI tools are not designed specifically for learning, education systems should incentivise the development of tools that aim to enhance teaching and learning"*; tools with an intentional pedagogical purpose *"tend to show sustained improvements in learning"*, while *"if designed or used without pedagogical guidance, outsourcing tasks to GenAI simply enhances performance with no real learning gains"* | OECD member systems | **OECD Digital Education Outlook 2026**, published January 2026 *(quoted from search excerpts of the PDF; `www.oecd.org` does not resolve in this session, so the report was not read at first hand)* |
| **April 2026 ADA accessibility deadline for large US public institutions** | United States | secondary (Campus Technology prediction round-up) |
| 2026 framed sector-wide as the year the question shifts from *whether* to use AI to *under what conditions* | global | 1EdTech |

🔵 **The OECD recommendation is the single most strategically useful sentence this page has acquired at p652, because it is an OECD-level endorsement of exactly what this KB's shelf is made of.** "Incentivise the development of tools that aim to enhance teaching and learning" is a policy instruction to fund purpose-built education AI over generic chatbots — and the purpose-built, permissively-licensed options are enumerated in `agents/top.md`. Pair it with the Digital Education Council's 15%/29% finding and the gap is explicit: systems are being told to buy purpose-built tools, 85% of students do not yet see AI in most courses, and 71% do not think their instructors could guide them if they did. **Tool supply is not the constraint; integration and teacher capability are.** That is a services market, and it is what Globant sells.

Four more 2026 estimates, none reconcilable with the others or with the figures above:

| Secondary compilation (Azumo) | **$12.3B** | — | — |

Set against the figures this page already carries ($9.58B → $42.48B by 2030 at 41.5%), **the 2026 base alone now ranges from $9.58B to $12.3B — a 28% spread — and the 2030-2035 forecasts differ by more than 3×.** 🔵 **The useful conclusion is not a number, it is a rule for proposals: do not quote a single market size for AI in education.** Quote the range, name the source, and anchor the business case on something measured instead — teacher adoption (60% US, Gallup), institutional governance coverage (26% LATAM, ~10% EMEA), or the client's own cohort data. Every one of those is a firmer number than the TAM, and each one points at work that is actually billable.

### 🔧 CORRECTED at p695 — `p664`'s "three sources, three CAGRs" is ONE source counted twice, and one of its two CAGRs is arithmetically impossible

🔴 **Read this before using the p664 table below.** It presents three rows as three sources and concludes that "the spread is now a factor of five on the same year, and the sources have stopped converging." **Two of its three rows are the same series, and the CAGR it attributes to that series cannot be the CAGR of the endpoints it prints in the same row.**

**The arithmetic, reproducible from the figures already on this page.** The series is Coherent Market Insights, **$11.40B (2026) → $57.25B (2033)** — seven years:

| Claim as published | Check | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| `p657` row: **~25.9%** | `(57.25 / 11.40) ^ (1/7) − 1 = ` **`25.93%`**; compounding back gives `11.40 × 1.2593⁷ = ` **`$57.25B`** | 🟢 **correct — it is the implied CAGR of the stated endpoints** |
| `p664` row: **16.8%** on the same `$11.40B → $57.25B` | `11.40 × 1.168⁷ = ` **`$33.81B`** — **`40.9%` below** the terminal printed in its own row. `16.8%` needs until **2036** to reach `$57.25B`, or a 2026 base of **`$19.31B`** | 🔴 **impossible as published** |
| `p664` row: *"A second aggregator, same segment — 25.9%"* | `25.9%` is `p657`'s own implied-CAGR arithmetic on the **identical** Coherent series | 🔴 **not a second source — it is this page's own calculation re-labelled** |

🔵 **What follows, and it cuts the other way from how p664 reads it.** Strip the duplicate and the impossible rate, and the p664 table holds **two** real series, not three: Coherent at **25.9%** and the Europe-only estimate at **31.9%**. **The independent spread is narrower than this page has been publishing, not a "factor of five."** The `2026` base spread (`$9.58B`–`$12.3B`, a `28%` range) and the `3×` disagreement in the terminal years are both real and are unaffected by this correction — they rest on genuinely different publishers.

🔴 **Channel limitation, stated rather than hidden.** `coherentmarketinsights.com` is outside this session's allowed network domains, so the vendor's own published CAGR could **not** be read this pass and it is unknown which of `16.8%` or `25.9%` it prints. **The defect does not depend on that:** a row cannot carry endpoints and a growth rate that contradict each other, whoever published them. Where the vendor itself prints `16.8%` alongside `$11.40B → $57.25B`, the vendor's table is the thing that is internally inconsistent and the correct action is to quote the endpoints and mark the rate as unreconciled.

🟢 **Standing guidance is reinforced, not weakened.** `p664`'s conclusion — *do not put a market level in a client deliverable from this file, quote the structure instead* — survives intact. **It was simply argued from a number that was partly this page's own echo.** `T77` predicted this: a knowledge base's own novelty measurement is a channel, and it decays toward optimism; here a self-citation was re-read as independent corroboration one pass later.

### 🔴 Re-measured at p664 — the spread is now a factor of five on the same year, and the sources have stopped converging

| Source class | 2026 level | Terminal | CAGR |
|---|---|---|---|
| Coherent Market Insights (AI-in-education) | **~$11.4B** | $57.25B by **2033** | **16.8%** |
| A second aggregator, same segment | — | — | **25.9%** |
| Europe only (vendor-style estimate) | **$2.64B** | $8.0B by **2030** | **31.9%** |

🔴 **Three sources, three CAGRs between 16.8% and 31.9%, on overlapping definitions of the same market.** The European *regional* figure carries the highest growth rate and the smallest base, which is the pattern a segment shows when its denominator is being defined differently by each publisher rather than when it is genuinely accelerating.

**Standing guidance, unchanged and reinforced at p664: do not put a market level in a client deliverable from this file.** Quote the *structure* — 87% use against 26% strategy in LATAM higher education, 60% of US teachers against most districts having no policy, four US states compelling district policy by statute, education named high-risk in Korea and now Vietnam. Those are counted populations with named sources. The dollar figures are not reconcilable and this base has now failed to reconcile them at p652, p657 and p664.

⚠️ **Adoption figures circulating this pass that this base declines to carry:** *"86% of students across 16 countries use AI in their studies"* and *"83% of K-12 teachers use generative AI"* both trace to a **single aggregator** with no primary instrument named. They are recorded here so the next pass does not mistake them for new evidence.

## Structural read of the market

Three facts shape every engagement:

1. **Usage has decisively outrun governance.** 86% of education organisations use generative AI, and most lack a policy. One 2026 benchmark reports 54% of K-12 students and 92% of university students using AI. The buying trigger in 2026 is frequently *governance*, not capability — institutions already have AI in the building and no control over it.
2. **Agent deployment is still rare.** The Bridges 2026 survey of K-12 and college leaders found relatively few districts and colleges running AI agents in day-to-day operations, with leaders explicitly wary of giving agents access to sensitive student data. The gap between "using chatbots" and "running agents" is where the integration work is.
3. **The evidence base is thin, and buyers know it.** A July 2026 scoping review of 474 studies found the field fragmented, with pedagogical design poorly connected to technical capability. A 2025 study found AI tutors adapt worse in real time than human tutors. Expect pilots with measurement attached, not big-bang rollouts.

| **Learning Equality (Kolibri)** | Nonprofit, open source, **MIT** | 1.2k ★ offline-first learning platform; the only permissive production platform found | Not an institutional LMS — no enrolment-at-scale or gradebook of the Moodle kind | The base for every low-connectivity engagement, and the one platform where a closed derivative is permitted |
| **AI-Shifu (Harbin Institute of Technology)** | Open source, Apache-2.0 | 331 ★; course authoring from a subject expert's intent, embeddable in the client's portal | Small relative to DeepTutor/OpenMAIC; research-led | The authoring end of the pipeline, where the client has subject experts but no instructional designers |
| **Squirrel AI** | Commercial (APAC) | Adaptive learning at scale in China | Closed, regionally bound | Competitive reference for APAC adaptive engagements |

## Opportunities by region

### North America

#### 🆕 Added at p721 — the leg returned REGULATION AND ZERO REPOSITORIES for the FOURTH pass running, and the DEADLINES have now started to bite

🟡 **Fourth consecutive nil on supply, and on a fourth reading it is a property rather than luck.** US authors neither tag repositories by country nor write in a non-English primary language, so both of this base's working region discriminators are blind here. **The region's opportunity is a compliance surface, not a code gap.**

| Datum (read 2026-11-09) | Figure | Why it is the engagement |
|---|---|---|
| 2026 state bills on AI in education | **`134` bills across `31` states** | The volume itself is the market: every bill creates a district-level policy and documentation obligation that nobody is staffed for |
| States requiring **both** state guidance and mandatory district AI policy | **`4`** — Maryland (**SB 720**), Idaho, Oklahoma, Virginia | A *mandatory district policy* is a procurement trigger with a named owner — Maryland's SB 720 requires a designated **coordinator** per district |
| Ohio district deadline | **passed — July 2026** | Every public district in Ohio was required to have a **written** AI policy. A passed deadline with uneven compliance is remediation work, available now |
| California | **AB 1159** bars student data from training AI models; **SB 243** adds companion-chatbot safety and disclosure duties | 🔴 **AB 1159 is the binding architectural constraint in the largest US market: no student data into training, full stop.** It forces retrieval-and-prompt designs over fine-tuning |
| New York | **A 9190** restricts classroom AI to **grade 9 and above**, excepting diagnostics and special-education interventions | An age gate in the product, not in the policy PDF |
| Federal | **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026** advanced out of the House Education and Workforce Committee | Not yet law — treat as direction, not obligation |
| Teacher adoption vs. governance | **`60%` of US teachers use AI tools** (Gallup, cited) while **most US public schools still lack a formal student AI policy** (Child Trends / US Dept. of Education) | 🔴 **The use/policy inversion persists: the tools are already in the classroom and the rules are not.** The studio sells the governed replacement for shadow usage |

🔵 **Opportunity, stated as the asymmetry:** this is the region with the most regulation and the least open-source supply in the entire base (`31 / 345` agent rows). **An engagement here is a compliance-and-integration build on imported components** — take the APAC tutor layer, wrap it in AB 1159-safe retrieval, and ship the district policy artefacts as part of the deliverable.

- Federal activity remains **executive action, not education-specific statute**.

🔴 **The trust gap, and it is the opportunity:** a Gallup figure relayed through secondary coverage puts US teacher AI use near **`60%`**, while most US public schools still have **no formal student AI policy**. 🟡 **Both numbers are secondhand in the sources this leg returned and should be re-sourced to Gallup and Child Trends before they enter a client deck.**

🟢 **Sharpest NA opportunity, unchanged in kind but now dated:** the bindable work is **policy-to-implementation** — model-policy drafting, district rollout, teacher training evidence — on statutory deadlines that are public. 🔵 **And a note the market numbers do not state: a district buying policy implementation is buying consulting, not product. See `720-D` in [`../repos/foundations.md`](../repos/foundations.md) — there is no live permissive school-administration codebase to hand them, so NA engagements are build-or-incumbent, not adopt.**

#### 🆕 Added at p719 — the North America leg returned REGULATION AND NO REPOSITORIES, and the asymmetry is itself the opportunity

🔴 **Stated first, because an unmoved count with no note beside it reads as coverage:** this leg produced **zero filable repositories**, and `North America 30` on [`../agents/top.md`](../agents/top.md) is unmoved as a result. What it produced instead is the densest regulatory supply of any region this pass.

**The state layer is where the binding rules are, and there are a lot of them.** Roughly **`134` AI-in-education bills across `31` states** in the 2026 session, with the posture consistently research-transparency-guardrails rather than mandated classroom adoption:

- **Maryland SB 720** — requires state guidance, **district-level AI policies, and designated AI coordinators**. Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia carry laws in the same family.
- **Ohio** — mandated that every public district adopt a **written** AI policy, deadline **July 2026** (now passed).
- **New York A 9190** — would restrict classroom AI use to **grade 9 and above**, with carve-outs for diagnostics and special education.
- **California AB 1159** — **bars using student data to train AI models.** Separately, OpenAI and Common Sense Media, previously running competing ballot measures, combined them into a proposed **"Parents & Kids Safe AI Act"** initiative.
- **Washington DC** — a non-mandatory policy that **bans AI from grading, discipline, IEP decisions and teacher evaluations.** Narrow, explicit, and the clearest enumeration of the four uses a district will refuse.
- **Federal** — the House Education and Workforce Committee advanced the **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026**; federal executive action remains general-purpose rather than education-specific.

**Adoption is ahead of governance, and the gap is the sales motion.** Gallup, surveying `2,200+` educators, finds **`60%` of US teachers using AI tools**, while a Child Trends / Department of Education analysis finds **most US public schools still have no formal AI policy for students**. UNESCO puts **`70%` of higher-education institutions across Europe and North America** as having or developing AI guidance — note this figure merges the two regions and cannot be read as a North America number.

🟢 **Opportunity, placed and specific.** The buyable work in North America in 2026 is **not a tutor** — it is **policy-to-control translation**: turning Maryland's coordinator requirement, Ohio's written-policy deadline and DC's four prohibited uses into enforced technical controls, with evidence a district can show an auditor. 🟢 **Two rows filed this pass are the natural substrate and neither is American:** [`ksambit/AI_STUDY_ASSISTANT`](https://github.com/ksambit/AI_STUDY_ASSISTANT) is local-only, which satisfies AB 1159's training bar **by construction** with no DPA to negotiate; and the Kenyan [`Moses-Githinji/meta-cv-hktn-edu-agent`](https://github.com/Moses-Githinji/meta-cv-hktn-edu-agent) ships a **two-stage teacher-then-HoD approval gate in its CLI**, which is exactly the artefact DC's grading and IEP prohibitions require someone to produce. **The compliance architecture an American district needs is already on this shelf; it just was not written there.**

🔵 **Unverified, flagged rather than used:** a single blog reports North Carolina earmarking `$10M` for Khan Academy's Khanmigo. One source, not corroborated, not relied on.

#### 🆕 Re-measured at p718 (2026-11-09) — regulation is state-level, moving fast, and every live bill lands on DATA rather than on pedagogy

| Instrument | What it does | Why it reaches an engagement |
|---|---|---|
| **California AB 1159** | Prohibits using **student data to train AI models** | 🔴 The single most consequential line for a studio build: it makes the de-identification layer (`presidio` → `arx` → `opendp`, filed at p718 on [`../repos/foundations.md`](../repos/foundations.md)) a **precondition**, not a hardening step |
| **Maryland SB 720** | Requires district AI policies and a designated **AI coordinator** per district | Creates a named buyer per district and a document a vendor must map onto |
| **New York A 9190** | Would limit classroom AI to **grade 9 and above**, excepting diagnostics and special education | A product with a K-8 surface needs an age gate in its architecture, not its terms of service |
| **Ohio** | Districts required to adopt written AI policies by a **July 2026** deadline | Policy work already done — the procurement question has moved to conformance |
| **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026** | Advanced out of House committee | Federal posture is described as pro-industry; the binding constraints remain state-level |
| **NYC DOE guidance** | Traffic-light framework; student data may not train models | Mirrors AB 1159 at district scale — the same control, a second jurisdiction |

**Volume and direction:** `134` bills on AI in education across `31` states this session, oriented to **research, transparency and guardrails** rather than adoption mandates (MultiState, FutureEd trackers).

**Adoption runs ahead of governance:** `60%` of US teachers report using AI tools while **most public schools still have no formal student AI policy**; `70%` of institutions across Europe and North America have or are developing AI guidance.

**Money with a named product attached:** North Carolina carries a **`USD 10M`** earmark tied to Khan Academy's **Khanmigo**, defended by legislators through controversy. 🟢 **Read this as the reference transaction for the region** — a state-level line item against one named platform is the procurement shape a studio is competing with.

🔴 **Declared gap, stated rather than left as apparent coverage: CANADA returned NOTHING.** The North America leg's results were effectively all United States. No Canadian federal or provincial instrument, adoption figure or procurement programme surfaced, so this base holds **no Canadian evidence at all** — while p718 placed two foundation rows on Canadian evidence (`Montreal-Forced-Aligner`, and `speechbrain`'s Mila/Concordia seats). **Supply evidence exists for Canada and demand evidence does not. A dedicated Canada leg is the cheapest unclaimed intelligence in this region.**


#### 🆕 Added at p717 — the opportunity here is a COMPLIANCE SURFACE, and it has hard dates attached

🟢 **State-level mandates are now the demand driver, not federal policy.** Secondary trackers put K-12 AI guidance at **`28`–`34` states plus DC** (the two counts disagree and both are secondary, so read the range, not a figure), and several states have crossed from guidance into binding rules with deadlines a delivery can be scoped against:

| Jurisdiction | Instrument | What it obliges | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ohio | state requirement + model policy | every public, community and STEM school must **adopt an AI framework** | `2026-07-01` |
| Maryland | SB 720 | statewide AI guidelines, a **district-level AI coordinator**, AI literacy in state standards | `2027-06-01` |
| Arizona | HB 4040 | K-12 **and public universities** must adopt student-AI-use policies | in force |
| New York | A 9190 (bill) | would restrict direct student AI use to **grade 9 and above** | pending |
| California | A.B. 1159 (bill) | would bar student data from **training AI models** absent direct school benefit | pending |

🟢 **The sellable gap is governance, not models.** One 2026 benchmark reports **`86%` of education organisations using generative AI while most lack a policy**, and teacher use roughly **doubled in a year** (`60%` in 2024-25). A district facing the Ohio or Maryland date needs an adoption inventory, a data-flow map and a policy artefact — work a studio can do without shipping a model at all. In higher education the counterpart is **SUNY embedding AI literacy into general education for all incoming undergraduates from Fall 2026**, and the countervailing signal that **Curtin University is disabling Turnitin AI detection in 2026**: detection is being abandoned as a control, which moves the spend to assessment redesign.

🔴 **Federal direction is a risk to price in, not an opportunity.** A 2026 White House framework recommends **Congress preempt state AI laws** to avoid a 50-state patchwork, and the *K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026* has **not passed**. A compliance product built tightly to one state's text is exposed to preemption; build the inventory and the policy scaffold, not the statute citation.

⚠️ Every figure in this block is from a secondary tracker or vendor blog. Confirm against the bill text and the state education department release before it reaches a client deck.

#### 🟢 p716 — North America: **student and institutional adoption are now BOTH measured, the state timetable has a bill count, and the first big incumbent casualty is on the record**

🟢 **Demand, two independent readings.** The US AI-in-education market is put at **`$3.28 B` (2025) → `$15.25 B` (2031)** by one research house, and North America is given **≈`38 %` of the global market** — the largest regional share, while APAC is simultaneously described as the fastest-growing. Both figures are commercial research-house estimates with undisclosed method; they are recorded as a bracket, not a number. 🟢 **The reading that is more useful than either is institutional: institution-wide implementation of AI is reported at `66 %`** — i.e. the region has moved past pilots, which is the procurement-relevant fact.

🟢 **Regulation: the state layer is the real layer, and it now has a count.** Trackers report **close to `100` state bills in 2026** that could directly affect students' use of AI in K-12, and **at least `15` states** have introduced or passed legislation specifically on AI in education. **Virginia's S.B. 394** is the shape to model: it requires state guidance that **covers teacher training** — i.e. the obligation lands on professional development, not on the software. Federal activity remains executive rather than statutory: executive orders between 2025 and early 2026 produced a national framework, agency action plans and a **K-12 AI instruction task force**, plus the **“Pledge to America's Youth: Investing in AI Education.”** 🔵 **Commentary consensus: guidance is proliferating, hard regulation is not** — and **NTIA** has separately noted that edtech adoption *“haven't been matched by serious scrutiny.”*

🔴 **The incumbent casualty, on the record and worth naming.** **Chegg cut `45 %` of its staff (`388` roles)** and brought back its former CEO, after generative AI and Google AI Overviews gutted homework-help traffic. 🔵 **Read it as a positioning lesson rather than a news item: the business destroyed was *answer delivery*.** Every tutor row on this base's agents shelf that refuses to give the answer — the Socratic/hint-only cohort — is positioned on the correct side of exactly this collapse. That is now an evidenced argument in an engagement conversation, not a design preference.

**Players named in this leg:** Chegg, Pearson, Cengage, **Instructure**, Coursera, Udemy, Blackboard. Public/non-profit actors: the White House, and **Panorama Education**, which worked with **Utah, North Carolina, West Virginia and Wisconsin** on guiding principles for AI in education.

🔵 **Opportunity, placed:** the teacher-training obligation (S.B. 394's shape) is a **services** deliverable, not a product one, and it is mandated rather than discretionary. Pair it with the gradebook-write and LTI plumbing now on [`../repos/foundations.md`](../repos/foundations.md) (`tl-its-umich-edu/canvas-lti-redirect-tool`, `716-F`'s standards parser) for districts that must evidence compliance rather than buy a tool.

#### 🟢 p715 — North America: **the state-level timetable is now DATED, and a dated deadline is a procurement window**

🔴 **The demand leg nils a `54th` time on addresses** — `0` repositories — and `North America 30` is **unmoved** for a third pass. Stated as a freeze.

🟢 **The actionable change this pass is that the US patchwork acquired hard dates.** `134` AI-in-education bills across `31` states, and **North Carolina Session Law 2026-41** fixes a three-stage timetable: **state model policy by `2026-12-31`**, **local school-unit policies by `2027-06-30`**, **specified teacher professional development by `2028-06-30`**. Maryland's package separately mandates state guidance, local policies and named **AI coordinators**. The engagement shape is policy-to-implementation: model-policy mapping, then teacher PD at scale.

🔴 **Still no primary federal statute.** Federal action remains executive — **EO 14277** on AI education for American youth, plus July-2025 Department of Education guidance on responsible AI use with federal grant funds. 🔴 This base has **not** read either primary text and does not assert their contents beyond the citation.

🔵 **Adoption, as a spread rather than a number:** `60%` of US teachers reported using AI tools; `55%` of high-school principals say their schools have **not** blocked or restricted access; most US public schools still lack a formal student AI policy. **The inversion `714` recorded persists** — use is ahead of permission.


#### 🟢 p714 — North America: **the region's binding constraint is now a USE/POLICY INVERSION, and it is the engagement**

🔴 **The demand leg nils a `53rd` time on addresses** — `0` repositories — and `North America 30` is **unmoved** for a second pass. Stated as a freeze, not omitted.

🟢 **The one hard, actionable reading this pass: students have adopted, institutions have not permitted, and the gap is measured.** A Gallup study reported in the leg finds **over `60%` of college students use AI weekly for coursework**, while **roughly half of their institutions officially discourage or prohibit it**. 🔵 **That is not an adoption problem, it is a policy-lag problem**, and it is the inverse of the LATAM `87%`-use / `26%`-strategy gap this base records at `P110` — same shape, different cause: LATAM lacks strategy, North America has strategy that says *no* while usage proceeds anyway.

🔵 **Market share readings, and they disagree — recorded as a spread, not averaged:** one source puts North America at **`36%`** of the global AI-in-education market at **`$3.68B` in 2026**; a second puts the region's `2025` share at **`38%`**; Technavio instead reports a **`41.7%` growth contribution** over its forecast period, which is a different quantity again and must not be read as share. 🔴 **An `86%` “educational organisations have adopted generative AI” figure also recurs in this leg and its measurement basis is not stated anywhere in the sources — it is recorded here as unverified rather than quoted as fact.**

🔴 **Regulation is diverging from the rest of the world, and a primary source says so.** UNC's `2026` trends piece argues US AI regulation is **slowing**, and names *“the growing chasm between the U.S. and its allies”* on policy as the risk. 🔵 **For a studio this is a concrete sequencing fact: a product built to EU AI Act obligations (`T189`) will satisfy North American requirements, but not the reverse** — so a multi-region education product should be specified against the EMEA bar and sold into North America, never the other way round.

🟡 **Why, stated as a market property rather than a sweep failure — and p709 can now name the mechanism precisely.** The institution-bound servers filed this pass wrap **`u:find`** (Vienna's own catalogue), **`LearnUs`** (Yonsei's own portal) and **Shinshu's own syllabus system** — all *institution-operated* systems. North American higher education overwhelmingly runs **vendor-operated** systems instead: Canvas, Banner, Workday, PeopleSoft. **A vendor system has no per-institution surface to name a repository after**, which is why `canvas-mcp` exists on this shelf as a *product* integration while `ufind-mcp` exists as an *institution* integration — and it is also why `709-C` refuses to place product integrations by region at all.

🟢 **Opportunity, as a consequence of that asymmetry:** in North America the reusable asset is **not** a campus server, because there is no self-operated campus surface to wrap. It is the **tenant-configurable product integration** — one Canvas or Banner adapter plus per-institution configuration — and the regulatory driver is now concrete enough to scope against: **`134` bills across `31` states**, with **North Carolina's `Session Law 2026-41` setting hard dates** (state model policy by `2026-12-31`, local policies by `2027-06-30`, teacher development by `2028-06`). 🟡 **The honest caveat this base keeps repeating because it keeps being true: Canada and Mexico returned nothing in this leg, again.**


#### 🔴 p708 — North America: the demand leg nils a **47th** time, and p708's supply cohort has **ZERO** North American members. The gap is now specific enough to act on

🔵 **Demand leg, as prescribed:** `AI education North America 2026 adoption regulation players`. 🔴 **Nil on novelty, and measured rather than asserted.** Every marker it returned was already published on this page or on [`../intel/trends.md`](trends.md), each checked by string: `134` bills across `31` states · Maryland **`SB 720`** (state guidance *and* mandatory district policy, one of four such states with Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia) · New York **`A 9190`** (classroom AI restricted to grade 9+, with diagnostic and special-education carve-outs) · California **`AB 1159`** (student data barred from model training) · Ohio's July-2026 written-policy deadline · North Carolina's **`$10M`** **Khanmigo** earmark · the **`K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026`** · Gallup's **`60%`** of US teachers using AI tools · the **April 2026 ADA** web-accessibility deadline for large public institutions · UNESCO's **`70%`** of institutions in Europe and North America with guidance in place or in development. **`10 / 10` already held.**

🔴 **The supply finding is the one that changes a plan, and it is a NIL WITH A SHAPE.** p708's productive channel was the **institutional agent** — an MCP server named after, and authenticating against, one university's running systems. It produced `7` filed subjects spanning Germany, Russia, Croatia and Argentina. 🔴 **Not one of them is North American**, and the agent shelf's `North America` column did not move: it stands at **`20 / 192`** — `10.4%` of the shelf, against `APAC 52` and `Global 83`.

🟡 **That is not a coverage failure of the sweep; it is a property of the market, and naming it is worth more than another leg.** North American higher education runs on **Canvas** and **PowerSchool** far more than on self-hosted Moodle, and those are commercial SaaS with vendor-governed API programmes — so the student or lecturer who would publish an unofficial MCP server for their own campus in Europe or Argentina has, in North America, either an official integration path or a terms-of-service problem. 🔵 **The addresses this base does hold on that side of the line are consistent with exactly that:** the Canvas cohort — [`jjuanrivvera/canvas-cli`](https://github.com/jjuanrivvera/canvas-cli), [`ImmortalKilan/Canvasist`](https://github.com/ImmortalKilan/Canvasist), [`mihirargulkar/canvas-obsidian`](https://github.com/mihirargulkar/canvas-obsidian), [`sriehl/PowerSchool-Student-Map`](https://github.com/sriehl/PowerSchool-Student-Map) — is **student-side, personal-scope tooling against a vendor API**, not an institution-side agent over institutional systems.

🟢 **Opportunity, stated as a consequence of that asymmetry rather than as a wish.** In North America the reusable asset is not a campus MCP server, because there is no self-hosted campus tree to put it in; it is the **authority layer** that the European and LATAM cohort had to invent for itself and that a Canvas/PowerSchool engagement must supply from scratch: role-aware scope enforced **server-side** (`changazo/udesa-mcp`'s *estudiante* / *docente* / *gestor* model), a compulsory **preview-and-approve** step before any write ([`EduardoKrausME/moodle-local_mcp`](https://github.com/EduardoKrausME/moodle-local_mcp)'s separate READ/WRITE registries), **student pseudonymisation** before anything reaches a model ([`falkenslab/miyagi`](https://github.com/falkenslab/miyagi)'s alias layer), and **provenance, coverage and warnings on every result** (`udesa-mcp` again). 🟢 **Those four controls are what California's `AB 1159` and the April-2026 ADA deadline make procurable, and all four exist today in permissively licensed code this base now shelves** — `udesa-mcp` under **Apache-2.0** with its express patent grant, `miyagi` under **MIT**. 🔴 **The thing to *not* promise a North American client is the in-tree variant: `moodle-local_mcp` is GPL-3.0 by construction and cannot ship into a proprietary SIS integration.**

#### 🔴 p707 — North America: the demand leg nils a **46th** time, and p707 adds **NO** North America supply row and **NO** new regional fact

#### 🟢 p705 — NA: `134` bills, `31` states, and for the first time a **dated compliance calendar** a studio can sell against

🟢 **The region's `2026` legislative volume, as returned this pass:** **`134`** AI-in-education bills introduced across **`31`** states; separately, ~**`100`** state bills touching K-12 student AI use within **`1,500`+** AI-related bills nationwide; and **`35`+ states** carrying official AI guidance from their education departments as of **`June 2026`**.

🔵 **The posture matters more than the count, and it is consistent across trackers: states are legislating *guardrails*, not adoption.** Recurring themes are data privacy, human oversight and parental consent — research-and-transparency duties rather than mandates to deploy. 🟡 **Commercially this inverts the usual pitch: in North America the buyer's problem in `2026` is not "help us adopt AI", it is "help us prove our adoption is defensible".**

🟢 **`705-NA-1` — North Carolina Session Law `2026-41` is the concrete clock, and it is the most sellable artifact on this page** because the dates are statutory rather than analyst-projected:

| Deadline | Obligation |
|---|---|
| **`31 Dec 2026`** | State **model policy** published |
| **`30 Jun 2027`** | Each **local school unit** adopts its own policy |
| **`June 2028`** | **Teacher professional development** delivered |

🔵 **Read as a sequence, that is a three-phase engagement with the phases pre-dated by statute** — policy drafting into template-to-district rollout into PD content and delivery — and the middle phase is the one that scales, because `115` local units cannot each draft independently. Maryland **SB 720** points the same way, requiring district AI policies and **designated coordinators** (a named role is a named budget line). 🟢 **Federal tailwind: the US Department of Education finalised a rule prioritising federal education grants for projects that expand understanding of AI or its appropriate and ethical use** — which funds exactly the evidence a defensible-adoption engagement produces.

🟢 **Where this pass's filed repositories land in NA:** [`Ta0p1/ResolveLab`](https://github.com/Ta0p1/ResolveLab) (MIT, `104★`, filed at [`../repos/foundations.md`](../repos/foundations.md)) is the strongest fit on the shelf for the *human-oversight* and evidence duties above — hash-locked test sets, registered studies, replayable episodes — because a guardrail that cannot be demonstrated to a state reviewer is not a guardrail.

🔴 **Gap, stated rather than left silent: Canada returned NOTHING on this pass's North America leg.** No federal position, no Ontario, no Québec — and Québec's language and privacy regimes in particular would change an engagement's shape. **This is an informed absence: the leg was run and Canada did not appear. It is not coverage.**

🟢 **Policy, read this pass and dated, because deadlines are what an engagement plans against:**

| Instrument | What it does | Date that matters |
|---|---|---|

| **New York A 9190** | Would limit classroom AI use to **grade 9 and above**, excepting diagnostics and special education | pending |
| District of Columbia policy | Non-binding, but **bans AI from grading and discipline decisions** | in force |
| Maryland, Idaho, Oklahoma, Virginia | 2026 laws requiring **both** state guidance **and** district-level policies | 2026 |
| **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026** | Advanced by the House Education and Workforce Committee | federal, in progress |

🔵 **The common denominator across almost every instrument above is a prohibition on autonomous grading and discipline, plus a provenance duty on student data.** That is a product constraint, not a compliance footnote: a North American deliverable must keep a human decision-maker in the loop on any consequential judgement and must be able to show what the model saw. The grounding assets filed at p700 are the right starting point.

🟢 **New at p701 — the measured structural finding, and it is about supply, not policy (`T138`).** The `topic:powerschool` leg returned exactly **one** permissive artefact: [`sriehl/PowerSchool-Student-Map`](https://github.com/sriehl/PowerSchool-Student-Map), MIT, `8★`, © **2019**, **last push `2024-03-27`**. PowerSchool holds most American K-12 student records and has, in effect, **no maintained open-source extension layer**. 🔵 **Canvas is the opposite** — `topic:canvas-lms` returned a live, maintained, almost entirely MIT client layer, of which [`jjuanrivvera/canvas-cli`](https://github.com/jjuanrivvera/canvas-cli) (`20★`, Go) and [`leejamesss/cmu-cli`](https://github.com/leejamesss/cmu-cli) (`6★`, Carnegie Mellon, spanning **Canvas + Piazza + Gradescope + Ed Discussion**) were filed this pass.

🟢 **Opportunity, stated as a bid difference.** For **higher education**, start from the maintained Canvas client layer and price the integration as configuration. For **K-12**, price PowerSchool integration as **bespoke build against a district-mediated vendor API** — and note that `cmu-cli`'s four-system shape is the real American campus topology, so "integrate with the LMS" is always an integration with four or five products.

##### 🆕 p686 — The regulatory surface is now 134 bills across 31 states, and that fragmentation IS the engagement

🔴 **Measured this pass:** `134` AI-in-education bills introduced across **`31` states** in the 2026 session, clustered on student-data privacy, classroom-use restriction and curriculum integration. The binding law is at **state** level while federal action remains executive: a **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act** advanced out of the House Education and Workforce Committee but **has not become law**, and should not be sold as though it had.

🟢 **Where the money is already committed, named:** **North Carolina SB 1006** carries a reported **$10 M** earmark for Khan Academy's Khanmigo — a state buying a *product*, not a platform. **Maryland SB 720** is the most complete template: state-level AI guidance plus a district-level obligation to adopt policy **with a designated coordinator**, mirrored by Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia. **Ohio** required districts to have written AI policy by **July 2026**. **California AB 1159** bars training models on student data outright; **Idaho SB 1227** mandates privacy protections for school AI tools.

🟢 **The opportunity is the coordinator, not the tutor.** Maryland-class statutes create a role — a district AI coordinator who must evidence a policy — and no product fills it. A **policy-attestation and evidence surface** (which cohort of staff was trained, which tools were reviewed, which decisions had a human in the loop) is procurable against an existing legal obligation in at least four states, and [`lantingzhang1119/cohort-harbor`](https://github.com/lantingzhang1119/cohort-harbor) (MIT, filed at p686) is a permissively-licensed starting point for exactly that shape.

⚠️ **Two hard constraints to price in.** **(1) AB 1159 makes student-data fine-tuning non-viable in the largest state market** — retrieval over institutional content and per-student state held outside the model (the DeepTutor shape) is the compliant architecture, not a fine-tune. **(2)** A separate **April 2026** ADA accessibility deadline for large public institutions lands on the same budgets, and accessibility remediation competes with AI spend rather than adding to it.

🔵 **Canada:** the same regulatory overview places Canadian AI rules on **workplace** AI, not education — so a Canadian engagement inherits no education-specific AI statute and is governed by provincial privacy law instead.

##### 🆕 p682 — the leg returned **one** fact this page did not hold, and it is a funding rule that creates a reporting obligation

🟢 **New, and it is the only genuinely new regional fact of this pass: `Workforce Pell`.** The US Department of Education's **final rule** creates the **Workforce Pell Grant Program**, extending Pell eligibility to **short-term programmes for the first time**. Dates, as the rule states them: provisions **generally effective `2026-07-20`**, with **optional early implementation from `2026-07-01`** at each institution's discretion. Scope: programmes of roughly **`150`–`599` clock hours** (about **8–15 weeks**), offered **only by accredited institutions**, with **governors in consultation with state workforce boards** designating the high-demand industries and approving programmes. 🔵 **Eligibility widens in a way traditional Pell does not:** it can reach students who **already hold a bachelor's degree**, and awards are **prorated by programme length**.

🟢 **Why this belongs in a market file and not only in a policy note — it is an engineering requirement with a date on it.** A Workforce Pell programme keeps its eligibility only by passing **completion, outcome and earnings tests per programme**, and a programme with zero or negative value-added earnings **loses eligibility**. That is **per-programme, longitudinal, auditable outcome reporting** — completion, credential attainment, and earnings linkage — demanded of exactly the institutions (community colleges, technical colleges, continuing-education arms) whose student-records estate is thinnest. 🔵 **This base already holds the layer that answers it:** the xAPI/LRS shelf ([`yetanalytics/lrsql`](https://github.com/yetanalytics/lrsql), `144★`, and [`adlnet/xAPI-Spec`](https://github.com/adlnet/xAPI-Spec), `955★`), the interoperability shelf ([`Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/edfi-oneroster`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/edfi-oneroster), [`jdolny/OneRoster.NET`](https://github.com/jdolny/OneRoster.NET)) and the early-warning row `p681` placed here ([`Saidkhusayn/student-dropout-early-warning`](https://github.com/Saidkhusayn/student-dropout-early-warning), MIT). **The buying trigger is a statutory deadline, not an AI ambition** — which is the most durable kind this file records.

🔴 **Everything else the leg returned was already held, and it is named so it is not re-searched:** `134` bills across `31` states; Berkeley Law's exam ban effective Summer 2026; Virginia's JLARC review of public-university AI rules; the *Claude for Higher Education* initiative and Northeastern's participation; Ellucian's institution-wide higher-ed adoption moving `49%` → `66%`; North America at ~`36%` of the global AI-in-education market; `70%` of institutions in Europe and North America with guidance in place or in development against `45%` in Latin America and the Caribbean. **No North America address was placed this pass** — the one row filed at p682 ([`fabioov/moodle-mcp`](https://github.com/fabioov/moodle-mcp)) carries no institution, locale default or non-English body text at its default ref, so it was **refused placement** rather than assigned here on a weak channel.

🔵 **Opportunity, placed — and it is narrower than the supply suggests.** Early warning is the use case North American state policy is most likely to fund (retention is a budget line, not a curriculum debate) and the one where open-source supply is thinnest: six dropout-prediction repositories were probed this pass and **two carry a permissive grant, both at `0★`, both built on the same UCI dataset.** So the engagement is not *adopt a system*; it is **build the pipeline around a published trajectory formulation, on the institution's own SIS data**, with the model as the small part. 🔴 **And the regulatory read matters here specifically:** a dropout-risk score that touches admissions, progression or access to a programme is an Annex III-shaped use case for any client with EU operations, and in the US it lands squarely in the FERPA-plus-state-privacy overlap that `134` bills across `31` states are currently writing. **Price the governance artefact — data boundaries, human review of every flag, an appeal route — as a deliverable, not as documentation.**

🟡 **New at p661, and marked secondary because neither was read in primary form.** The **Education Commission of the States** is reported to count **`29` states with an AI task force or commission**, and the **Southern Regional Education Board** is reported to run an **AI Commission**. Both reach this page through a vendor compilation rather than an ECS or SREB publication, so neither is quotable as a number. 🔵 **The reason they are recorded at all is procurement shape rather than market size:** a US state engagement has a named convener to approach, and for the US South the SREB commission is a regional one. This page's standing complaint about APAC — that the available player lists are vendor rankings rather than procurement-grade intelligence — applies here too, and these two bodies are the first governance-side conveners it has recorded for North America.

**Regulatory posture:** heavy state-level legislative activity, binding rules still uncommon. More than 134 bills across 31 states addressed AI in K-12 and higher education in the 2026 session (MultiState / FutureEd tracking). Enacted examples: **Idaho SB 1227** (statewide framework, local policies, AI-literacy standards, data-privacy requirements, and an explicit bar on AI replacing human teachers); **Maryland SB 720** (statewide guidance, district AI coordinators, the Maryland AI Education Collaborative); **Ohio** (districts required to adopt AI policies by 1 July 2026, but the statute is near-silent on content and names no enforcement mechanism); **Florida** (State Board approved AI rules for K-12 and the 28 state colleges). Federally, the K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026 advanced out of the House Education and Workforce Committee, and a 2026 federal grant rule prioritises AI literacy.

**Adoption (measured at p651):** the most mature market and the most policy-constrained.

| Reading | Source |
|---|---|
| **60% of US teachers use AI tools** | Gallup, via secondary reporting |
| **55% of high-school principals say their school has not blocked or restricted AI tool access** | secondary |
| ~70% of institutions across Europe and North America have guidance or are developing it | secondary |
| More than **1,500 AI-related bills** introduced across US legislatures in 2026; ~134 of them education-specific across 31 states | MultiState / FutureEd tracking |
| 92% university student usage against institutions that mostly lack policy | carried from p650 |

🔴 **One North America claim was tested this pass and does not verify.** A widely-repeated summary attributes to **North Carolina Session Law 2026-41** a state AI model policy due 2026-12-31, local district policies due 2027-06-30 and teacher training due 2028-06-30. Checked against the legislature's own summaries: **SL 2026-41 is the 2026 Appropriations Act (Senate Bill 257)**; it carries AI provisions (a DPI directed grant extending the AI Pilot Program to Alamance-Burlington Schools) but **no model-policy mandate was found in it**. The AI-literacy content-standards requirement appears under a **different** session law (**2026-61, House Bill 268**), and the DPI model-policy language appears only in *bills* — along with Senate Bill 981's proposed AI in Education Task Force reporting by 2028-03-15. **Those dates are not published on this page.** Full disposition in `intel/measured-unusable.md`.

🟢 **Canada, recorded for the first time after three consecutive passes where this leg returned only US sources.** The gap note below has said for three passes that "North America" on this page meant the United States. It no longer has to.

| | Reading (secondary; see the note on unreachable hosts) |
|---|---|
| Federal strategy | A **national AI strategy published 4 June 2026**, roughly 50 pages. Includes a **National AI Literacy Initiative** targeting **one million entry-level post-secondary students** and training **more than 3,000 educators** with AI learning kits. |
| Federal regulation | 🔴 **There is no Canadian AI Act.** AIDA (part of Bill C-27) **died when Parliament was prorogued in January 2025** and has not been reintroduced. Governance runs through privacy and sector law: PIPEDA federally, **Quebec's Law 25**, plus sector guidance (OSFI, Health Canada). The widely-circulated belief that AIDA is in force is wrong and worth correcting in any client conversation. |
| K-12 guidance | **Three provincial education ministries** had published K-12 AI guidance as of December 2025. **Quebec issued a March 2025 directive suspending the use of AI assistants**, still in place as of December 2025. **Nova Scotia has no provincewide policy** — regional centres set their own rules and the department calls provincewide guidance an "ongoing conversation". |
| Criticism of the strategy | No clear timelines or KPIs, and no named accountable owner in government. |

🔵 **What Canada actually is, commercially:** a large federal *capability* commitment with no binding regulation behind it and provincial policy ranging from an outright suspension (Quebec) to nothing at all (Nova Scotia). That is the opposite of the US profile on this page — there, mandates without guidance; here, funded ambition without mandates. The offers are therefore different, and a single "North America" proposal template will mis-fit one of the two countries.

**🆕 New at p653 (the rest of this section reproduced and is unchanged):**

| Reading | Source |
|---|---|
| **Four states — Maryland, Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia — have 2026 laws requiring *both* statewide guidance *and* mandatory district-level AI policies.** This is the tightest binding pattern in the US and the one that creates a dated, funded procurement at district level | Pursuit policy tracking (secondary) |
| 🔴 **Washington DC issued a policy barring AI from grading, discipline and IEP decisions** — non-mandatory, but it is the clearest US articulation yet of *where* AI is unacceptable rather than how to adopt it, and it maps almost exactly onto EU AI Act Annex III's high-risk list | Pursuit (secondary) |
| **New York A 9190** would restrict classroom AI use to **ninth grade and above**, with carve-outs for diagnostics and special education | MultiState (secondary) |
| **The "STUDENTS FIRST Act of 2026"** — a national framework for AI in schools authored by students from all 50 states, published 3 August 2026, explicitly rejecting *both* an outright ban *and* unrestricted adoption | AASA |

🔵 **Why the DC item matters more than its legal weight.** A non-binding district policy is not a market. But *grading, discipline and IEP decisions* is the same prohibition shape as Annex III, arrived at independently — which is the first evidence this base has that the US and EU are converging on **which uses are unacceptable** even while diverging completely on instruments. A deliverable designed against Annex III is therefore more portable into North America than the regulatory divergence suggests. Note this is a **reversal of direction** from T8 (regulation is diverging by region): divergence holds for instruments, convergence is appearing in prohibited uses.

**Opportunities:**
- 🟢 **Canada's AI-literacy commitment is a curriculum sell, not a compliance sell.** One million post-secondary students and 3,000+ educators is a delivery problem with public money attached and no regulation to satisfy. This is the one North American opportunity on this page where the deliverable is teaching material and teacher capacity rather than controls — and `patchy631/ai-engineering-hub` (MIT, 41.1k★) is a permissive base for exactly that. See `compose/patterns.md` **P10**.
- 🔵 **Provincial fragmentation is the Canadian version of the policy-to-implementation gap**, with one twist: in Quebec the current posture is *suspension*, so the sellable work is the evidence and governance case that would let a directive be lifted, not a deployment. Do not pitch classroom AI into Quebec as though the Ontario or Alberta conversation applies.
- **Policy-to-implementation gap as a service.** Ohio-style mandates require a policy with no guidance on content, and Maryland requires a district AI coordinator role that districts have no one to fill. Globant can productise the compliance artefact *plus* the technical controls behind it, repeatable across districts.
- **Canvas-first agent pilots.** Canvas dominates North American HE. `canvas-mcp` (MIT, 317★) reaches real course data with no migration — the shortest credible path to a funded pilot.
- **"No AI replacing teachers" as a design constraint, not an obstacle.** Idaho's bar makes teacher-augmentation framing legally safer than autonomy. `Instructional Agents` (MIT) automates course *preparation* — exactly the defensible side of that line.
- OATutor (MIT, UC Berkeley) carries domestic academic provenance, which matters in public procurement here.
- 🟢 **p655 — the legislative volume is now the sales argument, and it is quantified.** **134 bills on AI in education were introduced across 31 states in 2026** (MultiState / FutureEd trackers), and **four states — Maryland, Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia — now have 2026 laws requiring *both* state guidance and mandatory district-level AI policy adoption.** Federally it remains executive action plus a committee-stage bill (the K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026 advanced out of House Education and Workforce). **The offer this creates is not a tutor.** A district that must adopt a policy by statute, with no guidance on its content, buys policy-to-implementation work: the policy instrument, the staff training, the audit trail. Price that as the entry engagement and the platform work as phase two.
- 🟢 **p655 — the gap between teacher use and institutional policy is the live risk, and it is wide.** 60% of US teachers report using AI tools (Gallup, 2,200+ educators), while a joint Child Trends / US Department of Education analysis finds **most US public schools still have no formal AI policy for students**. Higher education is further along: ~70% of institutions across North America and Europe have guidance or are drafting it. Shadow adoption at 60% with no policy is a liability conversation, and it is the one a superintendent will take a meeting about.
- 🟢 **p655 — two instructive procurement constraints to design against, not around.** The **DC** OSSE policy for 2026-27 **bans AI from grading and discipline decisions outright**; **New York's A 9190** would restrict classroom AI to grade 9 and above with carve-outs for diagnostics and special education. Both make *teacher-in-the-loop with a hard human decision boundary* the only shippable architecture for K-12 here — which is exactly what `jupyter/nbgrader`'s release/collect/autograde/**manual grade** workflow already encodes (see `repos/foundations.md`, new at p655).
- 🟢 **p655 — the permissive technical-curriculum base is North American and BSD-licensed.** `jupyterhub` (BSD-3-Clause, 8,362★) and `jupyter/nbgrader` (BSD-3-Clause, 1,370★) are Project Jupyter, with a decade of US university production use. For a CS faculty, bootcamp or data-science academy engagement here, that provenance plus a fully permissive chain is a stronger procurement story than any AI-native tool found this pass. See `compose/patterns.md` **P15**.


##### 🟢 New at p656 — the accessibility mandate is a services engagement, and the evidence spine answers the state policies

🔴 **The April 2026 ADA deadline recorded above has no permissive answer, and that changes the engagement shape rather than the price.** p656 swept `topic:accessibility topic:education` (442 repositories) and read the grant payload of everything credible. The only production WCAG remediation tool is [`ucfopen/UDOIT`](https://github.com/ucfopen/UDOIT) — University of Central Florida, scans Canvas content, explains each defect and fixes many in place, 117 ★ / 78 forks, pushed 2026-11-04 — and it is **GPL-3.0** (35,147 B payload read). The permissive rows in that topic (`braille-tools`, MIT, 43 ★; `osunyorg/admin`, MIT, 49 ★) are not remediation tools. `adaptlearning/adapt_framework`, the authoring tool with accessibility as a design goal, is also GPL-3.0.

**So the North America accessibility opportunity is: deploy UDOIT inside the client's own estate, bill the remediation and the integration, ship no derivative.** It is a services engagement with a hard regulatory date, not a product line — and UDOIT being Canvas-specific is an advantage here, because Canvas is the dominant LMS in US higher education. Say "services" in the first conversation; a proposal that promises a platform will have to retract it. See `intel/trends.md` **T29**.

🟢 **Separately, the state-level policy wave is a measurement opportunity, and p656 shelved the components for it.** Maryland's SB 720 contemplates **certification of compliant tools**; North Carolina's SL 2026-41 requires local school-unit policies; NYC's guidance forbids student data being used to train models. Each of those is an *evidence* requirement before it is a software requirement — a district has to be able to show what a tool did with which learner's data. An Apache-2.0 Learning Record Store ([`yetanalytics/lrsql`](https://github.com/yetanalytics/lrsql)) plus LTI launch ([`tsugiproject/tsugi`](https://github.com/tsugiproject/tsugi), Apache-2.0) produces exactly that: a per-learner, per-attempt statement log the district owns, in its own database, with no vendor egress. **That is the cheapest credible answer to "certify this tool" that this KB can currently assemble**, and it is permissive end to end. See `compose/patterns.md` **P17**.

##### 🟢 New at p657 — three permissive autograders are American university software, and the grading prohibitions are a fit rather than an obstacle

**Supply.** Three of the six permissive production autograders named in **T30** are built and run by US universities: [`Submitty`](https://github.com/Submitty/Submitty) (BSD-3-Clause, 805 ★ / **1,119 forks**, Rensselaer), [`autolab/Autolab`](https://github.com/autolab/Autolab) (Apache-2.0, 808 ★, Carnegie Mellon) and [`ucbds-infra/otter-grader`](https://github.com/ucbds-infra/otter-grader) (BSD-3-Clause, 162 ★, UC Berkeley Data Science, **and it grades R**). With `nbgrader` and `okpy/ok`, North America is the **origin region for this entire layer** — which means peer references, familiar names and existing institutional deployments are all available in-region for a US or Canadian proposal.

**Regulation, measured this pass.** Roughly **134 AI-in-education bills across 31 states** in the 2026 session (MultiState), with legislatures favouring research, transparency and guardrails over adoption mandates. **Maryland, Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia** have 2026 laws requiring *both* state guidance *and* mandatory district-level AI policy. **NY A 9190** would restrict classroom AI to grade 9 and above, excepting diagnostics and special-education interventions. Federally, the House Education and Workforce Committee advanced the **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026** (⚠️ final status not confirmed in these sources), and the **US Department of Labor published an AI Literacy Framework in February 2026**. In Canada, workplace AI obligations sit under the *Working for Workers Four Act, 2024*, with Ontario AI-hiring-disclosure rules pending.

**Adoption.** **60% of US teachers report using AI tools** (Gallup, n > 2,200). **55% of high-school principals** say their school has not blocked or restricted AI on the school network. Most US public schools still lack a formal student AI policy (Child Trends / Dept. of Education). **70% of institutions across Europe and North America** have or are developing guidance.

🟢 **The opportunity, and it inverts how the prohibitions are usually read.** **DC OSSE's non-mandatory 2026-27 policy bars AI from grading, discipline and IEP decisions**, and **Clark County** bars AI as the sole basis for any high-stakes decision. Those rules do not block this stack — **they describe it.** `Submitty`'s first-class TA/manual-grading workflow and `Artemis`'s `Athena`, which *suggests* feedback rather than issuing it, are architecturally compliant by default. **The sellable artefact is the compliance evidence**: which AI subsystem is enabled for which cohort, what it touched, and where the human decided — produced from configuration and an xAPI statement log (**T28**) rather than from a policy document. ⚠️ A proposal that leads with autonomy rather than with the human boundary is unsellable in these districts; the same stack sold as governed assistance is well-positioned.

🔵 **Named watch items:** OpenAI and Common Sense Media's proposed California **"Parents & Kids Safe AI Act"** ballot initiative would set standards any AI-featured edtech would need to meet; and North Carolina's defence of a **$10M Khan Academy AI earmark** is the clearest signal that US state money is moving to named vendors rather than to capability building — which is an argument for bidding on the integration and evidence work rather than on the tutor.


🟢 **New at p658 — the CS-education delivery layer, and the licence line that runs through it.** North America *produces* the two biggest assets in this layer and licenses them in opposite ways. **Microsoft MakeCode (`microsoft/pxt`, MIT, 2,314★) is permissive end to end** — core, micro:bit target and Arcade target are all MIT, verified byte-for-byte this pass — which makes a branded, closed, white-labelled coding platform for a state education department or large district a **configuration** engagement rather than a fork (**P22**, 10–14 weeks). **Code.org (`code-dot-org/code-dot-org`, 902★) is Apache-2.0 for code and explicitly *not* for courseware**: artwork, lectures, videos, characters and fonts are carved out in a 77-line `NOTICE` and may not be embedded in another format, and the Minecraft assets inside it are Microsoft's, requiring a separate agreement (**T39**). 🔵 **The sales consequence is concrete:** a district that wants "our own Code.org" can have the platform and cannot have the lessons, and the honest version of that proposal prices curriculum authoring as the main line item. US districts under the 2026 written-AI-policy mandates (Ohio by July 2026, Maryland SB 720, Idaho, Oklahoma, Virginia) are already procuring; a coding platform whose licence permits a closed derivative is a cleaner answer to a board than a per-seat SaaS contract.


🟡 **New at p662, recorded as an indication and not as a verified transaction.** **Docebo (Toronto) acquired `365Talents` in January 2026** to add AI skills-mapping to its learning platform. Novel to this base — `0` hits across all eleven pages and both append-only logs — but it reaches this pass through an encyclopaedia entry, not a filing or a press release, so it is **not** shelved as a dated figure. 🔵 Why it is worth recording anyway: skills-mapping is the layer where corporate learning meets HR, and a North American LMS vendor buying into it is the demand-side signal behind the credentialing trend this KB already tracks via 1EdTech. The EMEA leg of the same transaction is noted under **EMEA**.


##### 🔴 New at p664 — North America is this shelf's new regional zero, and its 2026 statutes are a services brief written by legislators

**Measured this pass (all figures are a search channel's report of a secondary tracker unless stated; none is a primary legislative text, and none was read first-hand):**

- **134 AI-in-education bills across 31 states** in the 2026 session (MultiState, as of ~March 2026). The tracker characterises the posture as *measured* — research, transparency and guardrails rather than classroom mandates.
- **Four states now require BOTH state-issued AI guidance AND mandatory district-level AI policy adoption: Maryland, Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia.** Maryland's **SB 720** is the worked example: the state education department issues guidance, districts must adopt policies, designate **AI coordinators**, and receive statewide professional development.
- **New York A 9190** would restrict classroom AI use to **ninth grade and above**, with carve-outs for diagnostics and special education; **A.6972** would create a statewide AI working group.
- **District policy is already binding and already narrower than state law.** Clark County (NV) bars AI from being the **sole basis for high-stakes decisions** such as discipline or special-education placement (January 2026). Washington DC's non-mandatory 2026-27 policy **bans AI from grading and from IEP decisions**.
- **Federal:** the **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026** advanced out of the House Education and Workforce Committee; the **Department of Labor published an AI Literacy Framework in February 2026**. No education-specific binding federal rule.
- **California, from outside the education statute:** the **"Parents & Kids Safe AI Act"** ballot initiative, backed by OpenAI and Common Sense Media, would set standards for **any AI product used by minors** — which includes edtech regardless of what the education code says.
- **Adoption vs policy:** Gallup data put **60% of US teachers** using AI tools, while **most US public schools still lack a formal student AI policy** (Child Trends / Dept. of Education). UNESCO reports **70% of institutions in Europe and North America** have or are developing guidance — a higher-education figure that does not transfer to K-12.

🔴 **The structural finding, and it is about this KB and not about the market.** `agents/top.md` now places one agent row in North America (OATutor, UC Berkeley) against APAC's eight — and p664 closed EMEA and extended LATAM while North America stood still. **North America is the largest education-technology market on earth, it is where the Canvas-shaped institutions open source has demonstrably invested in are (T18), and it is now the thinnest region on this base's agent shelf.** That is an instrument problem of the same family as the one p663 and p664 diagnosed for LATAM and EMEA — except the query language is already English, so the explanation must be different and has not yet been found. **Registered as an open question, not explained.**

🟢 **Opportunities, in order of how directly a 2026 statute pays for them:**

1. **The district AI policy instrument, as a repeatable product.** Four states now compel every district to adopt a policy, designate a coordinator and train staff. That is a templated governance deliverable — policy, role definition, PD curriculum, an audit trail — sold per district and reusable across all four states. It needs no model and no new code; the measurement half can run on `OpenEuroLLM/JudgeArena` (`repos/foundations.md`) with a local judge so no student work leaves the district.
2. **"AI is not the sole basis" as an engineering requirement.** Clark County's rule and DC's grading/IEP bans are not vague. They demand a human-decision record: what the model proposed, what the human decided, and why. This base already holds the evidence spine for it — **P17** (LTI in, xAPI out) — and the prohibition is a *fit*, not an obstacle, as `p657` established for autograders.
3. **Grade-band gating as a product feature.** If New York A 9190 passes, any platform sold into NY K-12 needs per-grade AI gating with diagnostic and special-education exceptions. Nothing on this shelf does that today.
4. **AI literacy delivery against two funded federal instruments** (the DoL framework, the pending K-12 Act) — **P10** already scopes this shape.

⚠️ **What to verify before any of this is quoted to a client:** every bill number above against the originating legislature, because the trackers disagree among themselves (one lists Maryland's vehicle as HB 1057, another as SB 720).



🔵 **p667 — the North America leg is nil on discovery for the ninth consecutive pass, and this pass can finally say how much of that nil is real.** All eleven NA facts served this pass were scored against HEAD: `8` held on a sell shelf directly, `2` more held under a different spelling (Canada's *AI for All* strategy is recorded on this page by its **date**, `4 June 2026`, not its name; the principals statistic is spelled `high-school principals` here against the source's `high school principals`), and **`1` genuinely novel** — *Defending Education*, an advocacy group criticising NEA/AFT AI guidance, **measured and not filed** because it is contested commentary on guidance this page already records rather than adoptable intelligence. 🟢 **The supply half is also nil and that is the newer information:** of the `22` addresses verified this pass, **not one placed to North America** on the owner-location channel (`667-G1`), while EMEA took two and LATAM took two. The region with the densest regulatory activity on this page is contributing the least open-source supply, and that asymmetry has now held across several passes.


##### 🟢 New at p668 — the SIS layer is a North America opportunity specifically because the regulation names the use cases it holds

- 🔵 **`T50` + the state-law picture compose into one engagement.** AI in the system of record touches grading, discipline and IEP decisions — and those are precisely the uses North American jurisdictions are naming. DC's non-mandatory 2026-27 policy **bans AI from grading, discipline, IEP decisions and teacher evaluations**; New York's A 9190 restricts classroom AI to ninth grade and above with diagnostic and special-education exceptions; Maryland, Idaho, Oklahoma and Virginia require both state guidance **and** district-level policy. **`134` AI-in-education bills across `31` states this session.** 🟢 **So the deployer-documentation half of `P37` is not overhead here, it is the thing that makes the deployment approvable** — and `P37`'s process boundary keeps a GPL-2.0 student-records system out of the client's product while doing it.
- 🔴 **The governance gap is the quantified opportunity, restated because it has not closed.** `60%` of US teachers use AI (Gallup) and `55%` of high-school principals report their school has not restricted AI access on the network. Higher education is further along — UNESCO puts `70%` of institutions in Europe and North America as having or developing guidance — so the K-12 district, not the university, is where the policy work is unstaffed.
- 🔵 **Canada reads differently and should be sold differently.** Strategy without statute: *AI for All* (launched `2026-06-04`) commits to a national AI literacy push, watermarking and a **Canada Trusted AI Certification**. That is a certification-readiness engagement, not a compliance one.
- 🔴 **Supply nil, written down for the second consecutive pass.** p668 placed **one** new North American address by the `668-A`-corrected probe (`Fazmin` → Canada) and **no** new North American address was discovered by any channel: the four global legs returned `18/18` already held, the topic-page census `66/78` already held. **This is a measured absence of new North American open source education supply this window, not a gap in searching** — the searches ran and are listed in `agents/trending.md`.

- 🆕 **p671 — the sellable instrument this pass produced, and North America is where it is worth most.** `671-K`/`T52` measured `2` of `197` open-source addresses on this base's own shelves pointing at repositories that had **changed owner**, while `git ls-remote`, `raw.githubusercontent.com` and a `sha256` of the licence payload all kept passing at the dead address. 🔵 **Why this region first:** the regulatory load here is *procedural* — Maryland's `SB 720` and the `MD`/`ID`/`OK`/`VA` group require **state guidance *and* district-level AI policy**, Ohio districts faced a **July 2026** deadline for written policy, and `AB 1159` bars student data from model training in California. Those are all obligations that get discharged by producing an auditable inventory. **An inventory that cannot tell you a dependency changed stewardship is not auditable**, and `P40` is a one-week build that fixes it.
- 🔴 **Supply nil a third consecutive pass, and p671 now has a measured cause rather than an impression.** `671-E` ran `10` domain legs on `search/repositories` at a `stars:>=50` floor: `52` distinct addresses, `0` filable, and **no North American open-source education supply placed by any channel.** `671-F` names the mechanism that was inflating earlier shortlists — *term-of-art decomposition*, where the `knowledge tracing` leg returns a repository whose README contains **zero** education terms because its feature list happens to say *"knowledge bases, tracing"*. **The shortlists were never as full as they looked.**
