| highest-starred **education-domain** repository in the org | [`koski`](https://github.com/Opetushallitus/koski) — **`23★`**, 12 forks |
| licence family, read from payloads, across the `8` now shelved | 🟠 **EUPL-1.1 / 1.2-or-later** |
| … for which `gh api` reports a real SPDX | 🔴 **`1` in `100`** |

🔵 **Why this is market intelligence and not a repository note.** It establishes the **shape of the EMEA public-education buyer**: a ministry that already runs admissions, curriculum (national *and* local), statutory personal learning plans, attainment records, early-childhood administration and exam content **as public production software under a copyleft licence**. 🔴 **That rules out the usual "we will build you a platform" engagement** and rules in integration, agent layers and operational capacity around an estate the buyer already owns — which is what `P32`, `P33` and `P34` are scoped as. 🔵 **And it generalises as a prospecting method**: the EUPL is the European Commission's own licence, so other member-state agencies are a plausible and almost entirely unexplored supply of the same artefact class. **This base has enumerated exactly one of them.**

##### 🆕 p665 — a procurement signal no news leg returns: a national education ministry's production stack, in public, under EUPL

🟢 **The one genuinely new regional fact this pass, and it came from the repository channel rather than the news channel.** `Opetushallitus` — the **Finnish National Agency for Education** — runs its learner-facing stack as public repositories under the **European Union Public Licence**, and all seven were pushed on **2026-11-05**: applications (`ataru`, Clojure, 23 MB), statutory personal competence plans (`ehoks`, Clojure), the machine-readable national curriculum and qualification requirements (`eperusteet`, Java), attainments (`suorituspalvelu`, Scala), learner identity (`oppijanumerorekisteri`), organisations (`organisaatio`) and state grants (`valtionavustus`, TypeScript, 47 MB). Grants read byte-by-byte at p665; four are filed on [`../verticals/solutions.md`](../verticals/solutions.md) with the EUPL scoping note.

🔵 **Why this is market intelligence and not just a repository list.** Every EMEA public-sector education conversation on this page turns on two questions — *where does the data sit* and *what licence can the buyer accept*. Finland has answered both in public, in production: the reference implementation of a statutory personalised-learning object (`ehoks`) and of a machine-readable national curriculum (`eperusteet`) are readable, and the licence the ministry chose is the one an EU procurement office recognises in **23 languages**. 🔴 **It also sets the licence expectation an EMEA bid may have to meet: EUPL, not MIT** — and because the EUPL counts online availability as distribution, a vendor SaaS built by forking it inherits the obligation. The arm's-length integration shape is 🆕 **P32** in [`../compose/patterns.md`](../compose/patterns.md).

🟡 **The rest of the mandated EMEA leg was a nil, and the list is recorded so it is not re-bought:** the **Annex III** high-risk postponement to **2 December 2027** (stand-alone) and 2 August 2028 (embedded), the UK **DfE** January-2026 generative-AI product safety standards with Ofqual and Ofsted, and the UAE **KHDA** guidance to private schools were all already on this page. **`0` facts added by the news leg; `1` substantial fact added by the repository leg.** 🔵 **That asymmetry is now four passes old and is the clearest standing instruction this page can give: for EMEA, read what ministries publish as code, not what vendors publish as commentary.**

**Regulatory posture:** the most binding regime in the world, and the timeline just moved. The **Digital Omnibus (Regulation (EU) 2026/1744)** deferred high-risk obligations for stand-alone education AI from 2 August 2026 to **2 December 2027**. Guides still citing August 2026 are out of date. High-risk covers **admissions, learning-outcome evaluation, student level placement, and exam/behaviour monitoring**. **Article 50 transparency duties apply from 2 August 2026** — a school running a student-facing chatbot owes disclosure *now*. **Emotion recognition in schools has been prohibited since February 2025.** Article 4's AI-literacy duty was softened from "ensuring a level of AI literacy" to "taking measures". Schools are **deployers** under the Act.

🟢 **The Africa and Middle East half of EMEA, recorded for the first time.** Previous passes declared "no Africa-specific regulation or adoption data" as a gap. p651 found demand-side material and can now locate the gap precisely rather than leaving it as silence.

| | Reading (secondary sources; directional) |
|---|---|
| Market split | **Europe ≈ $2.64B (2026)**; **Middle East & Africa ≈ $0.56B (2026) → $1.6B (2030)**. Europe is ~5× MEA today and MEA grows faster. |
| K-12 leaders in Europe | Finland, Estonia and the Netherlands named as furthest along on integration |
| Institutional guidance | ~70% of institutions across Europe and North America have guidance or are developing it |
| Gulf policy | **Saudi Arabia has made AI literacy a mandatory component of undergraduate education**; the **UAE** has extended AI-focused programmes across all education levels. Gulf states lead the region on policy, and the emphasis is economic development and tertiary education rather than the ethics-and-transparency framing dominant in Western Europe (academic corpus comparison of six Western European and six MENA countries). |
| Africa regional frame | The African Union's **CESA 16-25** sets the continental digital-education direction; implementation varies widely by country and most structured AI use is government- or NGO-piloted. |
| Africa, this week | **Google launched a free, offline-capable AI and digital-skills training platform for Nigeria and other African markets around 3 November 2026** — lessons downloadable over Wi-Fi then usable without mobile data, in English, Swahili and Arabic with Hausa being added. Proprietary. Separately, **Google and Fab AI are working with innovators in Kenya, Ghana and South Africa on open Gemma models, with datasets, evaluation suites and open-source models stated to be released publicly** — the clearest forward signal of African open-source education supply this base has seen. *(`blog.google` does not resolve under this session's network policy, so Google's own announcement could not be read at first hand; both items rest on secondary reporting and should be re-verified.)* |
| Africa, supply side | **Mdali** (Johannesburg) adapts open-source models into an offline-first maths tutor for CAPS Grades 10–12 that runs on basic phones and keeps student data on-device; NVIDIA Inception member. **No public repository found.** Kenya's M-Shule (SMS-based) and Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire's Chalkboard Education are older offline-learning precedents. |

🔵 **Where that leaves the Africa gap:** it is **not** a demand gap and **not** an absence of builders. African AI-education products are real, offline-first by design, and built *on* open weights — but they ship as **proprietary applications rather than open repositories**. That is a precise, actionable finding: the partnering motion for Africa is co-development with existing product teams, not adoption of an African open-source stack, because there is not yet one to adopt. Watch the Google/Fab AI open-model releases, which would change this.

🟢 **The United Kingdom, recorded for the first time.** Posture is light-touch, principle-based and delivered through existing regulators rather than a statute — but two concrete, dated education instruments now exist: the **Department for Education's generative AI product safety standard for education (January 2026)**, and **Ofqual's resources on AI and coursework integrity (March 2026)**. A product safety standard is the most directly useful of the two for a vendor-side engagement: it is a published bar an edtech product can be built and tested against, in a market with no AI Act obligations to wait for.

🟢 **Africa, country level — the gap p651 located is now populated, and the shape of it is confirmed rather than overturned.** p651 declared that no country-level education AI regulation existed for any Sub-Saharan African state. Searched specifically this pass; the accurate statement is narrower and more useful: **national AI *strategies* with explicit education commitments exist; education-sector *rules* do not.**

| Country | Instrument | Education content | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Kenya** | **National AI Strategy 2025–2030**, launched 27 March 2025 | Talent pillar commits to **AI literacy across all demographics and integration of AI into primary, secondary and higher-education curricula**, plus expanded STEM with a focus on women and underrepresented groups; **DigiKen** Digital Innovation Hubs as curriculum anchors | Strategy, not a rule. 🔴 **No national AI-literacy framework has been published**: a September 2026 report describes the Education Cabinet Secretary and the government's Special Envoy on Technology *planning* how the system should prepare, with curriculum framing around Kenya's languages and local values, and education commentary from roughly two months ago still calls for a framework aligned to the Competency-Based Curriculum. A separate **Kenya AI Bill (2026)** is a legislative item, not education policy. |
| **South Africa** | Draft **National AI Policy** | — | 🔴 **Published for comment 10 April 2026 and withdrawn/abrogated 27 April 2026** — seventeen days. Status should be re-verified before it is quoted. Separately, the Department of Basic Education has pursued coding-and-robotics curriculum reform including AI elements. |
| **Nigeria** | **National Digital Economy Policy and Strategy (2020–2030)** | References AI; application in education systems is **aspirational rather than systemic** | Strategy, not a rule |
| **Continental** | African Union AI strategy | **Phase 1 (2025–2026) is establishing governance structures and national strategies** — i.e. the continent is, by its own timeline, still at the strategy stage | On schedule, by design |

🔵 **So the Africa finding is now two-sided and consistent.** p651 established that African AI-education *supply* is real but ships as proprietary apps rather than open repositories. p652 establishes that African AI-education *governance* is real but sits at strategy level rather than rule level. **Both halves point at the same engagement:** there is no compliance deadline to sell against and no open stack to adopt, so the motion is capability-building and co-development with existing teams and ministries — on a strategy's commitments, not a regulation's deadline. Kenya is the furthest along and the clearest entry point; its missing AI-literacy framework is a named, unfilled deliverable in a published national strategy.

**🆕 New at p653 — the capability denominators, which this base did not hold:**

| 🔴 **Only about one third of national school curricula in the EU include AI-related competences** | European Commission, AI-and-skills brief, 23 October 2026 |
| 🔴 **Around 30% of teachers have taken part in any AI-focused training**, and the Commission states plainly that most teachers still lack the skills to use AI responsibly in the classroom | European Commission, same brief |

🟢 **These two numbers are the most commercially useful readings in this section, because they are the denominators under the compliance deadline.** EMEA has the world's most binding education-AI regime, with high-risk obligations falling due **2 December 2027** and Article 50 transparency duties live since 2 August 2026 — and **two thirds of curricula and seven in ten teachers are not ready.** A conformity-by-design deliverable sold without a teacher-capability component is being sold into a system that cannot operate it. That makes AI literacy a **gating dependency of the compliance sale in EMEA**, not an adjacent upsell — the same conclusion `compose/patterns.md` P10 reached for funded mandates, arrived at here from the supply side.

🟢 **Supply-side change in this region, new at p653:** the Apache-2.0 institutional LMS this base found this pass, **OpenOlat**, is an EMEA asset — descended from the University of Zurich's OLAT, maintained by frentix GmbH in Zurich, with its deepest adoption in German-speaking higher education, and with `langchain4j` already in-tree so an in-jurisdiction no-egress deployment is configuration rather than a fork. For a European public-sector engagement that is the strongest licence-and-residency position on this shelf. See `verticals/solutions.md` **Pattern F** and `compose/patterns.md` **P11**.

Tracxn files it as **Series D**; it is the company's **first round since 2022**, and it follows the **January 2026 acquisition of StackFuel** (Germany, data and AI training). 🔴 **A name-clash trap worth recording before it causes an error.** **Multiverse** (London, edtech) and
**Multiverse Computing** (quantum and model compression, $215M Series B in June 2025) are different
companies, and search results mix them freely. One secondary source already attributes the edtech round to
"Multiverse Computing". Any funding figure for "Multiverse" must be checked against the business
description, not the name.

🟡 **Two further EMEA rounds were found and could not be confirmed, so they are filed as unverified
rather than published as readings:** **Medly AI** (reported $8M seed, August 2026, GCSE exam preparation)
and **Lucida AI** (reported $5.4M seed, April 2026, voice-first language coach). A targeted follow-up
search returned nothing on either. Treat as leads, not facts.

🔵 **The market context these sit in cuts against the funding headlines.** HolonIQ counted roughly
**$1B of global edtech venture funding in H1 2026, down 26% year over year** — so Multiverse's $70M is a
large share of a shrinking pool, which is a consolidation signal rather than a growth one. A claim that
Europe captured **62.3% of year-to-date 2026 edtech capital** circulates alongside it; it rests on a
small sample driven by a few large deals and should not be quoted as a market share.

**Opportunities:**
- 🟢 **Kenya's unwritten AI-literacy framework is the most specific unfilled deliverable this page holds for Africa.** A national strategy commits to AI literacy across three education levels; no framework implements it; the ministry is at the planning stage as of September 2026; and the published asks are alignment to the Competency-Based Curriculum and to UNESCO's competency frameworks, with teacher training and equitable access. That is a scope statement written by the client.
- 🟢 **The UK product safety standard is a build-to-spec opportunity with no waiting period.** Unlike the EU, there is nothing to be conformant *by*; there is a published bar to be conformant *to*, now. For a vendor-side client it de-risks procurement into UK schools immediately.
- **The December 2027 window is the commercial story.** Clients have roughly 13 months of runway to get high-risk systems conformant. Conformity-by-design engagements for admissions and assessment scoring are sellable now and have a deadline attached — the strongest forcing function in any region.
- **Transparency is live today.** Article 50 compliance for existing chatbots is immediate, small, and a clean door-opener into larger work.
- **Data-residency tutoring.** Self-hosted DeepTutor or Open TutorAI CE with local models via Ollama keeps learner data in-jurisdiction — the architecture EMEA public education actually buys.
- 🟢 **Gulf tertiary mandates are a procurement trigger, not a policy debate.** Saudi Arabia requiring AI literacy in undergraduate education creates curriculum, platform and teacher-capacity demand with an institutional buyer and no ambiguity about whether to proceed. This is the one part of EMEA where the sell is capability rather than compliance.
- 🟢 **Offline-first is the architectural requirement in Sub-Saharan Africa, and the open source shelf can meet it.** Every credible African offering found is offline-capable by design. `Kolibri` (MIT, offline-first) plus a local model via Ollama plus `fsrs-rs` (BSD-3-Clause) for on-device review scheduling is a stack that works without connectivity and keeps learner data on the device — which is also the posture African builders have already chosen for trust reasons.
- **Avoid, or price carefully:** proctoring and emotion-adjacent features. Emotion recognition is banned outright, and exam monitoring is high-risk. ILIAS (GPL-3.0) is the incumbent base in German-speaking markets.
- 🟢 **p655 — the deadline split is the thing to get right in a proposal, and most published guidance is still wrong about it.** Sources read this pass converge on **Regulation (EU) 2026/1744** moving the high-risk obligation for **stand-alone education AI systems from August 2026 to December 2027**, while the **transparency duties for chatbots and synthetic content still begin in August 2026**. Two separate obligations, ~16 months apart. Guidance published in 2025 still prints August 2026 for both and is now actively misleading. ⚠️ No primary EU text was read in this session (`europa.eu` not attempted; several institutional hosts do not resolve under this session's network policy) — **confirm against the Official Journal before putting a date in a contract.**
- 🔴 **p659 — attempted, and blocked. The gap above stays open, but it is no longer “not attempted”.** p659 tried to read the primary text directly: `https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj`. The host **does not resolve** under this session's network policy (`getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND eur-lex.europa.eu`) — a policy block, not a missing document, and it is the same ceiling p655 inferred without testing. 🔵 **So the status of this base's most load-bearing regulatory date is precisely this:** `2 December 2027` for stand-alone Annex III education systems and `2 August 2026` for Article 50 transparency are **reproduced across independent secondary sources at p655, p657 and again at p659**, and have **never** been read in the Official Journal by this base. That is a strong secondary consensus, not a primary citation. 🔴 **`ACTION p659-B`:** `eur-lex.europa.eu` must be added to the session's allowed network domains before this can be discharged — no amount of further searching will close it, because the blocker is the network policy and not the availability of the text. Until then, a proposal citing these dates should cite them as *reported*, and a contract should not cite them at all without counsel reading the OJ.
- 🔧 **p660 — attempted a third time, still blocked, and p659's diagnosis was wrong about the mechanism.** p660 re-tested `https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=OJ:L_202501744`. The failure is **not** `getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND` as p659 recorded: it is `CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403` — the session's **egress proxy refuses the CONNECT**, reporting `connect_rejected` because the host is not on this session's allow-list. DNS never enters into it. 🔵 **The correction matters for how `ACTION p659-B` gets discharged:** this is not a name-resolution problem that a different URL, mirror or `eli/` path could route around (p659 implied it might be), so no further search or URL variation will close it. One thing and one thing only closes it — `eur-lex.europa.eu` added to the environment's network policy. Restated as **`ACTION p660-A`**, same substance, correct cause.
- 🟢 **p660 — the Omnibus gains its publication and application dates, which this page did not carry.** The EMEA leg has cited `Regulation (EU) 2026/1744` since p655 without ever stating when it was published or when it began to apply. Secondary sources read at p660 give: **published in the Official Journal on 24 July 2026**, **applying since 27 July 2026**, following a **political agreement on 7 May 2026**, with the stand-alone Annex III high-risk obligation at **2 December 2027**. 🔴 **Same evidential status as the dates above — secondary, not primary.** Recorded because a proposal that names the instrument should be able to say when it took effect, and because the `27 July 2026` application date is what makes the `2 December 2027` deadline a *deferral already in force* rather than a proposal. All four dates are reported, none read in the OJ.
- 🟢 **p655 — what is already in force is the part clients under-plan for.** The **prohibition on emotion recognition in educational settings has applied since February 2025**, and the **AI-literacy obligation has applied since February 2025** — amended from July 2026 to require *measures that support* literacy rather than a guaranteed level per person. That amendment is commercially favourable: it makes a documented, delivered training programme sufficient, which is a thing Globant can ship and evidence. Pair it with permissive curriculum — `ai-engineering-hub` (MIT, 41.1k★) or `Fazmin/AILearningGuide` (MIT, 248★, desktop and offline-runnable, new at p655).
- 🟢 **p655 — the deployer-liability point is the one that closes deals.** Schools and universities are **deployers** under the Act and remain responsible even when the tool is a third-party vendor's, and **non-EU suppliers are in scope whenever their system reaches EU learners**. An institution cannot buy its way out of the obligation, which is precisely why conformity-by-design and an audit trail are billable rather than overhead.
- 🟢 **p655 — EMEA now has an MIT-licensed, EU-built platform in which the agent is core.** `Selleo/mentingo` (MIT, 106★, Poland) ships a multi-tenant, white-label LMS with a voice/chat AI mentor, Langfuse tracing and an **EU-only deployment that is a configuration choice rather than a paid tier**. Against Moodle (GPL-3.0), Open edX (AGPL-3.0) and Canvas (AGPL-3.0), MIT is the difference between a client-branded closed product and a licensing conversation. Low adoption is the honest trade — this is adopt-and-harden. See `compose/patterns.md` **P14**.


##### 🟢 New at p656 — two permissive EMEA-origin rows, and Article 50 is an evidence duty

🟢 **The permissive shelf now has EMEA provenance, which matters for public-sector procurement that weights local maintenance.** Two rows shelved at p656 are European and permissive: [`numbas/Numbas`](https://github.com/numbas/Numbas) (**Apache-2.0**, 216 ★, pushed 2026-10-28) is browser e-assessment built and maintained by **Newcastle University** for its own teaching; [`openfun/marsha`](https://github.com/openfun/marsha) (**MIT**, 151 ★, pushed 2026-11-01) is an LTI content-and-video provider from **France Université Numérique** — the same house as `openfun/richie`, already shelved, so the pair forms a coherent permissive French public-sector front end. Both are production software from public institutions, not research code.

🟢 **The EU AI Act's Article 50 transparency duty applies from 2 August 2026 — unchanged by the high-risk delay to 2 December 2027 — and it is an evidence requirement.** A deployer has to be able to say what the system did and tell the learner they are interacting with AI. The high-risk delay is therefore a **window, not a reprieve**: the transparency duty lands first, and the institutions that will face the 2027 obligations are procuring now. An xAPI statement log in an Apache-2.0 LRS is the cheapest artefact that satisfies an Article 50 enquiry and pre-positions for Annex III evidence — and it stays in the institution's own database, which is the sovereignty answer EMEA buyers ask for first. Sell **P17** before selling a tutor.

⚠️ **One honest constraint on the authoring half.** European institutions author heavily in **H5P**, and `h5p/h5p-php-library` and `Lumieducation/H5P-Nodejs-library` are both **GPL-3.0** (35,146 B each); `Lumieducation/Lumi`, the desktop editor, ships **no grant file at all** on its default branch. Integrate via their APIs, never fork, and do not build a product on Lumi's unstated grant.

##### 🟢 New at p657 — the most capable permissive education platform in open source is EMEA-built, and it is MIT

**Supply, and this is the headline for the region.** [`ls1intum/Artemis`](https://github.com/ls1intum/Artemis) — **MIT**, 826 ★ / 395 forks, pushed 2026-11-05, tag `10.3.1` — is built and operated by **TUM (Technical University of Munich)** for its own teaching, with **Iris** (LLM tutor), **Athena** (feedback suggestion) and **Hyperion** (AI exercise authoring) in-tree and configuration-gated. Alongside `OpenOlat` (Apache-2.0, Zurich) from p656 and `Mentingo` (MIT, Selleo, Poland) from p655, **the three most capable permissive platform-class rows in this KB are all EMEA-origin** — and Artemis is the only one of the three with the AI layer already written. 🟢 **For an EU public-sector or university procurement this is close to ideal**: German-built, self-hosted, MIT, every AI subsystem individually switchable and evidenceable. Also EMEA-origin this pass: `OpenPronounce` (MIT, French author) and `INGInious` (🔴 AGPL-3.0, UCLouvain, Belgium).

**Regulation.** Education is **Annex III high-risk** under the EU AI Act — admission decisions, student evaluation, exam scoring — carrying an obligation to support qualified staff who can **monitor, intervene, override and shut down** the system. ⚠️ **Sources still conflict on the deadline and the more recent one governs:** a guide updated **1 September 2026** gives **2 December 2027** for stand-alone Annex III systems and **2 August 2028** for AI embedded in regulated products; older material says August 2026. This KB already carries December 2027 (**T7**, **T15**) and this pass **reproduces it** — treat the August 2026 figure as stale wherever a client's advisers cite it. The **UK Department for Education published generative AI product safety standards for education in January 2026**; **Ofqual** and **Ofsted** are the assessment and inspection authorities. In the Gulf, **Dubai's KHDA** has issued explicit responsible-AI guidance to private schools.

**Market and adoption (⚠️ secondary sources — indicative only).** Europe's AI-in-education market is put at **$2.64B in 2026 growing to $8.0B by 2030 (31.9% CAGR)**, with **Finland, Estonia and the Netherlands** leading K-12 integration. Against that, **only ~10% of 450+ surveyed institutions have established formal AI guidelines** — the same adoption-ahead-of-governance shape UNESCO measured in LATAM, in a region with a binding statute coming. The **UK government invested £4M in AI tools for lesson planning and marking**.

🟢 **The opportunity.** The gap between a high-risk classification with a hard human-oversight duty and 10% of institutions having any written policy is a **governance-and-evidence engagement, and it is the one EMEA buy that is already funded by compliance budgets rather than innovation budgets.** The deliverable is Artemis or OpenOlat deployed on the client's own infrastructure, each AI subsystem individually gated and documented, an Apache-2.0 LRS producing the audit trail, and a conformity file that names which Annex III function is in scope. ⚠️ **Do not sell the December 2027 date as comfort** — the oversight capability has to exist before the system is in production, and procurement cycles in EU public HE run 12–18 months.


🟢 **New at p658 — the substrate under the world's school coding tools is now stewarded in the UK, and that is an argument in a public-sector room.** **Blockly moved from Google to the Raspberry Pi Foundation on 10 November 2025** (Apache-2.0 retained; verified here by identical HEAD SHAs across the `google/*` and `RaspberryPiFoundation/*` address pairs, so the old addresses are *redirects*, not forks — **T36**). For ministries and EU public bodies weighing digital-sovereignty and vendor-dependency questions, the library underneath MakeCode, Scratch Blocks and Code.org being run by an **education charity in the EMEA region** rather than a US advertising company is a procurement argument that did not exist a year ago, and this base did not hold it until p658.
Two further EMEA-anchored assets were verified this pass: **`microbit-foundation/micropython-microbit-v2`** (MIT, UK micro:bit Educational Foundation) — low stars, enormous installed base, the firmware under a device distributed to school systems by the million — and **`carlosperate/ardublockly`** (Apache-2.0, 489★) for Arduino hardware labs. 🔵 **Lead a hardware-anchored EMEA engagement with micro:bit**, not with a cloud platform: the device is already in the building, its firmware is MIT, and the EU AI Act's Annex III exposure is minimal because nothing here assesses or ranks a learner. That last point is the one worth saying out loud in an EU room — **this is the rare education build that is not high-risk**, against the 2 December 2027 date that governs the rest of the portfolio (Regulation (EU) 2026/1744).


🟡 **New at p662, same caveat as its North American counterpart.** `365Talents` is **Lyon-based**, so the Docebo acquisition recorded under **North America** is also an EMEA exit — a French AI-skills company absorbed by a Canadian platform. Recorded as a vendor-consolidation indication, unverified against any filing.


##### 🟢 New at p664 — EMEA gets its first permissive agent, and the Act's deadline moved far enough that most published guidance is now wrong

**🔴 The deadline conflict, stated plainly, because it inverts the sales argument.** Education AI used for admissions, student assessment and exam scoring is **Annex III high-risk** under the EU AI Act — that part is stable. The *date* is not:

| Source class | Date asserted for stand-alone Annex III obligations |
|---|---|
| Guidance published before mid-2026 | **2 August 2026** |
| The 2026 **Digital Omnibus** (two later sources, more recent) | **2 December 2027** for stand-alone Annex III; **2 August 2028** for AI embedded in regulated products |

The later date is probably current, and both sources describe it as a **political agreement pending formal adoption**. 🔴 **The consequence for a 2026 engagement: the large majority of EU-AI-Act-for-education material in circulation was written against August 2026 and is now stale.** This base recorded the move at **T7** and **T15**; p664 re-measures it and the spread is unchanged. **Do not sell an August 2026 compliance deadline. Do not sell relief either** — the obligations did not shrink, and an institution procuring a system in 2026 will operate it well past December 2027.

**Also measured this pass:**
- **UK:** the Department for Education issued **generative AI product safety standards for education in January 2026**; **Ofqual** has published resources on AI and coursework integrity. The UK is therefore running a *product-standards* regime while the EU runs a *risk-classification* one — two different conformity stories for the same tool.
- **Gulf:** **KHDA (Dubai)** has issued explicit responsible-AI guidance to private schools.
- **Market:** the European AI-in-education segment is estimated at **$2.64B in 2026 → $8.0B by 2030 at ~31.9% CAGR**. 🔴 Vendor-style estimate, single source, unreconciled with the global figures in this file's market-size section — treat the growth *rate* as directional and do not quote the level.
- **Readiness:** one survey of **450+ institutions** found only **~10%** have established formal AI-use guidelines. Against UNESCO's 70% figure for European and North American *higher* education, the gap is the K-12 and vocational tiers.
- **Sovereignty is now a procurement default, not a preference:** TU Eindhoven's published tooling guidance treats **Mistral as EU-hosted and GDPR-compliant, with open weights for local deployment** — i.e. a named university is already specifying model provenance in its tool list.

🟢 **The supply-side change at p664, and it is the one this base has waited for.** **`RUC-Digital-Media-Lab/RUCAI`** — MIT, Roskilde University's Digital Media Lab (Denmark) — is the **first EMEA agent row on `agents/top.md`**. It is a self-hosted course AI: PostgreSQL/pgvector, **Ollama for both embeddings and chat**, teacher workspaces, and **password-protected student instances scoped to selected material**. The separation of teacher and student surfaces, with separate credentials and separate document scope, is exactly the artefact an EU data-protection officer is asking for and what a single chat UI cannot produce. ⚠️ `0★`, pilot scale, and its EMEA placement is **provisional** pending a first-hand read of `DOI 10.5281/zenodo.20622874` (`agents/trending.md` `664-H`).

🟢 **And a permissive, publicly funded substrate underneath it.** **`OpenEuroLLM`** (EU co-funded, EuroHPC-targeted) is live — five of its fifteen repositories were pushed within 24 hours of this measurement — and its evaluation harness **`JudgeArena`** (Apache-2.0) is now on `repos/foundations.md`. ⚠️ Six of the fifteen carry **no licence at all**: resolve grants per repository, never per owner (**T16**).

🟢 **Opportunities, ordered by what the regulation actually compels:**

1. **The Annex III conformity file for an education buyer, built once and resold.** Risk classification, data governance, logging, human oversight, Article 50 transparency. The deadline move buys the *time to do it properly* and removes the panic discount — reprice accordingly.
2. **Sovereign, zero-egress tutoring that is demonstrable in an afternoon.** `RUCAI` + Ollama + pgvector, on the institution's own VM, is now a *named reference* rather than an architecture diagram — paired with **OpenTutor** and **wordlink** (both run with no API key) and the permissive platform tier (**OpenOlat**, Apache-2.0). See **P11**, **P12** and the new **P29**.
3. **Dual UK/EU conformity as a single deliverable.** A tool sold into both must satisfy DfE product safety standards *and* Annex III. Nobody is selling that as one artefact.
4. **Local-judge evaluation as the audit evidence.** `JudgeArena` with a local backend produces assessment-quality evidence without sending student work anywhere — the cheapest existing answer to *"show me this model is fit for exam scoring"*.



🟢 **p667 — EMEA is the strongest supply region of this pass, and both placements rest on a channel this base could not previously run.** Two addresses placed to EMEA on the owner's own location field (`667-G1`, positive on `location:Germany`): **`Human-Centric-Machine-Learning/memorize`** — the code *and dataset* for *Enhancing Human Learning via Spaced Repetition Optimization*, **PNAS 2019** — and **`telekom/ki-in-schulen`**, Deutsche Telekom AG's AI@School classroom material with a Fraunhofer IAIS contribution, the only corporate-backed MIT school curriculum this base holds. A third, **`insolitos/IA_para_aprender`**, is placed to EMEA on the repository's own text: an AI-literacy web application written for **Portuguese school libraries** against the Ensino Básico and Secundário cycles. 🔵 **Why the library channel is commercially interesting here:** the EMEA literacy obligations this section already tracks — the UK DfE product-safety standards and the EU AI Act's literacy duty — are procured by exactly this kind of institution, and a school-library delivery route is one no other asset on these shelves uses. 🔴 **The PNAS row matters for a different reason:** trend **T10** names the missing evidence base as this sector's weak point, and an EMEA engagement arguing for a particular review schedule now has a peer-reviewed result with its data attached rather than a vendor claim.


##### 🟢 New at p668 — two of the three EU obligations are already live, and that is the sales fact most guidance still gets wrong

- 🔴 **Re-verified at p668 and unchanged from `T7`/`T15`: the deadline that moved is the *only* one that moved.** The Digital Omnibus (**Regulation (EU) 2026/1744**) shifted stand-alone Annex III high-risk education systems — admissions, student evaluation, exam scoring — from `2026-08-02` to **`2027-12-02`**, with AI embedded in regulated products at `2028-08-02`. But **Article 50 transparency applies from `2026-08-02`** and **Article 4 AI literacy has applied since `2025-02-02`**. 🟢 **So a school that reads "the deadline moved to 2027" and stands down is already non-compliant on two counts today** — and that misreading is in circulation, because multiple vendor compliance guides still publish the August 2026 high-risk date.
- 🔵 **The deployer is the school, even when the tool is someone else's.** Schools carry deployer obligations for third-party tools, and vendors carry conformity duties, so `P37`'s week 6–8 artefact has two buyers in the same engagement. Enforcement splits: the Commission's **AI Office** supervises general-purpose model providers; national authorities take most of the rest.
- 🟢 **`T50`'s one permissive system of record matters more here than anywhere.** EMEA public-sector procurement is where a GPL-2.0 or AGPL-3.0 system of record most often *is* the incumbent, and `P32` already covers not inheriting the EUPL from a ministry stack. `P37` extends the same move to the SIS.
- 🟡 **One EMEA demand-side novelty found and *not* filed, declared so it is retried rather than re-searched.** European Parliament briefing **`784574`** — *"Artificial Intelligence in Classrooms: Pedagogical Dimensions"*, March 2026 — is unseen by this base on seven independent needles, but `www.europarl.europa.eu` **does not resolve under this session's network policy**, so it could not be byte-read and is not recorded as fact. 🔴 **An unverifiable novelty is a network gap, not a finding.**

- 🆕 **p671 — stewardship is a sovereignty question here, which makes `T52` an EMEA argument before it is an engineering one.** The two owner-changes `671-K` measured both moved **toward an institution**: an individual maintainer's accessibility library is now held by the **DAISY Consortium** (`daisy/MathCAT`), and a platform is now named for its own foundation (`openedx/openedx-platform`). 🔵 **For a buyer whose procurement already asks where a system is hosted and under whose law it is governed, *who stewards this dependency* is the same question one layer down** — and it is the question an AGPL/Annex-III-exposed education stack most needs answered, because the steward is who can relicense it. `P40` produces that answer as a standing weekly check rather than a one-off due-diligence slide.
- 🔵 **And it lands on an accessibility obligation this region already has.** The row `671-L` repaired is the mathematics half of accessibility — MathML to speech in 15 languages and braille — and its new owner is the body that writes the accessibility standards. `671-L` also closed the `🔴 unmeasured` star figure this base carried for 10 passes (`131★`), and `p671` filed its delivery half, `daisy/MathCATForPython` (MIT, `12★`), the NVDA plugin that puts it in front of a student using a real screen reader.
- 🔴 **Demand-side gap unchanged and restated:** no EMEA-wide adoption rate for AI in schools or universities was found this pass either; the European Parliament briefing `784574` remains **unverifiable from this session** (`www.europarl.europa.eu` does not resolve under this network policy), so it is still not filed.
- 🟢 **p680 places an EMEA address, and the interesting part is the evidence class rather than the row.** [`jsboige/CoursIA`](https://github.com/jsboige/CoursIA) — **MIT** (`1,075 B`, verbatim body), a French-language AI curriculum of Jupyter notebooks running from search and constraints through formal reasoning, uncertainty, game theory, ML, RL and generative AI to mechanised theorem proving in **Lean 4**, with many concepts shipped as **C#/Python twins** on .NET Interactive. Pushed `2026-11-06`. 🔵 **Why a `16★` repository is a sellable asset:** it carries **`153` forks** — `f/★ = 9.563`, nine times this base's census maximum — which is a **classroom** signature, one copy per learner, not a deployment count. For a French-speaking upskilling engagement this is an MIT curriculum with a first-class **.NET** track, which is rare and matters for enterprise clients already on a Microsoft stack. See `intel/trends.md` **T71**.
- 🔴 **A standing EMEA hazard, reproduced for a fifth time at p680 and worth saying to every client in the region:** the public literature this base's EMEA leg reaches still states that the EU AI Act's high-risk obligations for education begin in **August 2026**. This base holds **2 December 2027** (`T7`, `T15`). The stale date is **better indexed** than the current one, so an EMEA compliance conversation sourced from search will arrive with the wrong deadline. 🔵 Unchanged limitation: the primary text cannot be read from this session (`eur-lex.europa.eu` and `europarl.europa.eu` do not resolve under this network policy), so the `2027` reading remains **secondary** and should be presented as such.
- 🔴 **p684 — EMEA demand leg nil (`3/3` held).** Searched `AI education EMEA 2026 adoption regulation players`. Returned the UK DfE generative-AI product safety standards (January 2026), Ofqual's AI-and-coursework-integrity resources (March 2026), and Finland / Estonia / Netherlands as K-12 integration leaders — all three already held, and the Regulation **(EU) 2026/1744** dates (Annex III standalone `2027-12-02`, embedded `2028-08-02`) re-confirmed unchanged.
- 🟢 **p684 places an EMEA address, and the evidence class is the transferable part.** [`DSL4-Digital/RefExists`](https://github.com/DSL4-Digital/RefExists) — **MIT** (`1,076 B`, sha256₁₂ `a7f427ec0d9d`), `main` @ `5fa3ffbaf3bc` — is a fully client-side citation reality-checker against Crossref, arXiv and Semantic Scholar. It was placed **not** on language, not on a named institution, but on `S.L.U.` in its own copyright line: *Sociedad Limitada Unipersonal*, a company form existing only in the Spanish commercial register. 🟢 **A registry fact, at zero marginal cost, from a file this base already reads for every row — see `T80`**, which generalises the channel across `GmbH`, `B.V.`, `Oy`, `S.à r.l.` and the rest of the EMEA entity vocabulary.
- 🟢 **The EMEA sell this pass is assessment integrity, and the timing is better here than anywhere.** Ofqual has already published on coursework integrity, the UK DfE has product safety standards in force, and the EU's Annex III assessment obligations bind on `2027-12-02` — so a European institution has roughly a year of lead time and, per `T79`, **no permissive supply above `20★` to buy**. The decidable three in **P60** are the defensible answer, because a classifier-based detector cannot be evidenced to a regulator and a resolved DOI can.

### APAC

#### 🆕 Added at p721 — the region supplied EVERY agent filing this pass, and the compulsory-curriculum mandates explain why

🟢 **APAC filed `3 / 3` of this pass's agent rows** (`good-learning`, `HiNote`, `Morrow`) and now holds **`91 / 345`** — the largest region on the shelf. The supply is not an accident: it tracks national mandates that create a domestic user base before any export market exists.

| Country | Mandate / rule | Date |
|---|---|---|
| **China** | Compulsory national AI curriculum, **from age 6**; one of only two countries running one since the 2025-26 school year | in force |
| **UAE** | The other compulsory national AI curriculum | in force |
| **India** | AI and computational thinking **compulsory from Class 3**, tied to the IndiaAI Mission and teacher training | **2026-27 academic year** |
| **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act** in force | **22 Jan 2026** |
| **Vietnam** | AI law in force | **1 Mar 2026** |
| **China** | Anthropomorphic-AI rules issued 10 Apr 2026 | **effective 15 Jul 2026** |
| **India** | Amended IT Rules — AI content must carry prominent labels and provenance markers | **from 20 Feb 2026** |
| **Japan / Singapore / Australia** | Guidance-led, no binding AI statute | — |

🔵 **Adoption, with the caveat attached:** one report characterises the region as *"adoption breadth is extraordinary but depth remains shallow"*; India is described as APAC's fastest-growing AI market at **`38.9%` CAGR**. Public opinion is notably more permissive — **only `26%` in India support banning AI in schools against `39%` globally** (Ipsos). 🟡 UNESCO's read is that the region is **uneven**: China and Singapore have established AI-in-education policies while others are still meeting basic educational needs.

🔵 **Opportunity:** this is the **source region for components, not just a target market**. Every APAC agent filed this pass is MIT and built as a *skill on a host harness* rather than an application — **so the cheapest route to a North America or EMEA tutor product is to localise an APAC skill, not to build one.** 🔴 **The gate is language, not licence:** `good-learning`'s prompts, rubrics and output templates are Chinese-primary, so localisation is a translation-and-pedagogy task of real size, and it is the thing a client is actually paying for.


#### 🆕 Added at p720 — the regulatory map is now the most binding in the world, and APAC is also where this pass's best ARTEFACT came from

**Statutes, as returned by this pass's leg:**

- 🔴 **South Korea — the most advanced: the AI Basic Act entered into force `2026-01-22`**, imposing transparency, risk assessment and human-oversight duties on high-impact AI. A grace period focused on guidance rather than penalties runs through 2026. **The grace period, not the Act, is the window.**
- 🔴 **Vietnam — a risk-based law starting 2026, and a decision dated `2026-07-02` names high-risk AI systems in six sectors, EDUCATION AMONG THEM.** This is the detail most likely to be missed: Vietnam has explicitly scheduled education AI as high-risk.
- **China** — the most assertive: mandatory registration, strict content labelling, penalties.
- **Japan** — voluntary, innovation-friendly, possibly pivoting to mandatory.
- **Australia** — no AI-specific statute; the higher-education regulator has required providers to submit plans addressing generative-AI risks.
- **Singapore** — toolkits (**AI Verify**) rather than binding law.
- 🔵 **Cross-border: the EU AI Act reaches APAC vendors selling into EU schools, so the EMEA timetable above is an APAC compliance input too.**

🔴 **A named players list — Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson, Byju's, with China, India and Japan said to dominate — came from a single commercial market report and is LOW CONFIDENCE.** It names no regulators and no local providers, and this pass did not corroborate it. Recorded with that caveat rather than dropped, and it should not be cited in a deck.

🟢 **The supply story is the opposite of the policy story, and it is strong.** `APAC 85 → 88` this pass, and APAC produced **the best single artefact p720 found anywhere**: [`Divyakush2006/OS-Tutor`](https://github.com/Divyakush2006/OS-Tutor) (Vellore Institute of Technology, India) — a McNemar-tested, simulator-verified tutor at `0★` whose ablation shows **the solver is the lever and the fine-tune alone is not statistically significant**. [`turtlelnc/MES-Homework`](https://github.com/turtlelnc/MES-Homework) (China) independently implements the same boundary, and [`HKUDS/DeepTutor`](https://github.com/HKUDS/DeepTutor) (University of Hong Kong, `57,248★`) anchors the shelf.

🔵 **The APAC opportunity in one sentence: this is the region producing the architecture, and the region whose own regulation (Korea's oversight duties, Vietnam's high-risk designation) most rewards being able to PROVE an answer was computed rather than generated.** `720-V`'s pattern and APAC's rulebook fit each other exactly.

#### 🆕 Added at p719 — FOUR regimes, four different answers, and education is named high-risk in two of them

🟢 **APAC moved more than any other region on the agent shelf this pass (`77 → 85`), and the regulatory picture explains why the supply is domestic:** there is no single APAC rule to build to, so each market grows its own tooling.

- 🔴 **South Korea — the most comprehensive regime, and it is LIVE.** The **AI Basic Act entered into force 22 January 2026.** Enforcement decrees are still being finalised, and a **grace period running through 2026 is focused on guidance rather than penalties.** 🟢 **That grace window is a dated commercial opportunity:** governance work landed inside it is remediation-before-enforcement; the same work in 2027 is remediation-after.
- 🔴 **Vietnam — education is NAMED high-risk.** A risk-based framework identifies high-risk AI systems across six sectors **including education, with automated assessment given as the example**, under a Digital Technology Industry Law commencing 2026. 🟢 **This directly re-prices a row filed this pass:** [`duanaithcs-cloud/teacheros-hybrid-agent-template`](https://github.com/duanaithcs-cloud/teacheros-hybrid-agent-template) (MIT, placed `APAC` on `Vietnam` ×2 in its README) is a compliance artefact in its own jurisdiction, not just a lesson-plan scaffold.
- 🔴 **China — the most assertive.** Mandatory registration, strict **content-labelling** obligations, penalties up to **¥50M**. 🟢 **The four Chinese-primary rows filed this pass** (`master-teacher`, `Inwit`, `english-tutor-agent`, `research-pilot`) are what building inside that regime looks like, and the standards-and-provenance habit shows in the artefacts — `master-teacher`'s `来源分级` **source-tier grading** is a labelling-regime feature expressed as a product one.
- 🟡 **Japan and Singapore — voluntary.** Singapore works through frameworks and toolkits (**AI Verify**). 🔵 Note the interaction with `719-P`: the proprietary **AGENTIC STAR** platform behind two foundation rows filed this pass is **SoftBank**, Japan — a voluntary-regime market producing a closed vendor platform, which is a different competitive shape from China's or Korea's.
- 🟡 **Australia — no AI-specific law yet.** Its 2024 proposal **named education among high-risk areas** for mandatory guardrails; sources expect the guardrails to firm up through 2026.
- 🔴 **EU spillover is a real APAC constraint, not a footnote.** APAC edtech vendors selling into EU schools fall under the EU AI Act's high-risk education category regardless of their home regime — **so the Annex III date dispute recorded under EMEA binds APAC exporters too.**

**Market.** Commercial research names Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson and Byju's as key players and China, India and Japan as the dominant markets. 🔵 Vendor-report rankings, treated with caution.

🔴 **Declared gap, and it is a real one:** p719 found **no current, education-specific 2026 ADOPTION statistics for APAC.** Regulation is well covered; teacher- and student-level usage rates are not. Every APAC figure available to this pass was regulatory or vendor-commercial. **That gap is named rather than filled with a global number standing in for a regional one.**

🟢 **Opportunity, placed and specific.** The APAC pattern is **one product, many jurisdictional policy configurations** — Korea's live Act, Vietnam's high-risk education designation, China's labelling and registration, Australia's pending guardrails, plus EU Annex III for anyone exporting. The engagement shape is a **policy-configuration layer** over a common core, not a per-country rebuild. 🟢 **This region is also where the supply is, so the correct posture is COMPOSE rather than BUILD:** `APAC 85` is the largest non-`Global` cohort on the agent shelf and a studio entering Korea, Vietnam or China should expect to adapt a domestic artefact rather than port a North American one.

#### 🆕 Re-measured at p718 (2026-11-09) — the region now has BINDING law where most of the world has guidance, and education is named in it

| Jurisdiction | Instrument | Education exposure |
|---|---|---|
| **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act in force January 2026** — the most advanced in the region | 🟢 A grace period runs through 2026 with the ministry treating it as a **pilot year**: guidance over penalties. That is a closing window, and the cheapest year in which to get conformance right |
| **Vietnam** | Risk-based law commencing **2026**; a **July 2026 decision enumerates high-risk systems** | 🔴 **Automated assessment in education is named explicitly in that list** — among the most specific education-AI provisions anywhere |
| **China** | The most detailed rules: **mandatory registration and content labelling**, with penalties | Any learner-facing generative surface is in scope, and labelling is a product requirement |
| **Australia** | Proposed **mandatory guardrails** for high-risk uses **including education**; the higher-education regulator already **requires universities to submit plans addressing generative-AI risks** | 🟢 The submitted-plan requirement is a live, named deliverable a studio can be engaged to produce |
| **Japan · Singapore** | Voluntary frameworks and governance toolkits | 🟢 Singapore's toolkit channel is how `p713` reached `aiverify-foundation` — a regulator publishing testable, permissively-licensed instruments |

**Commercial structure:** a market report names **Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson and Byju's** as key players and **China, India and Japan** as the dominant markets, with ministries of education listed among the stakeholders. ⚠️ Commercial research estimate, not an authoritative source.

**Data protection is the soft spot:** several APAC governments are still *drafting* data-protection rules for AI in education, and some countries have no GDPR-equivalent at all. 🔴 **That is the inverse of the EMEA risk and it is worse for a vendor, not better** — the absence of a rule today is not a safe harbour, and p718's privacy ladder is the control that survives whichever rule arrives.

🔴 **Declared gap, measured not assumed: NO solid classroom-adoption statistics for APAC surfaced.** The leg returned regulation richly and behaviour not at all. This base holds `77` `APAC` agent rows and `0` credible APAC adoption rates — **the strongest supply-to-demand evidence asymmetry in this file, and the exact mirror image of LATAM's.** A per-ministry leg (Korea MOE, Singapore MOE, Australia TEQSA) is the way to close it.


#### 🆕 Added at p717 — the region split into THREE DIFFERENT MARKETS, and one of them went backwards

🟢 **China — a mandate, and it is the largest single education-AI demand event anywhere.** Ministry of Education guidelines (May 2025) make AI instruction compulsory **from roughly age six, across all school grades**. Reported shape: **at least `6` hours a year** for lower grades, **one hour a fortnight** for higher grades, formalised as a named subject — *"Artificial Intelligence and Technology"* — for the **2026–2027** year. 🔴 **Two hard constraints that invert the usual product:** primary pupils are **barred from using generative AI independently**, and teachers are **barred from substituting AI tools for core instructional duties**. So the buyable thing is **curriculum, teacher training and assessment of AI literacy** — not a student-facing chatbot, which is the thing most vendors are selling.

🔴 **South Korea — a REVERSAL, and it is the most useful negative datapoint in the region.** AI textbooks were slated as mandatory from 2025; lawmakers then **stripped them of "textbook" status and reclassified them as "supplementary materials"**, moving the adoption decision to individual principals. Reported uptake before the change was **under `30%` of `6,339` elementary schools**. 🟢 **Read it as a procurement lesson, not a market exit:** a national mandate that arrives before teacher capability and evidence does not convert, and the spend reappears as **`6,339` separate school-level decisions**. That is a channel problem, and it is a different engagement from a ministry programme.

🟢 **Japan — permissive and cautious at once, which favours pilots.** The AI Basic Plan was adopted **December 2025** with a **second-phase plan on `2026-07-14`**; regulation **promotes AI without penalties**. MEXT holds a **neutral stance** and is watching school pilots. Its guidelines (2024, updated 2026) steer generative AI to **lesson preparation, administrative drafting and communication, with teacher judgement of output required** — i.e. the Japanese buyer is the *teacher workload* case, not the student tutor case.

**Regulatory baseline for the rest of the region:** South Korea has a general AI law in force; China regulates **by type of service**; India has **no dedicated AI law** and works through its data-protection statute, sector regulators and voluntary guidance, with **AI-content labelling rules effective `2026-02-20`**; Vietnam is named by one source as holding the region's only comprehensive AI statute. 🔴 **Sources conflict on who is "first"** (one puts South Korea ahead, another Vietnam) — treat the ranking as unsettled.

**Sentiment, for pricing resistance:** Ipsos Education Monitor 2026 finds **weak support for banning AI in schools** — about **`26%` in India**, **`29%` in Japan** (up from `21%`). UNESCO records **China, Japan, South Korea and Singapore** already building AI-focused curricula.

🔴 **Gap, stated:** beyond the China mandate, the leg found **no 2026 education-specific ministry directive text** for India or Japan — only secondary coverage. The China figures are news and commentary, **not the directive itself**.

#### 🟡 p716 — APAC: **the adoption number is low and specific (`30 %` virtual tutors), the convening calendar is dense, and the REGULATORY read came back EMPTY — recorded as a gap, not as coverage**

🟢 **The one hard adoption figure: virtual-tutor adoption stands at `30 %` across the surveyed cohort**, against uptake described as **patchy**, with **limited connectivity named as the probable barrier**. 🔵 **That pairing is the most actionable thing in this leg.** The region is simultaneously called the **fastest-growing** (against North America's larger absolute share), yet its binding constraint is bandwidth rather than appetite. **It is also a direct demand signal for a specific property on this base's shelves: offline-first.** `716-E`'s `Meridian` (offline-first LMS) and `Sagavar` (a tutor stated to run in `4 GB` of RAM with no internet) are filed against exactly this constraint, and `Kolibri` has been on the verticals shelf for passes. A vendor-estimated **`+50 %` adoption of AI-based learning platforms over two years** also appeared; it is recorded as vendor-shaped and is **not** used.

🔵 **Attitudinal, and it cuts against the Western default.** **Ipsos** finds **lower support for banning AI in schools across the Asian markets examined** than elsewhere. The permission conversation that dominates North American and EMEA engagements is therefore **not** the opening conversation here — a difference in sales motion, not just in sentiment.

🔴 **The regulatory read FAILED, and p716 states it as a measured absence.** This leg returned **no region-wide APAC AI-in-schools regulatory framework** and **no verified list of leading APAC AI-education companies**. The only binding instruments it surfaced were the **UK's** (January 2026) and the **UAE's** (February 2026) — **both EMEA, and both refiled to the EMEA section above.** 🔵 **This is an informed gap, and it is written down because silence here would look exactly like coverage.** It does **not** mean APAC is unregulated: `p715` established that the first binding instrument in the region to name education as high-risk is **Vietnam's**, which this pass's leg did not surface at all. **The mandated regional leg cannot see national-ministry instruments in non-English languages — that is the channel defect, and it must be closed by probing ministries directly** (China, Singapore, Australia, South Korea, Japan, Vietnam, India) rather than by re-running the keyword leg and getting the same nil.

**Convening calendar — unusually dense, and dates are the usable part:** the **World Bank Global EdTech Policy Academy on AI**, Seoul, **`2026-08-31` → `2026-09-04`**; **Cambridge English**'s Asia-Pacific leaders' convening on AI in English-language education (`2026-05-11`); **Acer**'s Asia Pacific summit pushing AI-driven learning on an infrastructure-plus-devices-plus-partnerships strategy; and **EdTech Hub**'s South-East Asia work, which places **ethical governance among five AI-adoption dimensions** alongside partnerships and marginalised learners. **Times Higher Education** separately reports calls for *“more coordinated”* investment to unlock Asian AI potential.

🔵 **Opportunity, placed:** APAC is this base's largest *supply* region (`APAC 76` on the agents shelf, `p716`) and its thinnest *regulatory* region. The engagement shape that follows is **build-and-localise**, not comply-and-evidence — and the national-curriculum rows filed this pass (`CHNragdoll/anki-pipeline` for China's 考研, `cagoooo/taic-edu` for Taiwan's sovereign corpus, `hs-shell/dotbugi` for Hansung, `MarkChu-git/Always-Ontrack` for Monash) are the reusable evidence that curriculum-specific delivery is the regional norm rather than the exception.

#### 🟢 p715 — APAC: **the first binding instrument in the region to NAME education as high-risk is Vietnam's, not China's**

🔴 **`0` repositories, and `APAC 70` unmoved.** 🔴 **No APAC-specific adoption percentages returned**; the "key players" list the leg produced (Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson, Byju's) came from a **vendor market report** and is **not filed as fact**.

🟢 **The finding is a specific, dated, education-naming obligation.** **Vietnam's high-risk AI list explicitly covers education** — automated assessment and behavioural uses among them — and systems **already in operation before `2026-08-15`** carry additional requirements. Vietnam's Digital Technology Industry Law also takes effect in 2026. 🔵 **This matters because it inverts the regional expectation:** China has the most extensive framework overall (registration, content labelling, penalties), but it is **Vietnam** that names education as a high-risk domain in a binding list.

🟢 **Second hard obligation, and it is institution-level rather than vendor-level:** Australia's higher-education regulator **TEQSA requires every provider to file an institutional action plan** addressing generative-AI risk. That is a deliverable with a named owner at every university in the country.

🔵 **The rest of the regional map, as three regimes rather than one:** comprehensive risk-based (**South Korea** — AI Basic Act in force `2026-01-22`, on a guidance-first grace period through 2026, penalties held back; **Vietnam**), targeted (**China**), light-touch (**Japan**, **Singapore** — toolkits such as **AI Verify**; **Australia** pre-statute; **New Zealand** updating privacy and consumer rules instead of writing new AI law).

🔵 **Extraterritorial note:** the EU AI Act reaches APAC providers serving EU learners, so the Annex III contradiction recorded under EMEA this pass is an **APAC compliance input too**, not only a European one.


#### 🔴 p714 — APAC: **the region's own leg returned NO education content at all, and that nil is the entry**

🔴 **The mandated APAC leg produced `0` education-specific market sizings, `0` named edtech vendors and `0` ministry-level AI-in-schools regulations.** Nearly everything it returned was general enterprise AI: Boomi's APAC tech priorities, Forrester on pilots-to-production, an Omdia survey of `1,100+` decision-makers across Australia, New Zealand, Singapore, Malaysia and the Philippines **that does not break out education**, and Twilio on bot-disclosure rules in customer service. 🔵 **Written down explicitly, because an unrecorded nil is indistinguishable from coverage** — and because it stands in sharp contrast to the supply picture: `APAC 70` is the **second-largest region column on this base's agent shelf**, behind only `Global`. **APAC is where this base finds the most education code and the least education market data.** That asymmetry is itself the regional intelligence.

🟢 **The one durable regional theme the leg does support is SOVEREIGNTY, and it is sourced:** `itnews.asia` frames *“AI sovereignty will set the pace for Asia Pacific in 2026”*, Forrester describes APAC enterprises moving from pilots toward **sovereign, governed deployment**, and analysts expect the region to converge on shared principles — safety, transparency, accountability — while **implementing them per market**. 🔵 **For an education engagement that is a build constraint rather than a talking point: per-market implementation of common principles is exactly what `P111`'s policy-node architecture exists for, and sovereign deployment is what makes a self-hosted inference stack the default here rather than a concession.**

🔵 **Named instruments** (per `713-G`): **Acer Edu Summit Asia Pacific 2026** (regional convening, promotional source — weak), **Australia's National AI Plan** and its new **Chief AI Officers**, and Singapore's **MAS** consultation on AI in financial institutions (finance, not education). 🔴 **`0 / 4` resolved to a repository this pass** — p713's APAC leg named AI Verify and resolved it; p714's named nothing addressable. **The named-instrument channel works, but it depends on the leg naming an instrument, and this one named events and plans instead.**

🔵 **Education-adjacent regulatory pressure worth tracking:** governments across the region are refining **data-protection, privacy and cross-border transfer** rules, which bind education providers even though no source framed them that way — cross-border transfer in particular is what decides whether a student-data product can use a non-local model at all.

#### 🟢 p713 — APAC: **THE REGION'S REGULATORS SHIP OPEN SOURCE, AND THAT MAKES COMPLIANCE A BUILD RATHER THAN A PURCHASE**

🟢 **The pass's structural finding about APAC (`713-G`): it is the one region where a regulator's own testing instrument is on GitHub under a permissive licence.** Singapore's **AI Verify**, stewarded by the AI Verify Foundation under IMDA, is Apache-2.0 — [`aiverify-foundation/aiverify`](https://github.com/aiverify-foundation/aiverify), `106★`, now on `../verticals/solutions.md`, with its CI harness [`moonshot-cicd`](https://github.com/aiverify-foundation/moonshot-cicd) on `../agents/top.md`. **An institution in this region can demonstrate conformance by running the regulator's own harness, as a plugin-authoring job on an Apache-2.0 base.**

| jurisdiction | instrument | status |
|---|---|---|
| **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act** — transparency, risk assessment, human oversight, documentation for high-impact AI | 🔴 **in force `2026-01-22`**; enforcement decrees still being finalised, **guidance-first grace period through 2026** |
| **Vietnam** | **Decision No. 33/2026** names **education — specifically automated assessment — among six high-risk sectors**; Digital Technology Industry Law, risk-based | 🔴 **2026** |
| **China** | the region's most extensive framework: mandatory registration, strict content-labelling obligations | in force |
| **Singapore** | **AI Verify** toolkit — frameworks companies use to test and show accountability | 🟢 open source, Apache-2.0 |
| **Australia** | **TEQSA** has required **all** higher-education providers to submit institutional action plans addressing generative-AI risks | in force |
| **Japan** | light-touch, voluntary governance to encourage innovation | in force |

🔴 **Vietnam is the sharpest single line for this base: a national instrument that names automated assessment as high-risk.** Every item-generation and autograding pattern on `../compose/patterns.md` is in scope there by name, which makes the *verification-first* posture (`P109`) a regulatory requirement rather than a design preference.

🟢 **Australia's TEQSA mandate is the most directly saleable item in the table** — it obliges **every** provider to produce a document, with no technology procurement attached and no grace period. **That is a dated deliverable for a known, enumerable set of institutions.** 🔵 And `moodlehq/moodleapp`'s grant places Moodle HQ in Australia (`package.json`: *"Moodle Pty Ltd."*), so the platform most Australian providers run is itself an APAC artefact.

🔴 **Korea's grace period is the timing signal: guidance through 2026, penalties after.** Engagements started now land inside the window where an institution can still shape its own documentation rather than defend it.

🟢 **Supply: APAC `63 → 68`, the largest regional gain this pass** — but 🔴 **it is two owners, not a region opening up**: `HKUDS` (University of Hong Kong Data Intelligence Lab) contributed `7` rows across two shelves and `aiverify-foundation` `2`. **Concentration, stated so it is not read as breadth.** The `HKUDS` cohort is also where this pass's retrieval substrate came from — [`LightRAG`](https://github.com/HKUDS/LightRAG) (`40,609★`), [`RAG-Anything`](https://github.com/HKUDS/RAG-Anything) (`24,463★`), [`MiniRAG`](https://github.com/HKUDS/MiniRAG) — `67,100★` of MIT retrieval infrastructure from one APAC lab.

🔵 **Market framing, treated as a single-source claim:** one market report names **Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson and Byju's** as key players and says **China, India and Japan dominate** the regional AI-in-education market, while noting that **GDPR-equivalent laws are absent in many countries**, leaving student data exposed. 🔴 **Declared gap: the APAC leg returned no recent adoption statistics and no list of education-specific regulators** — the regulatory material it found is general AI regulation, not education-specific, except for Vietnam's sector naming and Australia's TEQSA action-plan requirement.

#### 🟡 p712 — APAC: **`APAC 62 → 63`, and the region's honest result is that its MANDATED LEG WAS THE THINNEST OF ALL FOUR — no regulator, no policy, no player named**

🔴 **Declared gap, stated plainly so silence is not read as coverage.** The mandated leg `AI education APAC 2026 adoption regulation players` returned **general enterprise-AI material and nothing education-specific**: no APAC education regulator, no national AI-in-schools policy, and no regional edtech player. One of its results was about the **United Kingdom**. What it did return is context — Forrester reporting APAC organisations investing in generative AI more aggressively than other regions, Singapore's financial-sector AI consultations as the template for how regulators there move, and a forecast that APAC converges on safety/transparency/accountability principles while implementing them per market — **none of which is about education.** This base holds far better APAC education detail from earlier passes; p712 adds no regulatory reading because the leg produced none.

🟢 **The one supply row came from the agent sweep, not the leg, and it is the largest system filed this pass.** [`wongcyrus/Google-AI-Classroom`](https://github.com/wongcyrus/Google-AI-Classroom) (MIT, `1,062 B` read from bytes, Hong Kong) is a full exam and lab platform: browser-native edge invigilation with MediaPipe face/iris mesh, LiteRT Whisper and LiteRT Gemma 4 E2B in isolated Web Workers at `15–30 FPS`, hot-swapping from cloud fallback to **100% on-device inference with zero egress**; WebAuthn passkey attendance from rotating HMAC-SHA256 QR codes on the projector; anti-proxy one-student-one-phone hardware locks with an exemption toggle for handsets without Google services.

🔵 **Three transferable things, and the privacy architecture is the commercial one.** (1) **Zero-egress proctoring**: the student's face and voice never leave the browser, which converts the hardest objection to AI invigilation — and the hardest data-protection filing — into an architecture choice. APAC data-protection and cross-border-transfer rules are tightening, so a design in which nothing crosses a border is a procurement advantage, not a feature. (2) **The stance**: explicitly *"not a punitive monitoring tool"* but a Proactive Proctor, Technical Support Assistant and Wellness Coach. (3) **Shared-PC reality**: pre-warming models in a readiness wizard on DeepFreeze lab machines, and an exemption path for non-GMS handsets — **the hardware assumptions of an institution that actually runs shared labs**, which no Western-authored row on these shelves makes.

🟢 **Placement, on two channels that agree:** the owner declares `Hong Kong`, and the artifact carries `Asia/Hong_Kong` in `37` files (`712-A`). 🔵 And one row on the sibling foundations shelf shows the region's research output continuing: [`Yumo216/MCKT`](https://github.com/Yumo216/MCKT) (owner declares `Japan`; grant holder's name written in Chinese characters) is the first Mamba-architecture knowledge tracer on these shelves.

🔴 **Declared gap, explicit rather than silent: the mandated APAC leg returned `0` repositories AND nothing on the adoption side** — no edtech vendor landscape, no ministry-led rollout figures, no uptake percentages for APAC education specifically. That asymmetry has now persisted for several passes and should be named as a standing hole, not re-discovered each time.

#### 🟡 p705 — APAC: the most heavily regulated region on this page and the **least measured**, and `2` of the `4` agents filed this pass are APAC

🔵 **Regulation, as returned this pass — note that education duties arrive via *high-risk classification* rather than education-specific statutes, with one exception:**

| Jurisdiction | Instrument / status at `p705` |
|---|---|
| 🟢 **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act in force `22 Jan 2026`** — transparency, risk assessment, human oversight, documentation for high-impact systems. Enforcement decrees still being finalised; **guidance-first grace period through `2026`** |
| 🟢 **Vietnam** | **AI law effective `1 Mar 2026`**, risk-based. A later decision names **six high-risk sectors and education is one**, with **automated assessment** given as the example |
| 🔴 **China** | Mandatory **registration**, strict **content labelling**, penalties up to **¥`50M`** |
| 🟡 **Japan** | Light-touch, **voluntary** governance, explicitly to encourage innovation |
| 🟡 **Singapore** | Frameworks and toolkits — **`AI Verify`** — for testing and demonstrating accountability |
| 🟢 **Australia** | **The education-specific one: `TEQSA` requires ALL higher-education providers to submit institutional action plans addressing generative-AI risk.** No AI-specific statute yet |
| 🟡 **Hong Kong** | Generative-AI guidance; focus on ethical and data-privacy governance |

🟢 **`705-APAC-1` — Australia's TEQSA mandate is the region's cleanest engagement and the reason is structural: it is a universal, per-institution, document-shaped obligation.** Every provider must file a plan; plans need evidence of controls; evidence needs instrumentation. 🔵 **Vietnam's naming of *automated assessment* as the worked example of education high-risk is the second-cleanest**, because it tells a vendor exactly which feature triggers the regime.

🟡 **Data sovereignty recurs as a procurement precondition across the region** (Malaysia treats it as foundational to AI adoption), which privileges **self-hostable and on-device** architectures over hosted APIs.

🟢 **Where this pass's filed repositories land in APAC — and this is the region's real supply story.** Three of the four agents filed at [`../agents/top.md`](../agents/top.md) this pass are APAC-authored: [`CogEvol/CogEvol-4B`](https://github.com/CogEvol/CogEvol-4B) (MIT code, `48★`), [`THU-MAIC/dsh-openmaic`](https://github.com/THU-MAIC/dsh-openmaic) (MIT, `118★`, first-party to the `42,969★` OpenMAIC runtime) and [`SimonsTang/feifei-companion`](https://github.com/SimonsTang/feifei-companion) (Apache-2.0, `112★`, K-12); plus [`baidu-baige/LoongSage`](https://github.com/baidu-baige/LoongSage) (Apache-2.0, `599★`) on the foundations shelf. 🔵 **`CogEvol-4B` is the direct answer to the data-sovereignty precondition above** — a `2.4 GB` quantized file generating complete courses with **no network at inference**, which satisfies sovereignty by construction rather than by contract.

🔴 **Gap, stated: this region's legs returned regulation and NOTHING ELSE.** No APAC classroom adoption rates, no education-vendor market map, no spend figures. 🔴 **So APAC is the inverse of LATAM on this page: LATAM is well measured and thinly regulated, APAC is heavily regulated and unmeasured** — and a pass that wants APAC *demand* numbers must find a channel other than these eight legs, because they have now failed to produce them repeatedly.


#### 🟢 p704 — APAC: the region supplies this base's agents, and `p704` adds a SUB-NATIONAL instrument, which is the shape engagements actually land on

🟢 **APAC remains the region that produces supply rather than only demand, and `p704` is more evidence:** `3` of the `8` agent rows filed this pass are APAC-placed on the artefact — [`KeWang0622/kaogong-skill`](https://github.com/KeWang0622/kaogong-skill) (`228★`, Chinese public-service exam syllabus), [`wenflow-org/wenflow`](https://github.com/wenflow-org/wenflow) (`64★`), [`Lee985-cmd/AI-30-Day-Challenge`](https://github.com/Lee985-cmd/AI-30-Day-Challenge) (`57★`). The shelf now stands at **`APAC 46`** of `166`, second only to `Global`.

🟢 **Novel at `p704`: the Odisha AI Policy 2025 (India, state-level)** — a decade-long roadmap placing AI learning inside a state education system. 🔵 **Why a sub-national instrument matters more than the national one here:** India's national layer (CBSE's AI curriculum from April 2026, AI and computational thinking from **Grade 3** in the 2026–27 academic year under NEP 2020) sets direction, but **procurement and rollout sit with states**. Punjab is reported as the first state to deploy an AI curriculum in government schools; Odisha has published a ten-year roadmap. **A state is a buyer with a budget; a national framework is not.** This page has carried the national layer for several passes and had **no** sub-national instrument until now.

🟡 **Held, not new:** China's Ministry of Education guidelines for primary and secondary AI education (including the instruction not to paste AI output into homework, and generative use confined to creative tasks under teacher guidance), AI compulsory from age six, Singapore's teacher-training and tiered curriculum-certification approach, Forrester's APAC enterprise-investment lead, the Acer Edu Summit Asia Pacific 2026, and the region's **AI-sovereignty** theme — all already on this page.

🔵 **The sovereignty theme and the supply pattern reinforce each other, which is the regional argument.** APAC buyers are reported to be converging on shared principles (safety, transparency, accountability) implemented **per market**, and simultaneously this is the region whose open-source education artefacts are **jurisdiction-bound by construction** — `kaogong-skill` encodes a Chinese national examination standard; it is not portable and is not meant to be. **A per-market build on a permissive local artefact is the native shape here**, and it is why `P94`/`P95` are specified to swap their content layer rather than their runtime.
