None of the four artefacts above would be returned by an English keyword leg at any star floor, because the README, the UI and the docs are not in English. **The discriminator that works is the `topic:` head read in the primary language.**

**Plus a named-institution estate.** [`michael-borck`](https://github.com/michael-borck)'s `243` repositories are one **Curtin University** (Australia) lecturer's teaching output — `ISYS2001`/`ISYS5002` name the institution in-repo — and yielded `4` rows this pass including [`assessment-bench`](https://github.com/michael-borck/assessment-bench), the instrument this base should have had before trusting any LLM-marking claim. 🔴 **This base already held one repository from that owner (`study-buddy`, `23★`) and had never queried the other `242`** (`T150`).

**Demand side, with its limits stated.** Forrester reports APAC leading enterprise AI adoption (63% within IT operations, 46% in data management); **AI sovereignty** is reported as the pacing theme for the region in 2026; Australia's National AI Plan and new Chief AI Officers are reshaping public-sector adoption; Singapore's consultations on AI in financial institutions set the transparency and accountability template other sectors follow. 🟡 **Measured gap: the leg returned NO concrete 2026 APAC education-specific regulation and no named education-sector market players.** That is an informed nil, not coverage — the next step is national education ministries directly (Singapore MOE, Korea, Japan MEXT, Australia's Department of Education), which p703 did not do.

**Opportunities.** (1) 🟢 **Read the primary language and compose what is already built** — the mechanisms above are better than anything this base would design, and they are MIT/Apache-2.0. (2) 🟢 **Sovereignty is the region's stated theme and these artefacts are sovereign by construction**: `chemeq-grader` runs on a teacher's Windows machine over the LAN, `fairhand` keeps the name roster on the PC, `ml-playgrounds` stores work in the student's own browser. That is a deployment story a ministry can accept without a data-residency negotiation. (3) **The university teaching estate is a partnership channel, not just a source** — a lecturer maintaining `243` teaching repositories is a collaborator.

#### 🔵 p702 — APAC: **the region leads this base's SUPPLY and trails its DEMAND intelligence**, and the asymmetry is this pass's regional finding

🔴 **The mandated leg (`AI education APAC 2026 adoption regulation players`) returned almost nothing education-specific, and this is written down rather than left silent.** What came back was enterprise AI: an APAC tech-priorities survey of `1,100+` decision-makers across Australia, New Zealand, Singapore, Malaysia and the Philippines (**enterprises, not schools**), Singapore's consultations on AI in **financial institutions**, and general data-protection and cross-border-transfer refinement. Explicitly absent, in the leg's own words: **no named education ministries, no national AI-in-schools policies, and no major APAC edtech players.** The only education-specific item was the **Acer Edu Summit Asia Pacific 2026**, a vendor-convened event. An informed gap: the demand picture for APAC education here is thin, and a country-level sweep (China, India, South Korea, Japan, Australia) is the unspent query.

🟢 **Supply is the opposite story. APAC is now this base's largest *placed* region on the agent shelf — `36` of `144` rows — and gained two more this pass, both on measured evidence rather than inference:**

| Row filed at p702 | Placement evidence, measured | What it contributes |
|---|---|---|
| [`XiucuiGuan/shumo-expert`](https://github.com/XiucuiGuan/shumo-expert) — MIT, `1,079 B` · `9069b2242529`, `26★` | primary README **`76.3%` CJK share of prose letters** (`p684` class) | **The only specified-refusal taxonomy on this shelf**: Bloom L1–L8 laddering, a four-stage P-M-A-D modelling walk, a 10-class probing-script library, and **`6` documented fallback classes for when the learner resists being guided** — the moment every tutor pilot actually dies. Composed into `P89` |
| [`ook370641-arch/study-parlor`](https://github.com/ook370641-arch/study-parlor) — MIT, `1,082 B` · `34af2afc64c8`, `6★` | primary README **`48.7%` CJK share** with a secondary English section | Local-first study environment that is explicitly *not a chatbot*: Socratic probing plus **automatic Markdown archiving**, so sessions accumulate into a searchable vault. `252` commits in six weeks |

🔵 **The asymmetry, stated so it is actionable.** For APAC this base holds **strong, verifiable supply and weak, second-hand demand** — the inverse of LATAM, where the institutional demand signal is well sourced and the supply shelf is thin. The cause is channel, not reality: `topic:` facets and prose-share measurement reach published APAC code directly, while the prose demand leg reaches APAC education policy only through English-language enterprise trade press. **An APAC engagement should therefore lead with assets and ask the client for the policy context**, which is the reverse of the LATAM approach below.

🔵 **Cross-sector regulatory direction, since it is the best available proxy.** Commentators expect APAC to **converge on common principles — safety, transparency, accountability — executed differently per market**, with **AI sovereignty** named as the pacing factor for 2026 and Forrester describing a shift from headline pilots to *sovereign-by-design* execution. For education that implies the same architecture `P88` assumes for compliance reasons: in-country or in-tenant hosting, swappable models, auditable prompts.

**Opportunity for Globant.** Sovereignty-first deployment is the regional wedge: a self-hosted, model-swappable education platform satisfies both the sovereignty expectation and the absent-policy problem, because it keeps the institution's options open while the rules settle. `P89` is the ready-made product for the region's strongest supply, and the `shumo-expert` taxonomies **transfer but need translating** — their structure is domain-independent, their trigger words and worked examples are not.


#### 🟢 p701 — APAC: three statutes now name education as high-risk, and the region supplied the **majority** of this pass's verified rows

🟢 **Regulation, read this pass — APAC is no longer the "voluntary" region:**

| Jurisdiction | Instrument | Status / date |
|---|---|---|
| **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act** | **In force `2026-01-22`**; enforcement decrees still being finalised, with a guidance-first grace period through 2026 |
| **Vietnam** | **Law 134/2025/QH15** (risk-based AI framework) | **Effective `2026-03-01`** |
| **Vietnam** | **Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg** | Names high-risk AI in six sectors **including education** (automated assessment, behavioural systems) |
| **Australia** | **TEQSA** | Requires **all** higher-education providers to file institutional action plans addressing generative-AI risk |
| **Singapore** | MAS AI Risk Management Guidelines | In consultation; covers third-party AI providers |
| **Indonesia** | Presidential Regulation on the national AI roadmap | Being finalised |
| **China** | Most comprehensive regional framework | In force |
| **Japan** | Light-touch voluntary governance | Unchanged |

🔴 **Vietnam is the one to brief a delivery lead on.** It is the clearest case in the region of a statute **naming education explicitly as high-risk**, with automated assessment called out, and its effective date (`2026-03-01`) has **already passed**. 🔵 **EU spillover compounds this:** an APAC ed-tech vendor selling proctoring or admissions tooling into EU schools is caught by Annex III regardless of local law — so the binding constraint for an APAC product with export ambition is **whichever of the two regimes is stricter**, and that is currently the EU's.

🟢 **Supply: APAC dominated this pass, and every placement came from content or grant payload — never an account field.** [`woolimi/robotics-roadmap`](https://github.com/woolimi/robotics-roadmap) (`95★`, Korean robotics self-study curriculum — 🔴 **but see `T135`: MIT shell, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 lessons**); [`TTNAN/yinshi`](https://github.com/TTNAN/yinshi) (`72★`, classical-poetry reader with pinyin **corrected to China's 统编 unified textbook readings**); [`zhuangzhaoyeye/llm_course`](https://github.com/zhuangzhaoyeye/llm_course) (`41★`, MIT first-principles LLM curriculum); [`cyberdaddyleo/workstation-subject-teacher`](https://github.com/cyberdaddyleo/workstation-subject-teacher) (`33★`, 🔴 **custom non-commercial — do not ship**); [`Famalhaut04/canvas-weekly-hub`](https://github.com/Famalhaut04/canvas-weekly-hub) (`18★`, CityU Hong Kong); [`JERRIYHAN/tongji-online`](https://github.com/JERRIYHAN/tongji-online) (`12★`, Tongji); [`Mikecode985/code-tutor-desktop`](https://github.com/Mikecode985/code-tutor-desktop) (MIT, targets **计算机二级**, China's National Computer Rank Examination Level 2); [`euron-sde-team/euron-open-lms-mobile-application`](https://github.com/euron-sde-team/euron-open-lms-mobile-application) (MIT, **placed APAC on the grant payload** — the copyright holder is constituted as a *Private Limited* — offline-video native classroom).
🔵 **Opportunity, and it is a licence-diligence opportunity as much as a build one.** APAC has the deepest permissive supply on these shelves **and** the two worst licence traps found this pass (`T135`, `T139`), both of which read as *"unknown licence"* to an automated gate and both of which are written in a language an English-language review may not resolve. A studio that does payload-level, translated licence diligence can use material competitors will either misuse or wrongly discard. 🟢 **Curriculum alignment is the product moat**: `yinshi` and `code-tutor-desktop` are valuable because they target a *named national instrument*, which is the hardest thing for a generic tool to retrofit.

#### 🟢 p699 — APAC: the region supplies the pass's entire deliverable, and the engagement shape here is **a state board operating national DPI**, not an institution

🟢 **Supply — the largest and most permissive public-sector education estate this base has measured.** India's **Sunbird**, enumerated this pass across six GitHub organisations: **`212` repositories, `196` permissive (`185` MIT + `11` Apache-2.0) = `92.5%`**. Six building blocks filed to [`../repos/foundations.md`](../repos/foundations.md) (content/taxonomy, identity/tenancy, devops, assessment authoring, assessment delivery, plus Singapore's accessibility scanner). Full org-by-org table in [`../verticals/solutions.md`](../verticals/solutions.md) `p699`; the licence-density comparison against Europe is `T126`.

🟢 **Demand — India, and it is a dated national mandate rather than a forecast.** From the **`2026–27`** academic session, **AI and computational thinking become compulsory from Class 3** in all schools, framed as a basic universal skill rather than an elective. Anchored on **NEP 2020** and **NCF-SE 2023**, delivered through a **NCERT–CBSE coordination committee** with KVS, NVS and the state/UT boards; **NCERT has constituted a textbook development team for Grades 11–12**, where AI stays optional; **CIET-NCERT** ran a *"Leveraging AI for Transforming School Education"* educator training series in **January 2026**. The delivery platform, **DIKSHA**, runs in **all `36` states and union territories in `30+` languages** — **and it is MIT-licensed Sunbird.** *The mandate and the open-source platform it lands on are one engagement.*

🔴 **The counter-reading, and it is from Indian sector analysis rather than from a vendor.** A 2026 state-of-the-sector report describes the year as **two stories — a fast top-down policy and curriculum push, and a much slower rollout on the ground** — and a companion analysis argues the policy is *"well-intentioned but premature at scale"* because foundational layers are missing in most of the country's **`1.471` million** schools. **For a studio this is the scope, not the objection:** the distance between a national mandate and 1.471 million schools is integration, teacher enablement and offline delivery work. A proposal that cites the mandate without citing the rollout gap will read as not having done the homework.

🟢 **Demand — Singapore, and this is the region's governance template.** MOE's **Student Learning Space (SLS)** has carried **Learning Feedback Assistants** since **December 2023**: a *Language Feedback Assistant* for English writing mechanics and a *Short Answer Feedback Assistant* that suggests grades and generates content feedback, with a mathematics variant offering hints and suggested marks. **The guardrails are the part worth copying:** a September 2026 MOE reply states the Learning Assistant requires **teacher activation** and lets a teacher **cap the number of interactions**, and teachers review or adjust automated feedback before it reaches a student. On the workforce side, MOE's **Teacher AI Literacy Professional Learning Roadmap** (**August 2026**) sets **one compulsory AI module by end-2026** and two more strongly recommended by end-2027; pupils use education-specific AI tools from **Primary 4** under supervision, which the education minister has described as deliberately low-exposure. MOE also states it is still investigating these tools' effect on learning.

🔵 **The opportunity, stated as a difference from this base's other regions.** Every North America and EMEA pattern here sells to an **institution** (a district, a university, an *académie*, a canton). APAC's two anchor countries both buy at **national-platform** level: the unit is a state board deploying shared DPI, or a ministry extending one national learner surface. That changes the deliverable from *integrate with their LMS* to **extend their platform without forking it**, and it is the first region where this base holds the platform's own source under a permissive grant. Wired concretely as [`../compose/patterns.md`](../compose/patterns.md) **`P82`**.

🔴 **Declared gaps, so silence is not mistaken for coverage.** (1) The mandated `AI education APAC 2026 adoption regulation players` leg returned **no education-specific APAC players or regulators at all** — it produced enterprise-IT material (a Forrester reading that APAC leads AI deployment at `63%` in IT operations) and one vendor summit. **The two findings above came from country-specific legs, not from the regional leg**; the regional token is the wrong instrument for this region and that is now measured twice. (2) **No APAC authority estate outside India and Singapore paid out:** `AusDTO` (`73` repos, `28` MIT) is horizontal digital-government, `digitalnz` is `11/17` GPL-3.0, and `education-services-australia`, `NZQA`, `KERIS-kr`, `mext-jp`, `nie-sg` and `data-gov-sg` **do not exist as GitHub logins** (`422`, `T129`). Japan and Korea remain unmeasured on the supply side.

#### 🔵 p698 — APAC: the supply leg nils, and the region's new fact is **public opposition**, which no other region on this page has measured

🔴 **Supply: nil.** No APAC education repository novel to this base was returned by any leg this pass. APAC remains the largest non-`Global` bloc on the agents shelf (`30 / 106`), and it gained nothing this window.

🟢 **Demand: a datapoint class this page has never held — how much the public wants AI *banned* from schools.** From the **Ipsos Education Monitor 2026**:

| Jurisdiction | Support for banning AI in schools | Movement |
|---|--:|---|
| Indonesia | `23 %` | lowest in the region |
| **Japan** | **`29 %`** | 🔴 **up from `21 %`** |
| **South Korea** | **`31 %`** | highest measured |

🔵 **Why this is worth more than an adoption percentage.** Every other demand figure on this page measures *uptake*, which rises monotonically and therefore says little about risk. **This measures resistance, and in Japan it rose `8` points in one cycle.** For an APAC engagement the practical reading is that the deployment risk is **reputational and parental** before it is regulatory — a pilot can be technically successful and still be withdrawn. **Budget for a parent- and teacher-facing transparency surface as a deliverable, not as communications.**

**Regulatory picture — held, with the dates restated:** South Korea's **AI Basic Act** in force **`2026-01-22`** (the region's first comprehensive AI statute); **Vietnam's Law on Artificial Intelligence** in force **`2026-03-01`**, with **longer transition periods for education** alongside health care and finance. China regulates through targeted measures (recommendation algorithms, deep synthesis, generative AI services, labelling of AI-generated content) rather than a single statute. Australia and Japan rely on existing law plus voluntary guidance; Singapore on **AI Verify**. UNESCO notes China, Japan, Korea and Singapore already integrating AI curricula while others lag on readiness.

🔴 **Recorded as unreliable, deliberately:** the APAC market figures returned this pass (`≈ $2.85B` at `35.3 %` CAGR; a `44.2 %` CAGR; *"95 %+ of Chinese secondary schools"*) trace to unnamed sources in commercial reports. **They are not carried as facts.** The `$2.85B` figure appears on this page already and its provenance is no better now than when it was filed.

| **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act**, in force **22 January 2026** | MSIT has signalled `2026` as an effective **pilot period with limited enforcement** — enforcement decrees still being finalised, grace focused on guidance rather than penalties. 🔵 A window: compliance work is cheap to pilot now and expensive to retrofit in 2027 |
| **Vietnam** | **Decision No. 33/2026/QD-TTg** | Names **education** among six high-risk sectors, explicitly including **automated assessment** and **behavioural monitoring** — the two use cases this base's assessment and proctoring shelves cover. `P70` already prices this |
| **Australia** | **TEQSA** | Requires **all** higher-education providers to submit **institutional action plans** addressing generative-AI risk. A mandatory per-institution artefact, which is a per-institution engagement |

#### 🟢 p696 — APAC: the demand leg nils a 10th time, and the region supplies the pass's regulator-citable evaluation harness plus its most capable agent surface

🔴 **The leg, measured:** `8` checkable facts (South Korea's **AI Basic Act** in force `22 Jan 2026` with a guidance-first grace period through 2026; **Vietnam Decree `33/2026/QD-TTg`** naming education — automated assessment and behavioural monitoring — among six high-risk sectors; Australia's **TEQSA** requiring institutional genAI action plans from all higher-education providers; Japan light-touch/voluntary; Australia, NZ, Malaysia sectoral; the EU AI Act's spillover onto APAC vendors selling into EU schools). **`8 / 8` already held**, several at clause level. Nil.

🟢 **Rows gained:** [`aiverify-foundation/moonshot`](https://github.com/aiverify-foundation/moonshot) (**Apache-2.0**, `361★`, Singapore AI Verify Foundation — benchmarking **and** red-teaming, **implementing IMDA's Starter Kit**), [`psychquant/che-ntucool-mcp`](https://github.com/psychquant/che-ntucool-mcp) (**MIT**, NTU COOL, National Taiwan University), [`krayin/laravel-crm`](https://github.com/krayin/laravel-crm) (**MIT**, `24,122★`, Webkul / India) and [`frappe/frappe`](https://github.com/frappe/frappe) (**MIT**, `11,065★`, India).

🔵 **`moonshot` is the regionally correct answer to the same problem `inspect_ai` solves in EMEA, and the difference matters commercially.** Both are permissive, state-backed evaluation harnesses. **`moonshot` implements a named APAC regulator's own starter kit (IMDA's)**, so in Singapore — and by reference across ASEAN procurement — it is the instrument the regulator already recognises. A studio serving both regions should carry **both** and choose by jurisdiction, not by preference.

🔵 **`che-ntucool-mcp` is the region's methodological contribution, and it outruns the regulation.** Its write path — grades and announcements — sits behind a human confirmation the server asks for **itself**, with the published reasoning that *“students are notified, and a notification cannot be un-sent”*, so there is no batch size below which the gate is skipped. That is **Annex III-grade human oversight, implemented by a one-star Taiwanese project**, and it is a better pattern than most funded products ship. 🔵 **India is this region's permissive platform engine:** both MIT platform rows gained this pass are Indian-authored, which is where a cost-sensitive APAC education build should start.

🔵 **The region's exposure remains a licence problem, and `frappe/frappe` sharpens it:** the framework and `frappe/education` are **MIT**, but **ERPNext is GPL-3.0** — the single easiest way for an APAC education build to acquire copyleft by accident.

#### 🔵 p691 — APAC: the leg nilled, and Vietnam's education clauses now have a third independent reading at clause level

🔴 **Leg run** (`AI education APAC 2026 adoption regulation players`). **`7` instruments returned, `7` already held, `0` novel.** Held: **South Korea's AI Basic Act in force `2026-01-22`**, enforcement decrees still being finalised with a guidance-first grace period through 2026; **China** as the region's most comprehensive regime (mandatory registration, content-labelling duties); **Japan's** light-touch voluntary governance; **Australia's TEQSA** requiring *all* higher-education providers to file institutional action plans on generative-AI risk; **Singapore's MAS** consultation on AI Risk Management Guidelines plus `AI Verify`; and Malaysia/Australia/New Zealand embedding *"mandatory guardrails"* sectorally rather than by standalone statute.

🟢 **Vietnam re-read at clause level, and the commercially decisive detail is the qualifier rather than the listing.** The statute is **Law `134/2025/QH15`** (in force **`2026-03-01`**) and the list is **Decision `33/2026/QD-TTg`** — `46` high-risk systems across `6` sectors. Education's three entries: **(1)** systems generating **self-learning content from uncontrolled data sources**; **(2)** **automated examination, assessment of learning outcomes, or ranking of learners**; **(3)** **biometric learner monitoring**, facial recognition and eye tracking named. 🔵 **Entry (2) is conditional, and the condition is the whole engagement question:** it bites only where the system's output is *"used as the official basis for assessing learning outcomes, evaluating competencies, or ranking learners"* **and** the use sits **inside the national education system**. A formative tutor that never issues the record of attainment is outside it; the same model wired to the gradebook of record is inside it. **Transition:** existing systems in **education, healthcare and finance** have until **`2027-09-01`**; all other existing high-risk systems until **`2027-03-01`**.

🟡 **Source disagreement recorded rather than resolved.** One outlet calls the instrument *"Decree 33"* and dates it `30 June`; the Vietnamese-law text calls it a **Prime Minister Decision**, and the majority of sources date its effect **`2026-08-15`**. The instrument number and the education clauses agree across all of them; only the form and the effective date diverge. **`0` novel facts** — the only string in this leg absent from the tree was the phrase *"biometric learner"*, which is wording, not a fact.


#### 🟢 p690 — APAC: **`Gap 304` is resolved** — Vietnam's statute confirmed on a second independent channel, with a new education compliance date

🟢 **This is the only region that gained a fact this pass, and it closes a named gap rather than opening an address.** `Gap 304` recorded Vietnam's AI statute as carried from a *secondary* channel with a live source conflict: one channel called the instrument a *Digital Technology Industry Law* effective 2026, and the statute text was unreachable. 🟢 **Both halves are now settled, and the base's reading was the correct one:**

- **Law on Artificial Intelligence No. 134/2025/QH15** — passed **10 December 2025** (`429` votes to `5`), **in force 1 March 2026**. 🟢 **The dates this base already held are confirmed**, now from two further independent channels (Allen & Gledhill's Vietnam practice; the English statute text at luatvietnam).
- 🔵 **The conflict is resolved by separation, not by correction:** the *Digital Technology Industry Law* is a **different instrument**. The conflated reading can be retired.
- 🆕 **New structural detail this base did not hold:** `8` chapters, **`35` articles**, a **three-tier** risk regime — described as the first comprehensive standalone AI statute in Southeast Asia.
- 🆕 **New, and this is the commercially load-bearing one — Article 34's transition periods.** Systems already running before 1 March 2026 get **18 months** where the sector is **healthcare, education or finance**, and `12` months elsewhere. 🔴 **So Vietnam's education compliance date is `1 September 2027`** — *three months before* the EU's `2 December 2027` Annex III date.
- 🟢 **The education limb is confirmed in the statute's own words:** education AI must suit learners' age and development, **prevent risks in the assessment and classification of learners**, and protect data and privacy.
- 🔴 **What is still NOT primary-verified, and should stay flagged:** the high-risk list itself, **Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg**, reported as `46` high-risk systems across `6` sectors but carrying **contradictory effective dates across channels** (`15 August 2026` vs `1 January 2026`), and implementing **Decree 142/2026/ND-CP**, reported effective `1 May 2026`. 🔵 **`Gap 304` should be narrowed to the Decision and the Decree rather than closed.**

🔵 **Why the `1 September 2027` date changes the regional pitch.** This base has been selling the EU Annex III date as the forcing function and treating APAC as the region whose statutes *name* education most explicitly. **Vietnam now supplies both: an explicit education limb and an earlier deadline.** A multinational with APAC education operations has its first hard date in Vietnam, not Europe — and `P67` (auditable agent tutoring for an Annex III deployer) is the same deliverable with the date changed.

🔴 **The rest of the APAC leg nilled.** Held: South Korea's **AI Basic Act** in force **22 January 2026** with a guidance-first grace period through 2026 and extraterritorial reach; China's mandatory registration and content-labelling regime with penalties to **¥50 M**; Japan's light-touch voluntary model; Singapore's **AI Verify**; Australia's **TEQSA** requirement that *all* higher-education providers file institutional action plans on generative-AI risk. 🟢 **TEQSA remains the only education-specific *regulator* obligation this base has found in any region.**

#### 🆕 p688 — China and India both now mandate AI *curriculum*, which is not the same thing as regulating AI

🟢 **China — the `AI + Education` Action Plan.** The **Ministry of Education with four other central departments** launched an **`AI + Education` action plan in mid-April 2026**, targeting **full curriculum integration by 2030**. It sits on top of May 2025 guidelines that put AI lessons from **age six**, and secondary reporting adds that the ministry **bars primary-school pupils from using generative AI independently** — a restriction read only in secondary sources and not reconciled to the ministry's own text, so quote it as reported rather than as rule.

🟢 **India — compulsory from Class 3.** The Ministry of Education has made **AI and Computational Thinking compulsory from Class 3, beginning with the `2026-27` academic year**. 🔴 **The binding constraint is named in the same sources and it is the opportunity:** the rollout requires training **over 1 crore (10M+) school teachers**. That is a teacher-enablement programme at a scale no vendor currently serves, and it is the single largest education-AI delivery number on this page.

🔧 **This narrows — but does not close — the gap this page has carried as *"no education-specific AI rules surfaced for China, India or Japan"*. The distinction has to be kept, because it changes what a client is buying:**

| instrument type | China | India | What it creates demand for |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Curriculum mandate** (what p688 found) | 🟢 `AI + Education` plan, 2026→2030 | 🟢 AI + Computational Thinking, Class 3+, AY `2026-27` | Content, teacher training, delivery platforms, assessment of AI literacy |
| **AI-safety / high-risk rule** (still absent) | 🔴 none education-specific | 🔴 none education-specific (content-labelling rules from `2026-02-20` are horizontal) | Conformity, documentation, audit — the EMEA/Korea motion |

🔵 **So the APAC sale is a delivery sale, not a compliance sale** — the inverse of EMEA, and the opposite of what this page's EU-shaped framing would predict. South Korea's **AI Basic Act** (in force `2026-01-22`, naming student assessment inside its high-impact definition) and **Vietnam's Law on AI** (`2026-03-01`, 18-month education transition) remain the region's only education-touching binding instruments, both already on this page.

🔵 **Re-confirmed:** the **ASEAN Foundation's February 2026 readiness study** — adoption outpacing institutional guidance, with evidence-based guidance for digital governance and AI policy named as the regional need — and the **SEAMEO INNOTECH / APO** discussions toward a regional AI-in-education policy framework. 🔴 **Not asserted:** sources conflict on whether **Singapore** has a *formal* national AI curriculum (one describes supplementary programmes and teacher training rather than a subject); this base will not claim one until a Singapore MOE source is read.

##### 🆕 p686 — Three statutes in force or imminent, and the only education-SPECIFIC regulator obligation found anywhere this pass

🟢 **Australia is where the named, education-specific obligation is.** **TEQSA**, the higher-education regulator, has required **all** higher education providers to submit **institutional action plans** addressing generative-AI risk. That is a mandated per-institution deliverable at national scale — the most directly procurable regulatory artefact surfaced by any of this pass's four regional legs. Australia still has **no** AI-specific statute; its 2024 proposal named mandatory guardrails for high-risk AI including **education**, so the TEQSA plan is currently the whole instrument.

🟢 **Statutes, with dates:** **South Korea's AI Basic Act entered into force 22 January 2026** — transparency, risk assessment, human oversight and documentation for high-impact systems, with a **grace period through 2026** that is guidance-led rather than penalty-led, i.e. a remediation window that closes at year end. **Vietnam's AI law takes effect 1 March 2026** (risk-based; sources disagree on whether it is a stand-alone act or the Digital Technology Industry Law — **verify before citing**). **China** runs the region's most comprehensive regime: registration requirements plus **content-labelling obligations**. **Japan and Singapore** stay light-touch and voluntary (`AI Verify` toolkits); **Hong Kong** frames its agenda as responsible adoption through data-privacy and ethics governance.

🟢 **The region is also where the open supply is.** Every large permissive education repository this base holds is APAC-placed on the repositories' own evidence: **OpenMAIC** (MIT, `42,843★`), **DeepTutor** (Apache-2.0, `56,024★`) and now **word-atlas** (MIT, `4,333★`, `70.4 %` CJK prose) and **GoAgent** (MIT). **APAC is a supply region for this industry, not only a demand region** — the practical consequence is that an APAC engagement can start from a locally-built permissive base, while a LATAM or EMEA one usually starts from an imported one.

⚠️ **Export constraint, newly cheap to state:** an APAC ed-tech vendor selling into Europe inherits Annex III high-risk status, and that deadline **moved to 2 December 2027** (see EMEA). APAC vendors building to the old August 2026 date have `16` more months than they think.

🔵 **Measured gap:** the APAC leg returned regulation densely and **named no ed-tech vendors and no classroom adoption rates**. Chinese content-labelling rules bear directly on AI-generated teaching material and this base holds no detail on how they apply to education specifically. Both need vendor-level and ministry-level legs.

**Regulatory posture:** no longer merely fragmented — **APAC now has a binding, horizontal, risk-tiered AI statute that names education explicitly**, and this KB did not hold it until p651.

🔴 **South Korea — the AI Basic Act (Law No. 20676), in force 22 January 2026.** The world's second comprehensive AI statute after the EU AI Act. Its **"high-impact AI" category covers AI used for student assessment in early-childhood, elementary and secondary education** — named in the statutory definition, not inferred from a sector list. Operators must determine whether their system qualifies before deployment (and may request a determination from MSIT), and must make efforts to assess the system's impact on fundamental rights before putting it into a product or service. **MSIT has signalled a grace period of at least one year before administrative fines**, so the enforcement edge falls around **January 2027**, with the interim focused on guidance through a new integrated support centre. A promotion-focused amendment commences 21 July 2026. *(Enactment date 21 January 2025 per the official text; one secondary source says December 2024 — rely on the official text. Grace-period length is reported as "at least one year"; confirm with MSIT before relying on it contractually.)*

🔴 **Vietnam — the dated hook p651 left open is closed, and it names education in a decision, not an inference.** The **Law on Artificial Intelligence (No. 134/2025/QH15)**, passed 10 December 2025, in force **1 March 2026**, sets a three-tier risk framework. The operative instrument is **Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg**, effective **15 August 2026**, which promulgates the list of high-risk AI systems: **46 systems across six sectors, of which education accounts for three.**

The primary education entry covers AI used to **automatically run examinations or assessments, assess learning outcomes, and rank learners** — and it is scoped: it applies **only where the results serve as the official basis** for assessing learning outcomes or ranking learners within the national education system. That scoping clause is the commercially important part. A formative or practice tool falls outside it; the same model wired into the system of record falls inside.

**Transition:** systems already operating before 15 August 2026 have until **1 September 2027** to comply in education, healthcare and finance (other listed sectors: 1 March 2027).

*(Law and decision numbers, dates and the education entry's wording are from law-firm and compliance summaries plus a reproduction of the decision text; the primary Vietnamese text was not read at first hand. Two further entries reported by one commentary — systems that monitor and analyse learner behaviour, and self-study tools generating content from uncontrolled sources — **could not be confirmed against the decision text and are not published here as fact.**)*

**Singapore** relies on governance toolkits rather than a statute: new AI governance guidance covering **generative and agentic** AI, with MAS consulting on AI Risk Management Guidelines. No education-specific rule.

🔴 **A claim about Australia was tested this pass and does not verify.** A widely-repeated summary states that **TEQSA requires all higher education providers to submit institutional action plans addressing generative-AI risks** — which would have been the most education-specific regulatory action in the region. **No such submission mandate was found.** What exists is guidance and a standards hook: TEQSA published *"Assuring quality learning in a gen AI-integrated future"* on **24 June 2026**, maintains a Gen AI Knowledge Hub for providers, staff and students, and frames academic-integrity risk through **Threshold Standard 5.2** rather than a new instrument; prior key documents are a 2024 report on gen AI strategies and a 2025 guide to enacting assessment reform. Australia's posture is therefore unchanged from what this page already says — existing law plus sector guidance — and the contradicting claim is recorded as rejected rather than quietly dropped.

🟢 **Two genuinely procurement-grade Australian assets surfaced while testing that claim**, and they are the kind of thing this page has been declaring absent in APAC: a **Zenodo dataset (October 2026) recording the public student generative-AI disclosure rules of all 220 providers on the TEQSA National Register**, and an **AJET study (July 2026) analysing how 39 universities align with TEQSA's gen-AI principles.** A census of every provider's published rule is exactly the artefact that lets an engagement open with the client's own position already mapped.

**China** regulates by service — Generative AI Regulation (2023), Deep Synthesis (2022), Recommendation Algorithm (2022); companion-service rules effective 15 July 2026; the amended Cybersecurity Law adds the first AI clause (Article 20). **India** has no dedicated AI law and governs via the DPDP Act and 2025 Rules plus voluntary MeitY guidelines; amended IT Rules on **synthetic-content labelling took effect 20 February 2026**. **Japan**'s AI Promotion Act (May 2025) is deliberately non-binding, with a government AI Basic Plan adopted by Cabinet in July 2026. **Australia and Hong Kong** apply existing law and sector guidance rather than a standalone AI act.

**Market:** APAC is the fastest-growing region — roughly **$2.85B in 2026 at a 35.3% CAGR**, about a fifth of global share (market-research secondary sources; directional). **South Korea spent roughly $740M on training teachers to use AI across 2024–2026.** Readiness is uneven: UNESCO notes China and Singapore have established AI-in-education policies while others have none, and adoption lags in rural South Asia and parts of the Pacific.

**Public sentiment (Ipsos Education Monitor 2026, support for banning AI in schools):** South Korea 31%, Japan 29%, Malaysia 29%, Singapore 28%, India 26%, Thailand 25%, Indonesia 23%. Japan rose from 21% to 29%. Australia and New Zealand record higher support for a ban than the Asian markets.

**🆕 New at p653: essentially nothing, and the leg is recorded as reproduced rather than skipped.**

The APAC search was run and returned **no reading this base did not already hold**: South Korea's AI Basic Act (in force 22 January 2026), Vietnam's binding law and its **1 September 2027** education transition, the ≈$2.85B / 35.3% CAGR market estimate, the Ipsos Education Monitor 2026 ban-support figures (South Korea 31% down to Indonesia 23%), and UNESCO's China-and-Singapore-versus-the-rest readiness split were all already sourced above. The only additions on offer were vendor-named player lists (Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson, Byju's, Squirrel AI) from market-research summaries with no primary citation, and a Microsoft announcement of expanded education commitments — **neither is recorded as a finding.**

🔵 **Stated explicitly because silence would look like coverage:** this is a *reproduction*, which is evidence the APAC picture is stable, not evidence it was re-investigated deeply. The genuine APAC gap is unchanged and is a supply gap, not an intelligence gap — APAC produces the overwhelming majority of this KB's agent supply (DeepTutor, OpenMAIC, AI-Shifu, OmniEdu) and almost none of its *regional* demand-side primary sourcing, because the statutes have not been read at first hand.

**Opportunities:**
- **This region is the open source supply side, not just a market.** Both leading permissive education agents originate here — OpenMAIC (Tsinghua, MIT, 42.7k★) and DeepTutor (HKU, Apache-2.0, 55k★). Globant can contribute to and productise these with regional credibility.
- 🟢 **South Korea is the one APAC engagement with a deadline attached, and it is the EMEA playbook in a second jurisdiction.** Student-assessment AI in K-12 is high-impact by statute; the fine window opens around January 2027. Conformity-by-design work for assessment and placement systems — the same offer that sells against the EU's 2 December 2027 date — is sellable in Korea now, against an *earlier* date. A client operating in both markets needs one conformity programme with two jurisdictional profiles, not two programmes.
- **Synthetic-content labelling (India, since 20 Feb 2026) is a concrete build.** AI-generated course material needs provenance labelling — a narrow, dated compliance requirement that is already live.
- 🟢 **Vietnam is the third jurisdiction for the conformity-by-design offer, and it has the sharpest scoping line.** The high-risk trigger is "results serve as the official basis" for assessment or ranking. That makes the architecture decision itself a billable deliverable: keeping an agent demonstrably on the formative side of the system of record is a design and evidence exercise, and the alternative — conformity work on a system of record — is a much larger programme with a **1 September 2027** deadline for anything already running. Clients with existing deployments in Vietnamese education are already inside the transition window.
- 🔵 **Lead Australian conversations with the census, not with a rule.** There is no submission mandate to comply with, so the compliance framing does not land. The 220-provider disclosure-rule dataset and the 39-university alignment study do: they let a proposal state where the client sits relative to every peer before any discovery work is done.
- **Rising public resistance is a positioning signal.** With ban-support climbing in Japan and high in ANZ, transparent, teacher-in-the-loop designs sell better than autonomous ones. Lead with OpenMAIC's visible AI-classmate framing rather than invisible automation.
- 🟢 **p655 — the ban-support numbers are now specific enough to brief a sales team with.** Ipsos Education Monitor 2026: support for **banning AI in schools runs from 23% (Indonesia) to 31% (South Korea)**, with Australia and New Zealand higher still, and **rising** in Japan and Australia versus the prior wave. Read it as a *market segmentation*, not a headwind: roughly a quarter to a third of the public in the region's most-developed education markets wants AI out of classrooms, which prices teacher-augmentation framing above learner-facing autonomy everywhere in APAC.
- 🟢 **p655 — the regional market figure and its concentration.** APAC is the fastest-growing region for AI in education at **≈$2.85B (2026)**, projected ≈$9.7B by 2030, with **China, India and Japan holding most of it** and heavy Chinese state backing. Commercial incumbents named in the regional reports are Google, Microsoft, IBM, Pearson and Byju's. ⚠️ Secondary market-research sourcing; treat as an order of magnitude.
- 🟢 **p655 — South Korea's teacher-training spend is the clearest public-money signal in the region.** ≈**$740M committed across 2024–2026 specifically to train teachers on AI tools**, alongside the **AI Basic Act in force since 22 January 2026**. Statute plus a funded training budget is the combination that turns AI literacy from a theme into a procurement line. **Vietnam's Law on Artificial Intelligence (in force 1 March 2026) gives education an 18-month transition** — the longest of any sector in that statute, which is a scoping gift: conformity work can be sold as scheduled rather than urgent.
- 🟡 **p655 — readiness inside APAC is the widest of any region, and that is the account-planning fact.** UNESCO's regional review places **China and Singapore with established AI-in-education policy** while other states in the same region are still working to meet basic education needs. Treat APAC as at least three markets: policy-mature (China, Singapore, Korea, Japan), scale-constrained (India, Indonesia, Vietnam), and infrastructure-first (Pacific, parts of South and Southeast Asia) — where the offline-first chain in `compose/patterns.md` **P7** is the only credible design.
- 🟢 **p655 — APAC still owns the open source supply side, and it widened.** The two highest-adoption education agents on this KB's shelf are both APAC academic (**DeepTutor**, HKUDS/Hong Kong, 55.0k★; **OpenMAIC**, Tsinghua, 42.7k★), and p655 adds **`Miaotofu01/Study-Mate` (MIT, 1,338★)** — the largest permissive *non-English* education agent found to date. For APAC engagements that previously had no permissive non-English base, there is now one.


##### 🟢 New at p656 — Korea's high-impact assessment scope has a permissive, auditable, on-premise answer

🟢 **The one education-specific binding rule in the region now maps to named components.** South Korea's AI Basic Act (in force 22 January 2026) places **student assessment** in early-childhood, elementary and secondary education inside its high-impact definition. That is an auditability requirement on the assessment path specifically — and p656 shelved a permissive, on-premise stack for exactly that path: [`numbas/Numbas`](https://github.com/numbas/Numbas) (Apache-2.0) delivers and marks with deterministic, inspectable rules rather than LLM scoring; [`yetanalytics/lrsql`](https://github.com/yetanalytics/lrsql) (Apache-2.0) records every attempt in the institution's own PostgreSQL; [`tsugiproject/tsugi`](https://github.com/tsugiproject/tsugi) (Apache-2.0) launches it from whatever LMS the school runs. 🔵 **Deterministic marking plus a full statement log is a far easier compliance story under a high-impact designation than an LLM grader with a rubric prompt**, and it composes with **P9**'s on-premise weights for the non-graded tutoring path.

🟢 **LTI matters more in APAC than elsewhere because the LMS estate is heterogeneous.** The region has no single dominant platform the way LATAM has Moodle or US higher education has Canvas, so a per-platform integration strategy multiplies cost across markets. One LTI 1.3 tool launches inside all of them.

⚠️ **One demand-side figure added this pass, and it is secondary.** Reporting on the region cites a South Korean government investment of **approximately US$740 million across 2024–2026 to train teachers on AI tools** — a funded AI-literacy mandate of the kind **P10** is built for. 🔴 **This comes from market-research and vendor compilations, not from a Korean government source, and was not reconciled to a primary budget document.** Treat it as an indication that the mandate is funded, not as a number to quote to a client. The named-player hole on the APAC demand side, recorded in this page's declared gaps, is unchanged.

##### 🟢 New at p657 — the measurement layer is almost entirely APAC-built, and Korea's high-impact duties land during a grace period that is now half spent

**Supply, and the regional concentration is striking.** Of the thirteen permissive psychometric-measurement rows added at p657 (**T31**), **seven come from one APAC laboratory** — `EduKTM`, `EduCDM`, `EduCAT`, `EduNLP` (University of Science and Technology of China, with `EduData` from the same group) — plus [`17zuoye/pyirt`](https://github.com/17zuoye/pyirt) (MIT), written by a Chinese K-12 company against production homework data, and [`liguozheng-ac/japanese-speech-xray`](https://github.com/liguozheng-ac/japanese-speech-xray) (MIT, live). **Cognitive diagnosis and adaptive testing are an APAC research strength expressed as Apache-2.0 and MIT code**, and that is a regional asset this KB had not recorded. ⚠️ With the caveat from **T31**: almost all of it is dormant, and `catsim` (BSD-3-Clause, Brazil) is the one live component.

**Regulation.** **South Korea's AI Basic Act entered into force 22 January 2026** — transparency, risk assessment, **human oversight** and documentation duties for high-impact systems, with a **guidance-rather-than-penalties grace period running through 2026**, i.e. roughly two months left at the date of this pass. **Vietnam's risk-based AI law takes effect 1 March 2026** and education appears in its high-risk sector list (🔧 **repaired at p659:** this parenthesis read *“the sector list sits in a decision document dated July 2026 — the timeline is internally inconsistent across sources and should be checked in primary form”*. It was **already resolved higher up this same page**, in the APAC section's Vietnam block: the instrument is **`Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg`**, effective **15 August 2026**, promulgating **46 high-risk systems across six sectors, of which education accounts for three**, with a transition to **1 September 2027** for education systems already running. Two statements about one instrument, in one file, one of them a stale caveat against the other — the caveat is withdrawn). **China**: mandatory registration, strict content labelling, penalties. **Japan**: light-touch, voluntary governance. **Singapore**: frameworks and toolkits (**AI Verify**). **Australia**: no AI-specific statute, but the 2024 mandatory-guardrails proposal named education, and **TEQSA now requires every higher-education provider to submit an institutional action plan addressing generative-AI risk** — a dated, named, compliance-funded deliverable. The **EU AI Act reaches APAC vendors** selling proctoring or admissions tools into European institutions.

🔴 **The informed gap, stated rather than left silent.** Four searches produced **no reliable region-wide figures for AI adoption rates in APAC schools or universities, and no credible ranking of education-sector vendors in the region.** EMEA, North America and LATAM all have survey instruments (Gallup, the European institutional surveys, UNESCO IESALC, the Digital Education Council); APAC has regulatory analysis and almost no comparable adoption measurement in these sources. **This is a measurement gap in the public record, not an absence of activity, and it is the single largest hole in this page.** A regional engagement should expect to commission its own baseline — which is itself billable, and which no competitor can quote from a report either.

🟢 **The opportunity.** Korea's high-impact scope covers **student evaluation**, and the permissive answer is already APAC-built and auditable: `catsim` makes item selection an explainable published algorithm rather than a learned policy, `EduCDM` makes skill diagnosis inspectable, and both can run entirely on-premise. **TEQSA's action-plan requirement is the nearest-dated, best-specified opportunity in the region** — every Australian provider must produce one, the deadline is institutional rather than negotiable, and the deliverable is governance work on top of software the client already runs. ⚠️ Note **T11**: public support for restricting AI in schools is rising in parts of APAC, so consumer-facing autonomous tutoring is the weakest proposition here and institutional assessment infrastructure the strongest.


🟢 **New at p658 — APAC supplied the one agentic asset in the learn-to-code layer, reinforcing this page's standing read that the region is this KB's supply side.** **`TutoriaLLM/TutoriaLLM`** (MIT, 303★, pushed 2026-11-03) is the only permissive agent this base has found that watches a learner's **Blockly workspace and guides them inside it** rather than chatting beside it — placed to **Japan** on two in-tree channels (a `_jp` brand asset and `readme_ja.md`), not on description language, since the owner's location field is unset and under p647-H a zero there carries no information. It anchors **P23** (block-to-text progression, 6–8 weeks), which addresses the highest-attrition moment in any coding curriculum and for which there is essentially no commercial tooling.

🔵 **p667 — APAC's regulatory facts are saturated (`6` of `6` held at HEAD) and its supply contribution this pass is one row, but a structurally important one.** **`maimemo/SSP-MMC`** (MIT, `227★`) is a stochastic-shortest-path review scheduler that minimises total review cost to reach a target recall, trained on MaiMemo's own large-scale learner data and published as research. 🟢 **It is the first *alternative* to FSRS this base has filed.** Trend **T13** has said for several passes that FSRS is "the one permissive education algorithm proven at consumer scale" — true, and it left clients with a single answer. An APAC engagement can now be shown a **choice of objective function**: FSRS optimises predicted recall, SSP-MMC optimises cost-to-mastery, both permissive, both with published evidence. 🔴 **The declared APAC gap from prior passes stands and is restated rather than quietly dropped:** no APAC-wide figures on AI adoption in schools or universities were found this pass either. Returned Korea's AI Basic Act (in force `2026-01-22`, enforcement decrees still being finalised through a 2026 guidance-first grace period), Vietnam's Decision `33/2026/QD-TTg` naming automated assessment and behavioural analysis as high-risk education uses, Singapore's AI Verify, and TEQSA's requirement that every Australian higher-education provider file a generative-AI risk action plan — all four already held. 🔴 **The APAC adoption-denominator gap is declared again:** no APAC-wide figure for AI adoption in schools or universities was found this pass either.
