##### 🆕 p677 — the leg held in full, and the honest reading is that LATAM's constraint has not moved

🟡 **Re-served and already held, every one of them:** UNESCO **IESALC** on **`200`** higher-education institutions across **`19`** countries — **`87%`** already using AI in some capacity, **`26%`** with a formal AI strategy; the **UNESCO Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean**, launched **`2026-04-14`** in Santiago with CAF, **CENIA**, Cetic.br/NIC.br, ECLAC, **Fundación Ceibal**, Fundación Santillana, **Tecnológico de Monterrey**, ProFuturo, Universidad del Desarrollo and IRCAI; the **Digital Education Council × Tec de Monterrey** LATAM survey at **`92%`** of students and **`79%`** of faculty actively engaging with AI; **~`45%`** of LAC institutions with or developing AI guidance against **~`70%`** in Europe and North America; Universidad de los Andes' count of **`716`** AI-related regulatory instruments and proposals across **`23`** countries and territories; Google.org's **$4.6M** for AI education across the region; the LATAM AI-in-education market at **$0.67bn** for 2026.

🔵 **The `87%`-use / `26%`-strategy gap and the `45%`-vs-`70%` governance gap are the same number told twice, and they are still the most commercially useful pair on this page.** The region has the adoption and not the governance, so **the governance artefact is the product** — policy, DPIA-equivalent, model-use register, evidence trail — and it sells *ahead of* the tutor, not after it. See **P5** and **P27**.

🔴 **Declared, because silence would read as coverage: p677 found no new LATAM repository, no new Brazilian or Mexican education-specific AI regulation, and nothing that closes the `p670` gap on either country.** The LATAM leg is now four consecutive passes without a novel address. 🔵 **Latam-GPT status, restated precisely rather than optimistically:** presented `2026-02-10` by CENIA, CAF, the Chilean government, AWS and Data Observatory; training and validation reported complete; **it is explicitly not a public chatbot at launch**. Brazil joined after a Chile–Brazil memorandum and Brazil and Mexico supplied most of the training corpus. Education uses are **intended and piloted, not deployed at scale**: a planned multilingual chatbot for schools and libraries, Colombian adaptation under discussion, several Chilean municipalities testing it for dropout reduction, and **Ceibal piloting AI tutors in its computational-thinking curriculum without any source confirming those tutors run on Latam-GPT**. ⚠️ One blog reports a Mexico-City edtech running an AI tutor for half a million students on Latam-GPT — **uncorroborated, do not repeat it to a client.** 🔴 And sources still disagree on whether the first major release was February or September 2026.

🟢 **One usable supply-side note for LATAM this pass:** the newly-filed [`aureuserp/aureuserp`](https://github.com/aureuserp/aureuserp) (**MIT**, `12.2k★`) finally gives the institutional back-office half of a `Pattern D` split a permissive option, alongside `Academico` (MIT) and `gegok12` (MIT) on the student-record half — so a **fully MIT administrative stack** for a LATAM private institution is now assemblable from this base for the first time.


##### 🆕 p676 — the leg nilled a 28th time, and the region's movement is the consumable half of Brazil's national curriculum

🔴 **The mandated LATAM leg returned nothing this page did not already hold.** `4` facts scored, `4` held: the **UNESCO IESALC** 2026 study (**200** higher-education institutions across **19** countries — **87%** use AI in at least one area, **26%** have any formal framework), **Cetic.br** on Brazilian schools (**53%** of administrators use AI, **22%** have guidelines), **Ceibal** on Uruguay (**75%** of public-school teachers use these tools), and the **UNESCO Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean**, launched in **Santiago, April 2026**.

🟢 **Two addresses placed LATAM this pass.** [`bncc-dev/bncc-pacotes`](https://github.com/bncc-dev/bncc-pacotes) — MIT code + CC BY 4.0 data, an explicitly declared split grant written in Portuguese — ships `npm` and `PyPI` packages **plus an MCP server** over the `bncc-dados` dataset `p673` filed, so an agent can resolve a **BNCC** competency code without shipping the dataset. And [`webtech-network/autograder`](https://github.com/webtech-network/autograder) (Apache-2.0, canonical to the byte, `65★`, pushed today), the first permissive autograder on this base built around **Brazilian-Portuguese** submissions rather than English ones.

🔵 **Why the curriculum packages matter more than their `10★` suggests.** The `87%`/`26%` split is the whole LATAM thesis on this page: adoption is near-universal and governance is absent, so the sellable work is **the layer that makes an AI claim auditable**. A tutor that says *“aligned to the national curriculum”* and cannot name the competency code is exactly what an institution with no framework cannot defend. `bncc-pacotes` turns that claim into a resolvable identifier pinned to a specific upstream commit, with the CC BY 4.0 grant travelling with the data. 🟢 **That is a governance artefact disguised as a package**, and it is cheap to adopt.

🔵 **And `T60` opens a segment this region has in volume.** `academico-sis/academico` (MIT, `411★`) is a **permissive, multi-tenant** system of record for small and medium institutions — private schools, language schools, training centres, franchise networks. LATAM's private education sector is large, its procurement cycles are short, and `T60` says the AI layer can be built *inside* the tenant model instead of bolted in front of a GPL core. Contrast the ministry engagement, where `P43`'s permissive adapter in front of the incumbent SIS remains the right and only answer.

🔴 **Gaps, restated.** No national AI-in-education *law* surfaced for any LATAM country — the governance instruments this leg finds are UNESCO frameworks, observatories and institutional policies, and the binding external rule remains the **EU AI Act** reaching LATAM firms whose systems have EU users. And the region's measured figures still come almost entirely from **two** national sources (Cetic.br, Ceibal) plus one UNESCO study; **16 of the 19 countries in that study have no separate figure on this page.**

##### 🆕 p675 — LATAM: the leg nilled, and the region's standing gap is now the *only* one of the four this base cannot even partly source

🔴 **Leg run** (`AI education LATAM Latin America 2026 adoption regulation players Brazil Mexico`). **`8` checkable facts, `8` already at HEAD, `0` novel**: **Brazil's PL 2338** Senate-approved `2024-12-10` with the Chamber still pending; Brazil's **regulatory sandbox**, the region's first; **EBIA**'s education and training pillar; **Mexico** having no horizontal AI law, with 2026 reforms as its first binding obligations and a February 2026 bill proposing a risk-classifying regulator; the **Chapultepec Principles** presented January 2026 by SECIHTI and ATDT; Mexico's **Public AI Training Center** (INFOTEC + TecNM + 14 companies) targeting **10,000 specialists in 2026 and 25,000/year** after; Mexico's school-level computational-thinking push; and the regional characterisation of fragmented, differently-staged regulation.

🔴 **And the honest reading of that nil.** Of the four regions, LATAM is the one where the leg returns **governance and training-capacity facts but no measured classroom adoption and no education-specific statute** — and the leg explicitly reported that it *"did not find sources that quantify AI adoption in classrooms in either country."* 🔵 **This page's LATAM adoption figures therefore rest on earlier passes' sources and have not been independently re-sourced this window.** Stated plainly so it is not mistaken for coverage.

🟢 **What the p675 supply finding means here specifically, because it is not the same as in North America.** The newly-licensable **PERSUADE 2.0** corpus is **English-language, US grade 6–12 writing**. 🔴 **It does not transfer to Portuguese or Spanish writing assessment** — the construct, the rubric and the discourse elements are all English-US. 🟢 So the LATAM version of the automated-writing-evaluation opportunity is a **data-collection workstream first**: marked student scripts in the client's own language, under the client's own consent regime, with `bncc-dev/bncc-dados` (MIT) supplying the curriculum objectives to align against. 🔵 **That is a longer and more defensible engagement than the North American one, not a lesser one** — and it is the single clearest instance on this page of why a finding has to be placed before it can be sold.

🔴 **No new LATAM market fact.** The OEI/UNESCO teacher-adoption figures (**Brazil `56%`**, Chile `55%`, Colombia `53%`, Costa Rica `52%` against an **OECD average of `36%`**), the governance deficit (`53%` of school administrators using AI but only `22%` with school guidelines; `87%` of regional universities using AI but only `26%` with a formal strategy), CENIA's **`13` of `19`** LAC countries not teaching early AI adoption, the UNESCO regional observatory launched in Santiago (April 2026), and Brazil's near-half share of the HolonIQ LATAM EdTech 100: **all held.**

Flagged for one re-probe next pass, because a grant added later would make it filable immediately.

🟢 **The region's filed rows are stable.** `LabSirius/TutorIA`, `AndresGuido9820/trabajo2-agente-tutor`, `ThallesYVasconcelos/Agente-Educacional`, `GuilhermeScarpini/agente-bncc-professores` and `psiagoleal/teacher-helper` all re-read at their published values. ⚠️ **All five remain at `0 ★`** — unchanged at p674, and still the honest statement of this region's position: LATAM is this base's best-*documented* region on demand data and its weakest on *adopted* permissive code. The opportunity is unchanged and so is the reason for it.


##### 🆕 p673 — the LATAM leg is the richest of the four on data and returned nothing new; the region's gain is **four permissive addresses**, one of them the most load-bearing filed this pass

🔴 **Leg result: `23` facts returned, `23` already held, `0` novel** — the most productive leg of the eight and still a nil. UNESCO IESALC's study of **~200 higher-education institutions across 19 countries** (**`87%`** using AI in at least one area, only **`26%`** with any formal framework, **`55%`** with no AI budget, public universities at **`4%`**), Uruguay's **`75%`** of public-school teachers (Ceibal 2026), Brazil's **`65%`** of internet users aged 9–17 (Cetic.br 2026) and **`53%`** of school administrators using AI against **`22%`** whose schools have guidelines, the **Observatory on AI in Education for LAC launched `2026-04-14`** at ECLAC in Santiago, CENIA, and the Tecnológico de Monterrey–UNESCO agreement of March 2026 — **all carried.** ⚠️ Several were held only in `%` form and scored `0` in `per cent` form: `667-G2`/`667-B` again, and the reason no absence is claimed from a single needle.

🟢 **Four addresses placed, and the first of them is the most consequential row filed this pass:**

- **[`Cvmcosta/ltijs`](https://github.com/Cvmcosta/ltijs)** (Apache-2.0, **373★**, pushed `2026-11-05`) — a **fully conformant LTI 1.3 / Advantage tool provider**, and therefore the component every agent on this base needs in order to run *inside* an institution's LMS rather than beside it. Placed on the strongest channel: the README credits the **Federal University of Maranhão** and **UNA-SUS/UFMA**, with `ufma.br` domains. 🟢 **The single most reused piece of education interop software this base holds was built at a Brazilian federal university** — which refutes, on the strongest evidence class available, the recurring reading that LATAM supplies adoption and imports software.
- **[`bncc-dev/bncc-dados`](https://github.com/bncc-dev/bncc-dados)** (MIT) — Brazil's **BNCC national curriculum as verified open data**: `1,721` learning objectives in JSON/SQLite/CSV with per-record provenance to MEC/CNE.
- **[`cortega26/chile-hub`](https://github.com/cortega26/chile-hub)** (MIT, `137★`, pushed `2026-11-03`) — curated, normalised, validated Chilean public data including education.
- **[`menthorlabs/menthor`](https://github.com/menthorlabs/menthor)** (Apache-2.0, `582★`) — Portuguese-first programming education platform, ⚠️ **dormant since `2024-04-21`** and filed as a fork base on that basis.

🟢 **Opportunity, stated concretely — and it is the sharpest arithmetic on this page.** `87%` of LAC institutions use AI; `26%` have a framework; `55%` have no budget and public universities **`4%`**. The gap is **not** tooling and **not** willingness — it is **governance capacity at near-zero budget**. The sellable unit is therefore a **fixed-scope, low-cost governance-and-evidence package** built from permissive parts that need no licence spend: `ltijs` for launch context, `ralph` for the record, `bncc-dados` for curriculum provenance, `terracotta` where an effect claim must be defended. `T32` recorded that in LATAM adoption and licensability are inversely correlated and licence remediation is the cheapest intervention; `p673` adds that the **remediation targets are now all on this base's own shelves**, and three of the four are Brazilian or Chilean in origin.

What *is* readable from it is qualitative and already held here: that infrastructure, device access and digital literacy gate AI-tutor adoption in much of the region. **Filed nowhere.** 🔵 **Recorded so the next pass does not re-buy it — a vendor report title is not a market figure**, and this page's market-size spread is already wide enough without adding an unread point to it.

Marking written work is the largest single consumer of the scarce resource. **That is the clearest demand-to-supply match this page has recorded for LATAM** — and it is gated, as ever, on the `26%`-with-a-strategy governance figure above, which is why `compose/patterns.md` **P42** puts the rubric and the human-review gate before the model.

🆕 **p670 — the LATAM leg nil'd, and the gap it leaves is the same one p669 left, so it is restated as a standing gap rather than a new one.** Re-served and held: Brazil's **PL 2338/2023** past the Senate and pending in the Chamber (`14` hits) plus the region's first AI regulatory sandbox; Mexico's conflicting 2026 status — one reading has binding corporate AI obligations in force, another has no AI bill enacted, and a February 2026 bill would create a risk-classifying regulator; Mexico's **Chapultepec Principles** from ATDT/SECIHTI, January 2026 (`1`); the **INFOTEC + TecNM** Public AI Training Centre with 14 technology companies and a **10,000-specialist target for 2026** (`10`/`7`); OECD AI Principles adopted by Brazil and Mexico; the `45%` AI-guidance figure for Latin America and the Caribbean against ~`70%` in North America and Europe. 🔴 **Declared explicitly, because silence here looks like coverage: the leg returned no 2026 data on AI curricula, edtech adoption, or education-specific regulation for either Brazil or Mexico.** Everything above is general AI governance plus one workforce-training programme. **The education-specific LATAM regulatory picture remains unmeasured by this base**, and that is a gap in the sources, not in the region. 🔵 **The `45%`-versus-`70%` guidance gap stays the most actionable LATAM number on this page** — it prices a governance-first engagement, and the INFOTEC/TecNM programme names the counterpart who is already buying capability at scale. ⚠️ **The AGPL document-editor exposure found at p670 applies here too and is worse where budgets are tighter:** a managed-service model over a forked AGPL editor is the one architecture to refuse outright. Country-level: **Uruguay `75%` of public-school teachers** (Ceibal, 2026); **Brazil `53%` of school administrators using AI but only `22%` with school guidelines** (Cetic.br, 2026). The **UNESCO Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean launched 14 April 2026** at ECLAC in Santiago. 🟢 **The governance gap *is* the engagement, and it is the cleanest one in this document:** a `61`-point spread between use and framework is a demand signal for exactly the governed-deployment work this KB's shelves support — Moodle dominance in the region plus `TutorIA`, `Profe Bit` and `EduRAG` as placed LATAM rows, with `portabilis/i-educar` and `academico` on the administration side.

##### 🆕 p666 — the mandated LATAM leg returned nothing this page did not already hold

🟡 **Nil, with its list.** `6` checkable facts, all held: the **Digital Education Council LATAM 2026** survey (**`92 %`** of students and **`79 %`** of faculty engaging with AI, **`30,000+`** responses across **`29`** institutions, run with **Tecnológico de Monterrey**); LATAM faculty **`72 %`** positive on AI against a **`57 %`** global average, with the most conservative use in **assessment** tasks; **Brazil, Chile and Uruguay** in the most advanced regional readiness tier; most countries holding an AI strategy while **only Brazil and Colombia explicitly budget for one**; and **more than half** of regional countries having placed AI in school curricula.

🟢 **The one new LATAM asset this pass produced came from the repository channel, not the news channel** — and it is on `agents/top.md`: [`GuilhermeScarpini/agente-bncc-professores`](https://github.com/GuilhermeScarpini/agente-bncc-professores), **MIT**, which binds lesson-plan and exam generation to **official BNCC skill codes**. 🔵 **Read it against the survey finding above:** the region's faculty are most conservative precisely where this agent operates — **assessment** — so the adoption obstacle is not capability but **inspectability**. An artefact traceable code-by-code to the national curriculum is the direct answer to that hesitancy, which is why `P33` is scoped around the binding rather than the model.

##### 🆕 p665 — the mandated LATAM leg returned nothing this page did not already hold, and here is the list

The leg was run (`AI education LATAM 2026 adoption regulation players`). **Already held:** the Universidad de los Andes count of **716** AI regulatory instruments across 23 countries and territories with **>80 % initiated since 2023**; UNESCO's **Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean** out of Santiago and its partner set (CAF, **CENIA**, CETIC.br, ECLAC, Fundación Santillana, Tecnológico de Monterrey, ProFuturo, Fundación **Ceibal**); Brazil's ANPD-coordinated governance proposal and Chile's high-risk advisory-council model. **Nothing new: `0` facts added.** 🔵 **Recorded as an informed nil**, and worth naming plainly: LATAM is the region where this page's *market* intelligence is most complete and this base's *supply* intelligence is thinnest in production terms — three placed agent rows, all research or pilot artefacts, against a market the same sources size in the billions. **That is the gap a LATAM engagement is sold into, and it has not moved this pass.**

**Regulatory posture:** the least settled region. Only **Peru** has an enacted AI law. **Brazil**'s EU-inspired bill is the most developed framework in the region (tiered risk, civil liability) but remains pending; a regulatory sandbox launched in 2025. **Mexico** has no horizontal AI law; a February 2026 bill would create a body to classify AI systems by risk. **Chile**'s comprehensive bill is pending (Boletín 16821-19), with the National AI Policy updated in 2024 carrying explicit funding and sectoral mandates.

**Adoption:** usage far ahead of governance — and p651 adds the denominators that were missing.

| Reading | Scope | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **87% use AI in at least one area; only 26% have a formal strategy; 55% have no specific AI budget** | ~200 higher-education institutions across **19 countries** | UNESCO IESALC with UNU-IAS |
| **92% of students and 79% of faculty** actively engaging with AI | LATAM higher education | Digital Education Council, LATAM survey 2026 |
| **53% of school administrators use AI; only 22% say their school has guidelines on its use** | **Brazil**, K-12 | Cetic.br |
| >50% of teachers in Chile and Brazil use AI tools; <10% of institutions have formal guidelines or sufficient capacity | regional | UNESCO |
| **716 AI-related regulatory instruments, rules and legislative proposals across 23 countries and territories** | regional | Universidad de los Andes, 2026 |

🟢 **p652 re-derived the IESALC figures from an independent search and they reproduce**: ~200 higher-education institutions, 19 countries, **87%** using AI in at least one area, **26%** with a formal strategy, and **~9% evaluating what their AI use delivers**. The 9% outcome-evaluation figure is new to this page and is the most damning of the three — it means roughly one institution in eleven can say whether any of this worked. Two further national readings, also new: **75% of public-school teachers in Uruguay** use these tools, and **65% of Brazilian internet users aged 9–17** report using AI. The Uruguayan figure is the highest teacher-adoption number in this KB for any country in any region.

🔵 **Two of those figures look contradictory and are not.** UNESCO's "<10% have formal guidelines" and IESALC's "26% have a formal strategy" measure different objects — an operative guideline document that staff must follow, versus an institutional strategy. Quote them separately and name the scope; a client will notice if they are blended. The pair is also the clearest statement of the regional problem: roughly a quarter of institutions have decided what they want, under a tenth have written down what staff may do, and **more than half have allocated no money at all.** UNESCO launched its **Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean** on 14 April (regional event in Santiago). A Chilean university reported **194 pedagogical agents across nearly 100 courses serving 4,600+ students** in February 2026 — the most concrete agentic-education deployment found anywhere this pass. Mexico's Public AI Training Center (INFOTEC + TecNM with 14 companies) targets 10,000 AI specialists in 2026 and 25,000/year thereafter. **Tecnológico de Monterrey signed an agreement with UNESCO on AI in education in March 2026** — the region's most visible institutional partner, and a reference account for any peer-institution sell.

**🆕 New at p653:**

| Reading | Scope | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 🔴 **45% of institutions in Latin America and the Caribbean have AI guidance or are developing it, against ~70% across Europe and North America** | regional | UNESCO (secondary) |
| 🟢 **UNESCO launched the Regional Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean on 14 April 2026 in Santiago, Chile.** Named partners: **CAF, CENIA (Chile), Cetic.br/NIC.br, ECLAC, Fundación Ceibal (Uruguay), Fundación Santillana, Tecnológico de Monterrey, ProFuturo, Universidad del Desarrollo, IRCAI.** A further partnership with Efekta Education Group was announced in October 2026 | regional | UNESCO |
| **UNESCO IESALC's standing recommendations to governments**: bring higher education into national AI strategies, fund it specifically, publish national guidance, and **integrate AI literacy and governance into accreditation and quality assurance** | regional | UNESCO IESALC |

🟢 **The accreditation recommendation is the most actionable item in this section and deserves to be read as a demand signal.** This region's defining problem, already measured above, is that usage runs far ahead of governance — 87% of institutions using AI, 26% with a strategy, 55% with no AI budget. **A governance deliverable with no budget line is a hard sell; the same deliverable attached to accreditation is a compliance obligation with an owner and a deadline.** If IESALC's recommendation lands in any national quality-assurance framework, the LATAM governance-first play in `compose/patterns.md` **P5** stops being a discretionary purchase. Worth monitoring per country, and worth naming in a proposal as the trajectory even before it lands.

🔵 **The named partner list is itself usable intelligence** — it is the shortest available map of who holds convening power on education AI in this region, and `Fundación Ceibal`, `CENIA` and `Tecnológico de Monterrey` are all plausible engagement counterparties or references rather than merely institutions to cite.

**🆕 New at p654 — Brazil's AI sandbox, measured rather than mentioned.** This page has carried
"a regulatory sandbox launched in 2025" for several passes. It is a named instrument with a published
end date, a scoring rubric and a result worth recording:

| Reading | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **The ANPD's pilot AI-and-data-protection regulatory sandbox runs until December 2026** | Up to three participants, selected under **Edital/Notice 2/2025**. Testing began **March 2026**; the ANPD published a first partial monitoring report in **early July 2026** and held a public consultation on **13 August 2026** setting four themes | ANPD launch notice + monitoring coverage (secondary) |
| 🔴 **A participant scored 45 of 100 on the sandbox's ethics framework — against a stated continuation floor of 50 — and was kept in** | The participant is Synapse (metro route recommendation) | ppc.land (secondary, single source) |
| 🟡 **The participant list does not reconcile across sources** | One list: Synapse, **Prevvine Tecnologia** (STAIDOC, a medical LLM chatbot), **Metatext**. The October 2025 Notice 2/2025 selection list instead names Metatext, Synapse and **IA Greenworld**. The third slot is disputed | two secondary lists, unreconciled |
| **A follow-on sandbox cycle is signalled but undated** | A legal bulletin refers to the regulator moving on to its next cycle; no dates published | TozziniFreire bulletin (secondary) |

🔴 **None of the participants is an education project, and that is the finding.** The sandbox is a
**mechanism** precedent, not an education precedent — it does not weaken this page's standing gap that
Brazil has published no education-sector AI rules. Read correctly it is still the most concrete LATAM
opportunity on this page, for two reasons. First, **it is a closing window**: a December 2026 end date is
seven weeks out, so an engagement that intends to use *this* cycle is already late, and the realistic play
is positioning for the undated next cycle. Second, **the 45-of-100 result is the more durable
intelligence**: a regulator that keeps a participant below its own published floor is telling the market
that this cycle is calibration rather than enforcement. Both facts are secondary-sourced and the
participant list is contradictory, so verify against the ANPD's own site before either goes in front of a
client.

🔴 **Unchanged and still the sharpest asymmetry in this KB:** no LATAM education agent repository has been found in any pass under a usable licence. The one real, LATAM-placed project (`Micel357/Edu-mentor-AI`, Recife, Brazil) has **no grant file**. The region with the widest adoption gap has the thinnest open source supply.

**Opportunities:**
- 🔴 **Qualify on budget before qualifying on need.** **55% of LATAM universities have no specific AI budget** (IESALC). The governance gap is real and the demand is real, but in more than half of these institutions there is no line item to sell into. The practical consequence: lead with a small, fixed-fee governance artefact that a dean can authorise from discretionary funds, and use it to create the budget line for the implementation work — not the reverse. An engagement sized to the need will not close in a majority of this market.
- **The governance gap is the largest single opportunity in this KB.** >50% teacher usage against <10% institutional guidelines is an unmanaged-risk position at national scale. Institutional AI governance frameworks plus the technical controls to enforce them, delivered in Spanish and Portuguese, is a repeatable offering across the region.
- 🟢 **The ~9% outcome-evaluation figure is a product, not just a statistic.** 87% adoption against 9% evaluation means the region's institutions have no instrument for answering their own boards. A measurement and evaluation wrapper — baseline, control, learning-outcome instrumentation, reporting a dean can take upstairs — is small, fixed-fee, authorisable from discretionary funds, and it is the thing that creates the budget line that 55% of these institutions lack. It is also the offer most defensible against this KB's own **T10**: nobody has to believe a vendor's efficacy claim if the engagement's deliverable is the client's own measurement. See `compose/patterns.md` **P5**.
- 🔵 **Uruguay is the highest-adoption teacher population in this KB (75%, public schools) and it is small enough to be a national reference account.** A country-scale proof point in Spanish, with a public-sector buyer and existing teacher uptake, is worth more as a reference than a larger fragmented market. Note also that Uruguay has academic standing in this field: the **RETUYT-INCO** group (Universidad de la República) published on AI-tutor evaluation with lightweight models at the BEA 2025 shared task — a local research partner rather than an imported one.
- **The Chilean 194-agent deployment is a reference architecture.** It proves scaled pedagogical agents work in a LATAM university. That is a sellable case pattern for every peer institution in the region.
- **Moodle and Chamilo are the installed base**, both GPL-3.0 and both reachable with out-of-tree agents over REST. No migration required — the lowest-friction entry in any region.
- 🟢 **Position for the ANPD's next sandbox cycle, not this one.** The pilot closes December 2026. A sandbox place is the cheapest regulatory cover available in this region for a product that does student profiling or automated assessment, and no education project has held one yet — so the slot is uncontested in this vertical. The deliverable that wins it is the governance artefact this section already recommends selling, which means the same small fixed-fee engagement serves both purposes.
- **Low regulatory load is a window, not a permanence.** Brazil's bill will pass in some form. Designs that are already EU-AI-Act-shaped will survive it; expedient ones will need rebuilding.
- Mexico's training-capacity push signals public money moving toward AI skills — adjacent demand for curriculum and platform work.
- 🟢 **p655 — the adoption-versus-governance gap in LATAM is now measured, and it is the sharpest in the world.** Across the **13 regional countries assessed by PISA 2025, nine in ten 15-year-olds have already used an AI chatbot for schoolwork** (UNESCO). In higher education **87% of institutions report using AI in teaching, research or administration but only 26% have a formal AI strategy** (UNESCO IESALC, 2026), and adoption is driven by individual academics rather than institutional plans. In Brazil, **53% of school administrators use AI while only 22% report their school has any guidelines** (Cetic.br, 2026). **Universal use, near-absent governance.** The first sellable engagement in this region is therefore not a tutor — it is the governance and measurement wrapper in `compose/patterns.md` **P5**, and the Brazilian 53/22 split is the single most persuasive slide available for it.
- 🟢 **p655 — regulatory fragmentation is quantified, which changes how to price multi-country work.** A 2026 Universidad de los Andes study counts **716 AI-related regulatory instruments, rules and legislative proposals across 23 countries and territories, more than 80% initiated since 2023**. Education-specific rules remain thin and **Mexico's general AI proposal replicates the EU risk model**. The practical consequence: an EU-AI-Act-shaped design is the cheapest way to be forward-compatible across the region, and multi-country rollouts should be priced per jurisdiction, not per region.
- 🟢 **p655 — the institutional map is now concrete, and it is who to partner with rather than sell to.** The **UNESCO Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean launched 14 April 2026 in Santiago**, run by UNESCO with **CAF**, and partners **CENIA (Chile), Cetic.br, ECLAC, Fundación Ceibal, Fundación Santillana, Tecnológico de Monterrey, ProFuturo and IRCAI**. A **Mexico pilot is co-building centres of excellence with CONALEP and DGETI** — the federal technical and vocational networks, i.e. hundreds of institutions behind one relationship. **Latam-GPT (CENIA, ~50B parameters, Spanish-language corpora including Colombian school textbooks) is explicitly open-source and public**, which makes it the sovereignty answer for a regional tutoring deployment. The **Raspberry Pi Foundation's Experience AI**, with $4.6M from Google.org, targets **24,000 educators and 1.25M students by 2028 across nine countries including Brazil and Mexico** — AI literacy at scale, and adjacent demand for exactly the curriculum and platform work above.
- 🟢 **p655 — the permissive pedagogy base for this region exists and has a regional deployment record.** `oppia/oppia` (**Apache-2.0, 6,974★**, branch `develop`) is misconception-responsive structured-lesson delivery, built for low prior attainment and low bandwidth, with ministry and NGO deployments in Brazil. Apache-2.0 permits a closed, client-branded derivative, which Moodle (GPL-3.0) and Chamilo (GPL-3.0) — the regional installed base — do not. **The correct architecture is both:** Moodle/Chamilo stay the system of record, Oppia supplies the instructional layer.
- 🔴 **p655 — an informed gap, searched directly and stated rather than left silent: there is still no LATAM-origin open source education-AI repository with meaningful adoption.** Six targeted GitHub searches were run this pass (Spanish and Portuguese terms — *educación*, *educativo*, *educação*, *aprendizaje adaptativo*, *tutor inteligente*, ENEM, Moodle+IA, Ceibal, ProFuturo). **Every LATAM-origin result was a sub-5-star student or coursework project**, several of them literally graded group assignments. The region's real AI-in-education assets are **institutional, not code**: Latam-GPT, the UNESCO Observatory, Ceibal, ProFuturo, Cetic.br. **This is a positioning fact, not an absence of information.** A LATAM engagement is served by global permissive code plus regional institutional partnership; anyone promising a "local open source base" is describing something that does not exist.


##### 🟢 New at p656 — the cheapest fix for the PoC-to-P&L gap is a statement log, and it is permissive

🔴 **This page's sharpest LATAM finding is a governance and evidence gap, not a technology gap:** high usage against formal AI strategy at a small minority of institutions, and under a third of proofs-of-concept reaching P&L. p656 shelved the components that make a LATAM pilot *defensible*, which is the binding constraint on whether it survives into a budget cycle.

🟢 **Why this fits LATAM specifically, on three counts.** First, **licence**: Moodle (GPL-3.0) and Chamilo (GPL-3.0) dominate the regional installed base, and an LTI 1.3 launch is a separate work — so [`tsugiproject/tsugi`](https://github.com/tsugiproject/tsugi) (Apache-2.0) or [`dmitry-viskov/pylti1.3`](https://github.com/dmitry-viskov/pylti1.3) (MIT) puts a Globant-owned tool inside the ministry's Moodle **without forking it and without inheriting GPL**. Second, **infrastructure**: [`yetanalytics/lrsql`](https://github.com/yetanalytics/lrsql) runs on SQLite or a single PostgreSQL instance — no cloud analytics contract, no egress, which is both cheaper and the data-residency answer. Third, **the sale**: a per-learner statement log is what converts "the pilot felt good" into a cohort delta a finance director will fund. **P5** has sold governance-and-measurement in this region without naming a component; **P17** names them.

⚠️ **Scope the dependency honestly, because it is thinner than it looks.** `lrsql` is the **only** live, maintained, permissively-licensed LRS in existence. If a client already runs `LearningLocker/learninglocker` — plausible, at 584 ★ and years of installed base — it is **GPL-3.0 and dormant since 2024-05-30**; the recommendation is migration, not extension, and that is a line item.

🔴 **No new LATAM-specific repository was found this pass.** The two permissive rows shelved at p656 with identifiable provenance are both EMEA (Newcastle University, France Université Numérique). Searched `topic:xapi`, `topic:lti`, `topic:scorm` and `topic:accessibility topic:education`; **no LATAM-origin project surfaced in any of the four.** Recorded as a measured absence rather than left as silence — and consistent with this page's standing finding that the region's AI-education activity is deployment and policy rather than published open source.

##### 🟢 New at p657 — the one live component of the measurement layer is Brazilian, and spoken assessment is the region's best-fitting new opportunity

**Supply.** [`douglasrizzo/catsim`](https://github.com/douglasrizzo/catsim) — **BSD-3-Clause**, 153 ★, pushed **2026-11-01**, tag `0.9.1`, default branch 🔴 **`dev`** — is the **only maintained component in the entire permissive psychometric-measurement layer** (**T31**), and it is Brazilian. This KB has repeatedly recorded LATAM as a consumer of open education software rather than a producer; **the single most load-bearing row in the layer added this pass is LATAM-origin**, and that is worth stating plainly in a regional pitch.

**Adoption, and it is the best-measured region in this KB.** UNESCO IESALC, across **~200 higher-education institutions in 19 countries**: **87% use AI in at least one area, and only 26% have any formal framework.** The Digital Education Council's **LATAM 2026 survey** (with Tecnológico de Monterrey and its Institute for the Future of Education): **92% of students and 79% of faculty actively engage with AI.** In schools: **75% of Uruguayan public-school teachers** report using these tools (Ceibal); in Brazil, **65% of internet users aged 9–17** use AI tools and **53% of school administrators** use AI while **only 22% report that their school has guidelines** (Cetic.br).

**Regulation.** Universidad de los Andes (Bogotá) counted **716 AI-related regulatory instruments, rules and legislative proposals across 23 countries and territories, more than 80% initiated since 2023** — volume without convergence. The regional argument is for **common definitions, risk categories, audit principles and mutual recognition** rather than a single statute. **UNESCO launched its Observatory on Artificial Intelligence in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean on 14 April**, and its recommendations to governments are specific: fold higher education into national AI strategies, fund it, issue national guidance, and **integrate AI literacy and governance into accreditation and quality assurance** — which converts governance work from optional to accreditation-critical. Country bodies to name: **Ceibal** (Uruguay), **Cetic.br** (Brazil), **CENIA** (Chile), plus the **UNESCO–Tec de Monterrey** agreement.

🟢 **The opportunities, in priority order.**
1. **Spoken-language assessment (new, and the best regional fit of anything found this pass).** English-language training is large, funded and measurable across the region, and `OpenPronounce` (MIT) + `wikipron` (Apache-2.0, which supplies Spanish and Portuguese lexicons) replaces a **metered per-utterance cloud API** with a CPU container — a cost argument that lands harder where budgets are tighter. See `verticals/solutions.md` Pattern K and `compose/patterns.md` **P20**.
2. **Governance tied to accreditation.** The 87%-use / 26%-framework gap is the largest adoption-governance gap this KB measures anywhere, and UNESCO's accreditation recommendation gives it a deadline-shaped driver. The cheapest credible deliverable remains an Apache-2.0 statement log (**T28**).
3. **Adaptive assessment on a LATAM-maintained component.** `catsim` plus a calibrated item bank is a defensible, explainable adaptive-testing engagement for a ministry or national platform — and the maintainer is in-region.

⚠️ **The standing caveat has not changed:** this region's constraint is the PoC-to-P&L gap, not enthusiasm. 92% of students using AI is not a budget line; accreditation and procurement are.


🔴 **New at p658 — a measured absence, stated because it is the fourth consecutive pass to find it.** The learn-to-code layer swept this pass produced **21 verified addresses and not one of LATAM origin.** Origins break down as North America (MakeCode, Code.org, Scratch Foundation), EMEA (Blockly/Raspberry Pi Foundation, micro:bit Foundation, ardublockly) and APAC (TutoriaLLM, openblock, exam-platform). This reproduces p656's result across four different topic heads and p647-I's finding that LATAM supply is not nil but is very thin and hard to place.
🟢 **The opportunity is unchanged in shape and sharpened in detail, and it is a localisation play rather than a build.** Every component of **P22** is MIT and **compiles in the browser and runs offline** — `microsoft/pxt` plus `openblock-desktop` need no egress at all. That matters more here than anywhere: connectivity, not licensing, is the binding constraint on LATAM classroom software, and this is the first stack on this page that is simultaneously permissive, brandable and genuinely offline. Combined with the region's measured adoption — **Uruguay 75% of public-school teachers using AI tools (Ceibal, 2026)**, **Brazil 65% of 9–17-year-old internet users (Cetic.br, 2026)** against **only 26% of 200 higher-education institutions across 19 countries holding a formal AI strategy (UNESCO IESALC)** — the sellable deliverable is a **Spanish- and Portuguese-localised national coding platform**, which is curriculum and translation work on a stack that is free and offline-capable. 🔵 Ceibal (Uruguay) is the natural reference client: a national one-laptop programme with published usage data and an existing device fleet.




🟡 **New at p662 — one named regional integrator, recorded and not ranked.** **Softtek** is named as a LATAM AI-in-education player alongside Microsoft, Google, AWS and IBM. Novel to this base (`0` hits anywhere). It reaches this pass through a market-research vendor page with no methodology attached, so it is recorded as **a name to know in a regional bid**, not as a market position. 🔵 It is nonetheless the only LATAM-headquartered integrator any mandated leg has surfaced in eighteen passes, which is itself the finding: the regional supplier picture this base can evidence remains thin, and that is a gap rather than an absence of suppliers.

##### 🟢 New at p663 — LATAM stops being a region with no permissive agent supply, and the reason it looked that way was the query language

**Supply — the standing “zero placed LATAM agent rows” is closed.** `agents/top.md` now carries **two**, both Colombian, both placed on a **named institution in the repository's own text** rather than on an author name or a README language:

| Row | Address | Licence (byte-read 2026-11-05) | Placement evidence | Depth |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **TutorIA** | [`LabSirius/TutorIA`](https://github.com/LabSirius/TutorIA) | MIT · 1,069 B · `346d7b5d3913` · © *Grupo Sirius* | **Universidad Tecnológica de Pereira**, rural higher education in **Risaralda, Colombia**; organisational licensor in the grant | `0★` / 4 commits · **Open edX XBlock** |
| **Profe Bit** | [`AndresGuido9820/trabajo2-agente-tutor`](https://github.com/AndresGuido9820/trabajo2-agente-tutor) | MIT · 1,085 B · `900b1f1215bc` | **Universidad Nacional de Colombia**, named professor and course | `0★` / **151 commits / 314 tests** · local-capable (Ollama + Pyodide) |

🔵 **The diagnostic finding is about this base's instrument, not about LATAM.** The four mandated English-language supply legs returned **zero** in-scope addresses for the sixth consecutive pass. Both rows came from **Spanish- and Portuguese-language** legs. This page has carried “no placed LATAM agent supply” as a *sector* fact; a measurable part of it was a **query-language gap**. Every regional supply conclusion on this page that rests only on English-language legs should be treated as provisional until re-run in the region's own language.

⚠️ **What this does not license anyone to say.** Both rows are `0★` research artefacts from university coursework. LATAM now has **placed, permissive, documented prior art** — it does **not** have an adoptable production base. The engagement shape is unchanged from what this page already describes for the region: *adopt-and-harden on a published decomposition*, not *ship someone's `0★` repository*. **TutorIA is the more strategically placed of the two** because it is an Open edX XBlock, and Open edX is the platform tier this region actually runs; **Profe Bit is the more engineered**, and the only one whose depth this base could even see, because the commit-count channel only became readable at p663 (`agents/trending.md` `663-A`).

🟢 **The denominator that reconciles this page's two LATAM adoption figures — new at p663.** This page holds the Digital Education Council LATAM 2026 figure (`92%` students / `79%` faculty) and **declines to quote it** because it does not reconcile with the UNESCO IESALC institution-level readings (`87%` / `26%` / `55%`). The sample size is the reason, and it was missing: the **DEC LATAM survey covers `29` universities** and additionally reports **`67%` of students using AI at least weekly**, against **IESALC's ~`200` higher-education institutions**. Two different populations and two different sampling frames — a self-selected `29`-university council network versus a regional UN-body census — so the figures are not in conflict and neither corrects the other. 🔵 **Usable consequence:** quote DEC for *intensity among engaged universities* (`67%` weekly is the number that matters in a proposal) and IESALC for *regional coverage*. Do not average them.

##### 🔴 New at p664 — the LATAM supply story from p663 was half right, and the other half is worse: the agents people actually use have no licence

🟢 **Promoted this pass: `ThallesYVasconcelos/Agente-Educacional` ("EduRAG") — MIT, LATAM**, a third placed LATAM agent row. It is an agentic RAG assistant **for Brazilian primary-school teachers (1º–4º ano)** that answers with page-level citations over public **MEC** documents and generates lesson plans **keyed to named BNCC skill codes (EF01–EF04)**, with **PCN** and **PNLD** in corpus and Self-RAG faithfulness checking. LangGraph supervisor pattern, ChromaDB + `bge-m3`, Ollama-hosted local models with `gpt-4o-mini` only as fallback, a FastAPI **MCP server**, and **Giskard** for evaluation. It had been in this base's ledger since **p469** under a verdict of `unstated` that reading the file refutes (`agents/trending.md` `664-B`).

🔴 **But the headline finding of this pass is a negative one, and it corrects p663.** p663 concluded that LATAM's apparent lack of permissive agent supply was a **query-language gap** in this base's instrument rather than a gap in the sector. That was true and it was incomplete. Running the legs in Portuguese surfaces the most-adopted Brazilian education agent this base has ever measured:

| Address | Stars | Forks | Grant |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`digitalinnovationone/dio-agent`** (DIO, a major Brazilian technology-education platform) | **2,988** | **367** | 🔴 **None.** No file at `LICENSE`, `LICENSE.md`, `LICENSE.txt` or `COPYING`; `gh api spdx = null` |
| `LabSirius/TutorIA` (Univ. Tecnológica de Pereira) | 0 | 0 | 🟢 MIT |
| `AndresGuido9820/trabajo2-agente-tutor` (Univ. Nacional de Colombia) | 0 | 0 | 🟢 MIT |
| `ThallesYVasconcelos/Agente-Educacional` (BNCC-grounded) | 0 | 0 | 🟢 MIT |

**So LATAM's real supply problem is not "no permissive agents exist." It is: the agents with adoption have no licence, and the agents with a licence have no adoption.** No amount of additional searching fixes that shape. Upstreaming a licence file does — and that is a short conversation with a named publisher, not an engineering project.

**Demand and governance, measured this pass:**

- **UNESCO IESALC**, across ~**200 higher education institutions in 19 countries**: **87% use AI in at least one area; only 26% have a formal strategy.** Just over a quarter have any formal framework at all.
- **Uruguay: 75% of public-school teachers** use AI (Ceibal, 2026). **Brazil: 65% of internet users aged 9–17** (Cetic.br, 2026). These are the two best-instrumented education systems in the region and it is not a coincidence that both have a national digital-education agency.
- **Fewer than 10%** of institutions in the region have formal guidelines **and sufficient capacity** to apply them (UNESCO observatory launch).
- **716 AI-related regulatory instruments, rules and legislative proposals across 23 countries and territories** (Universidad de los Andes, 2026) — most of them recent. The region is not under-regulated; it is **un-consolidated**.
- **Brazil: PL 2.338/2023**, general AI rules on a risk-based approach. **Colombia: CONPES 4144** national AI policy (February 2025) — a policy-first route rather than a statute. **OECD AI Principles** adopted by Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Mexico and Peru.
- **New institutional actor:** UNESCO IESALC launched the **Observatory on Artificial Intelligence in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean in April 2026**. **Tecnológico de Monterrey** has signed an AI-in-education agreement with UNESCO.

🟢 **Opportunities, and the first one is new at p664:**

1. **🆕 Licence remediation as a paid engagement, starting with the highest-adoption repositories.** `dio-agent` has 2,988 stars, 367 forks and no grant — meaning nobody downstream, including DIO's own 367 forkers, can lawfully build a product on it. Getting a permissive licence file upstreamed into the region's most-used education agents converts dead adoption into a reusable base, buys standing with the publisher, and costs weeks. **This is the cheapest high-leverage move available in LATAM and it is not an engineering project.** See **P29**.
2. **The 87%-use / 26%-strategy gap is the single largest governance opportunity in any region in this file.** It is a formal-strategy deliverable with a UNESCO-published denominator to cite — and **P5** (governance and measurement wrapper) is already the pattern.
3. **BNCC-grounded teacher assistance for Brazil, on a template that now exists.** EduRAG demonstrates the whole shape — national curriculum in corpus, output keyed to official skill codes, local models by default, faithfulness-checked. ⚠️ It is `0★` prior art, not a dependency: the engagement is to harden it.
4. **Spanish-language tutoring inside Open edX for public universities that cannot export student data** — **P28**, unchanged and still the best-fitting LATAM pattern, with `TutorIA` as the integration reference.
5. **Uruguay and Brazil as reference accounts.** Ceibal and Cetic.br publish the instrumentation; an engagement there produces measurable, citable outcomes, which is precisely what the region's `<1/3 of PoCs reach P&L` problem lacks.



🟢 **p667 — the LATAM supply question that `p647-I` left open is answered, and the answer came from fixing a measurement rather than from finding more repositories.** `p647-I` named four LATAM-plausible addresses and **deliberately promoted none**, correctly, because only a name-or-language signal supported them and `p647-H` had shown that signal mis-places between Portugal and Brazil. 🔴 **The probe `p647` relied on to do better could never have worked:** `location:` is silently unsupported on the `search/repositories` endpoint and returns `0` for every input (`667-G`). 🟢 **A working probe on `search/users` places two LATAM addresses on the owner's own location field** (`667-G1`), and the free-text spelling turns out to decide the answer: `user:portabilis location:Brasil` → `1` while `location:Brazil` → `0`, and `user:psiagoleal location:Brazil` → `1` while `location:Brasil` → `0` — **two accounts in the same country answering to mutually exclusive spellings** (`667-G2`).

🟢 **The commercially significant one is `portabilis/i-educar`, now on `verticals/solutions.md`.** It is the largest free-software school-management system in Brazil — enrolment, records, classes, grades, transfers and the statutory reporting a municipal education secretariat owes — and 🔧 **it is a re-filing rather than a discovery** — in this base's ledger since `p11` with `35` measurements across `24` passes, filed on a shelf once and `MOVED` off it at `p315`, and on no sell shelf since. Its adoption signal is the forks, not the stars: **`724★` against `555` forks, a ratio of `0.766`**, which ranks **third** on this base's own `forks-per-star` census and is its **only LATAM member of the `deploy-per-institution` class** — the signature of a system each municipality forks and runs as its own instance. 🔴 **Scope it as GPL-2.0** (payload read first-hand this pass, `18,092 B`): a configure-and-host engagement with a public body, not a white-label product, which is the same constraint this page records for Moodle in the region. 🔵 **Why this is the right LATAM entry point:** the UNESCO IESALC finding this section already carries — 87% of institutions using AI against 26% with any formal framework, and 55% with no AI budget — describes a governance gap, and the system that governance has to attach to is the one the secretariat already runs.


##### 🟢 New at p668 — the governance gap is the product, and the numbers that size it were re-verified

The Digital Education Council's LATAM survey 2026 puts individual engagement higher still — **`92%` of students, `79%` of faculty**. 🟢 **A 61-point gap between use and strategy is the clearest "the artefact *is* the deliverable" number in any region this base tracks.**

For a multi-country client this is `P6`'s policy-node shape again, and for a single-country one it is `P29`/`P33`'s licence-and-curriculum work.
- 🔵 **The institutional buyers are named and they are coordinating.** UNESCO's regional **Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean** (launched April 2026, Santiago) runs with **CAF**, and partners including **CENIA** (Chile), **Cetic.br**, **ECLAC**, **Ceibal** (Uruguay), **Fundación Santillana** and **Tecnológico de Monterrey**. 🟢 **These are the reference-customer routes, and `T5`'s LATAM deployment proof point is what to lead with.**

Returned the UNESCO LAC Observatory on AI in Education (launched Santiago, `2026-04-14`), IESALC's `87 %` using AI against `26 %` holding a formal strategy across ~`200` institutions in `19` countries, the `55 %` of universities with no AI budget line, Cetic.br's `53 %` of Brazilian school administrators using AI against `22 %` with guidelines, and Ceibal's `75 %` of Uruguayan public-school teachers — every figure already on this page.
- 🟢 **But LATAM is where p684's two genuinely novel facts came from, and they did not come from this leg.** **Lei 12.764** — Brazil's *Política Nacional de Proteção dos Direitos da Pessoa com Transtorno do Espectro Autista* — and the **LBI / Lei 13.146** were **absent from this entire knowledge base** before p684, and both arrived through the README of a `1★` repository found by a `topic:special-education` sweep. 🔴 **See `T81`: at this base's maturity, a working implementation cites the statutes its author must actually comply with, and secondary commentary does not.**
- 🟢 **The address filed, and why a `1★` prototype earns a shelf row.** [`Fernandosr85/sistema-gestao-pei`](https://github.com/Fernandosr85/sistema-gestao-pei) — **MIT** (`1,085 B`, sha256₁₂ `c4795c361c10`), `main` @ `3c2d87f18f05`, React 18.3 + TypeScript — manages **PEI** (*Planos Educacionais Individualizados*), the Brazilian counterpart of the IEP and a document a school is **legally obliged** to produce for each student with a disability. ⚠️ Prototype with fictional demonstration data by its own statement; filed to `verticals/solutions.md` because a statutorily mandated workflow had **no** permissive entry on that shelf, and flagged as such in the row.
- 🟢 **The commercial read for the region is the budget line, not the technology.** `55 %` of LATAM universities have no AI budget and public universities have the lowest rate of dedicated funding, which rules out anything that needs hosting. `DSL4-Digital/RefExists` runs **entirely in the browser with nothing to host**, and `OSLL/code-plagiarism` is a PyPI install on a laptop — so the integrity control that EMEA will buy as compliance is the one LATAM can deploy at zero infrastructure cost. **P60** is specified so that the client-side-only subset is a complete deliverable on its own.

### Global

#### 🆕 Added at p721 — the 2026 market bracket RE-MEASURED across six houses, and the figure this base publishes is a SPLICE of two incompatible series

🔴 **Correction to a number this KB has quoted for multiple cycles.** The rotation-log summaries carry *"AI-Edu-$9.58B->$42.48B(2030,41.5%)"*. **Those two endpoints come from two different research houses with different market definitions and different end years, joined into one false series.**

| House | 2026 base | Projection | CAGR |
|---|--:|---|--:|
| **Research and Markets** | **`$10.6B`** | `$42.48B` by **2030** | `41.5%` |
| **Precedence Research** | **`$9.58B`** | `$136.79B` by **2035** | `34.52%` |
| **Grand View Research** | **`$11.4B`** | `$57.2B` by 2033 | `25.9%` |
| **Coherent Market Insights** | **`$11.40B`** | `$57.25B` by 2033 | `16.8%` |
| **Trend X Insights** | **≈`$7.30B`** | `$61.26B` by 2034 | `31.50%` |
| **Technavio** | — | **incremental `+$3.37B`** 2025-2030 | `45%` |

🔴 **The published splice takes Precedence's base and Research and Markets' endpoint and CAGR.** `$9.58B` compounding at `41.5%` does not reach `$42.48B` in 2030 — the series is not merely mixed, it is **internally inconsistent**, which is the same defect class `p695` caught once before on this page.

🟢 **What this base publishes instead, as a bracket rather than a point:** **2026 AI-in-education is `$7.30B` – `$11.40B` across six houses (a `1.56×` spread on the SAME year), with 2030-2035 projections spanning `$42B` – `$137B` and CAGRs from `16.8%` to `45%` (a `2.7×` spread).** 🔵 **Technavio's figure is incremental growth, not market size, and must never be compared to the others** — it is the most likely of the six to be mis-cited.

🟢 **The demand-side reading that is consistent across sources, and is the more useful one:** 2026 is the year AI in education moves **from experimentation to governance**. 1EdTech frames it as governance and oversight becoming essential; the OECD's 2026 Digital Education Outlook and Teach Better both point to **purpose-built, controlled education platforms displacing generic open tools**; Campus Technology's panel expects AI to become **embedded infrastructure** across admissions, advising and administration; HolonIQ expects **selective** acceleration weighted to demonstrated instructional value; Faculty Focus ties growth to **teacher workload** amid shortages and burnout; and 1EdTech adds **digital credentials and competencies** rising on employer demand.

🔵 **Opportunity:** the convergent signal across all five regions is that **the buyer is now purchasing governance as much as capability** — a tutor that cannot produce its oversight trail, its data-handling posture and its disclosure behaviour is unsellable in North America and EMEA regardless of pedagogical quality. **That is a build requirement, and it is the same one in every region.**


#### 🆕 Added at p720 — the 2026 base holds, and a star figure this KB was carrying from prose was wrong by `27%`

🟢 **No change to the `USD 9.58B`–`11.40B` 2026 bracket recorded at `p717`; this pass's leg returned `USD 10.6B` (Research and Markets) and `USD 12.3B by 2026` (HolonIQ), both inside or adjacent to it.** The bracket is stable across four passes. **Cite the base, never the CAGR** — `p717`'s arithmetic objection to Coherent's `16.8%` stands.

**Demand-side consensus across this pass's leg, five independent sources saying one thing:**

- **HolonIQ** — systems becoming deliberate about where they invest attention and technology.
- **1EdTech** — the question is *how and under what conditions*, no longer *whether*; plus a shift toward a **skills-driven** credential ecosystem.
- **Campus Technology** — AI moving from experimentation to **embedded infrastructure** across admissions, advising and administration.
- **OECD 2026 outlook** — move **beyond general-purpose tools toward purpose-built educational AI**.
- **Faculty Focus** — growth driven by **teacher workload and burnout**, mainly via administrative relief.

🔵 **Those five converge on a single procurement shape and it is not a chatbot: purpose-built, embedded in an existing administrative workflow, justified by staff time recovered.** That is the brief to write against.

🔴 **A correction to this KB's own practice, not to a source.** A November-2026 trending roundup reports [`HKUDS/DeepTutor`](https://github.com/HKUDS/DeepTutor) at *"about 45K stars"*. Read from the API this pass: **`57,248`**. The same roundups give one repository `38K` and `40K` in two articles a month apart. 🟢 **Since `720-M` reopened the metadata channel, adoption figures on these shelves are read from the API and the prose figure is treated as commentary.** 🔵 **Generalised: a secondary source is adequate for market structure and inadequate for any number that a primary endpoint will return on request.**

#### 🆕 Added at p719 — the 2026 figure DISAGREES WITH ITSELF across two reputable sources, and the consensus theme has shifted from capability to governance

🔴 **A `$1.7B` disagreement on the current year, recorded rather than averaged.** Research and Markets puts the 2026 AI-in-education market at **`$10.6B`**, rising to roughly **`$42.5B` by 2030**. HolonIQ puts **2026 at `$12.3B`**. Both are current, both are cited by secondary outlets this pass, and **they do not reconcile.** A third projection reaches **`$136.79B` by 2035** on a different basis entirely. 🔵 **This base's standing rule applies: cite the bracket `$10.6B–$12.3B` for 2026, never a point estimate, and never the mean of two sources that disagree — an average of two incompatible methodologies is a number no source supports.**

**Demand-side readings.**
- **`86%` of students across 16 countries already use AI in their studies.**
- 🔴 **But only about `15%` of students WITH Khanmigo access actually use it** — the sharpest engagement-versus-availability gap in this pass, and the correct rebuttal to any proposal that treats provisioning as adoption.
- **Roughly two-thirds of higher-education institutions still have no formal AI policy.**

**The consensus themes converged this pass, which they had not before.** Five independent sources state variations of the same four:
1. 🟢 **Experimentation → governance.** 1EdTech frames the shift explicitly: policies, data boundaries and oversight are now the gating items. HolonIQ expects 2026 emphasis on ethics, transparency, agentic use cases and **proven instructional benefit**.
2. 🟢 **Embedded, not standalone.** Industry leaders expect AI to become embedded infrastructure across admissions, advising and administration rather than a separate product.
3. 🟢 **Education-specific over general-purpose.** Named as *the* defining trend by one source and independently recommended by the **OECD 2026 Digital Education Outlook**. 🔵 **This is the reading that most directly validates these shelves' existence:** a general-purpose assistant is no longer the default answer, which makes a curated per-industry open-source inventory the scarce asset.
4. 🟢 **Disciplined, bounded, measured adoption** — and **teacher workload** as the benefit that actually gets funded, via administrative-burden reduction rather than instruction.

🟢 **Cross-regional opportunity, and it follows from theme 1 plus the Khanmigo number.** The global pattern is that **provisioning is solved and VALUE EVIDENCE is not** — `86%` student use, two-thirds of institutions with no policy, `15%` utilisation of a provisioned tool, and UNESCO IESALC's ten-to-one using-versus-measuring ratio in LATAM all say the same thing from four directions. 🟢 **The engagement that sells in every region is therefore the measurement layer, and p719 filed both of its halves** (`719-E`): unbiasable items from a procedural generator, and deterministic judge-agnostic scoring with mandatory rationales. 🔵 **Skills and credentials** — employer expectations pushing education toward skills-driven pathways and digital credentials — is named by 1EdTech and is the one global theme these shelves still hold **nothing** against; it is carried forward as a declared gap.

#### 🆕 Added at p718 (2026-11-09) — the two cross-regional signals that every leg returned independently

🟢 **(1) The 2026 consensus is not about capability — it is about CONDITIONS.** 1EdTech states the live question is *how and under what conditions* to use AI, not whether. HolonIQ expects **selective** acceleration favouring demonstrable instructional value. The OECD's 2026 Digital Education Outlook and multiple independent sources converge on **purpose-built educational AI over general-purpose chatbots**. 🔵 **For this KB that is a direct mandate: a general-purpose agent wrapped in an education prompt is now the thing buyers are explicitly moving away from**, which is the argument for the `309` education-specific agent rows on [`../agents/top.md`](../agents/top.md) over a thin layer on a frontier model.

🟢 **(2) Digital credentials were named by the global leg AND the standards channel, independently.** 1EdTech points to the long-anticipated rise of **digital credentials and digital competencies, driven by employer expectations**. p718's `718-S` found this base held `4` of the `46` repositories published by the body that actually standardises them, and filed the mint-and-verify pair (`OpenCASE`, the Open Badges public validator, `cert-schema`). **Two unrelated channels converging on one capability in the same pass is the strongest signal this base gets.**

🔴 **The counter-signal that belongs beside every growth number in this file:** edtech VC funding fell to **`USD 2.4B` in 2025, down `89%` from the 2021 peak** (HolonIQ), with AI-enabled startups taking most of what remained. **A market growing at a `34–41%` CAGR while its venture funding falls `89%` is a market consolidating onto open components and incumbent platforms** — which is the commercial case for this KB, stated plainly.

⚠️ **Standing caveat on the teacher-adoption figure in circulation:** `83%` of K-12 teachers using generative AI comes from a secondary aggregator, not a primary survey, and should not be quoted in a client document without a primary source. The `60%` US figure in the North America section is from a different sample and is **not** comparable.


#### 🔵 p716 — Global: **the `2026` market bracket holds, the `86 %` adoption figure is re-sourced, and the fading list is now as useful as the growing list**

🟢 **Market, consistent with this base's existing bracket.** AI in education is put at **`$10.6 B` (2026) → `$42.48 B` (2030)** at a **`41.5 %` CAGR** — the series this base already holds and which `p705` found finally brackets. A much broader global **EdTech** figure of **`$238.4 B` for 2026** also appeared; the two measure different things and must not be mixed. 🔴 **The standing caution is re-confirmed rather than softened: specific market sizes differ significantly across research firms, and `p695`/`p664` both found internal arithmetic failures in these series.** Quote the bracket, never a point estimate.

🟢 **Adoption, with the source named.** **Microsoft's 2025 report puts generative-AI use among education organisations at `86 %` — the highest of any industry it measured.** Independently, **RAND** finds the share of **K-12 teachers using generative AI for their own work roughly doubled** between the 2023-24 and 2024-25 school years. 🔵 Two different instruments, two different populations, same direction — which is why this base treats near-universal adoption as settled and treats *governance* as the open variable in all four regions (`T198`).

🟢 **The authority to cite in any region: the OECD's `Digital Education Outlook 2026`**, whose position is that **AI helps learning only when pedagogy remains in control.** That is the sentence to put in front of a client that is buying capability and has not yet decided who decides.

🟢 **Market discipline, and it is the most strategically useful item in this leg.** Analysts describe **rising revenue against falling investment**: the market now **rewards products that can prove results.** The momentum/fading split is explicit:

| momentum | fading |
|---|---|
| AI tutoring and adaptive learning | 🔴 blockchain credentialing |
| AI-assisted grading | 🔴 full-classroom VR |
| micro-credentials | |

🔵 **Both columns are actionable, and the right-hand one more so.** *Falling investment plus a demand for proof* means an engagement must ship an **evaluation harness alongside the product** — which is why this base keeps `716-F`'s standards layer and the assessment cohort in scope: provable delivery requires a format a gradebook accepts. And **micro-credentials** having real momentum is corroborated by a vendor move in the same leg: **Instructure launched `Canvas Career`, a skills-first AI LMS for workforce learning** — the incumbent LMS vendor moving into skills-first delivery, which is the segment `tachkovsa/plerni` (filed to [`../verticals/solutions.md`](../verticals/solutions.md) this pass as a competency-model **LXP**) addresses on an Apache-2.0 grant.

🔵 **Channel finding that governs this whole file, from `716-C`:** the four mandated **regional** legs returned **`0` repository addresses** again, but produced essentially everything new in this section and the four above it. **They are an intelligence channel that this base had been scoring as a failed repository channel.** Judged as intel, they are the most productive legs in the mandate — and they should be run for intel and never judged on repository yield again.

#### 🔵 p715 — Global: **a licence-risk read that applies to every region, and a warning about secondary star counts**

🟢 **The pass's transferable commercial rule.** Across four independent causes of a `NOASSERTION` licence field — an unnameable family (EUPL), a bespoke licence under a language-suffixed filename (IDB), a content licence (CC BY-SA 4.0), and a `19`-byte assertion instead of a licence text (`frappe/education`) — **`4 / 4` grants were recovered and `0 / 4` were permissive.** 🔴 **`NOASSERTION` is therefore a weak *negative* predictor of a buildable grant, not noise around a permissive one.** For a studio the default must be *assume not buildable until the clauses are read*, and the specific tell to grep for is a **termination-at-discretion clause**, which no OSI-approved licence contains. 🔴 **A licence whose name contains "Open-Source" is not evidence that it is one.**

🔵 **Market size, unchanged this pass and still a spread, not a number:** roughly **`$10.6B` → `$42.5B` by 2030** on one series, against a **`$12.3B`** 2026 figure attributed to HolonIQ on another. This base continues to publish the bracket rather than averaging two methodologies.

🟢 **The demand-side consensus across all four regional legs is the same shape, and it is the engagement:** adoption is near-universal and **governance lags it everywhere**. Roughly two-thirds of higher-education institutions still lack a formal AI policy; `86%` of students across `16` countries report using AI in their studies; and only `15%` of students with Khanmigo access actually use it — 🔵 **that last figure is the most commercially useful number in this file, because it separates *procured* from *adopted* and prices the difference.** The repeatable deliverable is not a model, it is **governance plus adoption engineering**.

🔴 **Instrument warning, measured this pass.** Third-party star counts are unreliable **in both directions**. Blog aggregators reported `HKUDS/DeepTutor` at **`45,224★`**; the API says **`57,037`** — understated by `11,813`, a **`26%` error**, quoted to five significant figures. This shelf held `56,640`, `11,416` closer. 🟢 **Rule: `stargazers_count` from the API or the repository page only, and a precise-looking number in a secondary source is not more trustworthy than a round one.**

🔴 **`0 / 4` global supply legs produced a novel permissive address.** `top open source AI agents education 2026 github MIT` returned `5` addresses, `5 / 5` already held, and surfaced **no education-specific roundup at all** — its results were general agent listicles. `open source platform education ERP CRM MIT Apache` returned the **identical saturated set** `681-H`, `685`, `691` and `713` each recorded. `AI education industry trends 2026` returned market prose and `0` addresses for a `35th` pass.

🟢 **The productive global channel remains the one the mandate does not contain:** the grant placed **in the query** (`license:mit pushed:>2026-10-15`), which produced `11` of this pass's `15` agent rows. 🔵 **The structural market reading behind that: education-specific agent supply is now overwhelmingly AGENT SKILLS rather than applications** — `9` of `15` rows filed this pass are a `SKILL.md` and a protocol, not a service. **That shifts the studio's unit of reuse from a deployable to a specification**, and it is why `714-C`'s split-grant class matters: when the product is a document, the document's licence is the product's licence.

#### 🟡 p713 — Global: **THE MARKET-SIZE FIGURES DISAGREE BY `2.5×`, SO THE SPREAD IS RECORDED INSTEAD OF A NUMBER**

🔴 **Four sources in this pass give four different 2026 figures for the same market, and none is primary.** Recording the spread rather than picking one, because a single quoted figure from this set would be indefensible in a client document:

| 2026 figure | implied by | character |
|---|---|---|
| **$10.6B** (up from $7.52B in 2025) | aggregator statistics page | vendor-adjacent |
| **$11.40B** | market report | paid research, cited second-hand |
| **$12.3B** | HolonIQ | sector analyst |
| **$2.64B** (Europe only) | market report | unverified, regional |

🔵 **`4.6×` between the lowest regional and highest global figure, and `~16%` spread across the three global ones.** The honest statement for a client conversation is *"an order of `$10`–`12B` in 2026 on disagreeing third-party estimates"*, and **`ACTION TQ` registers finding one primary source** — the market-size cell has been carried on secondary research for many passes and that is a standing weakness of this shelf, not a p713 one.

🟢 **The global trend readings are more robust than the global numbers, and three of them agree across independent sources:**

1. 🟢 **From pilots to governed use.** Clear policies, data boundaries and oversight are becoming the precondition rather than the follow-up. This agrees with the regional evidence from **every** region in this pass — NA's `134` bills, EMEA's Annex III classification, APAC's Korea/Vietnam frameworks, LATAM's `87%`-use / `26%`-framework gap.
2. 🟢 **Purpose-built over general-purpose.** The **OECD 2026 Digital Education Outlook** recommends moving beyond general-purpose AI tools toward purpose-built educational AI, and teach-focused sources independently report a move to education-specific platforms with age-appropriate, curriculum-aligned safeguards. 🔵 **This is a direct endorsement of what these shelves are for** — and it is also why `HKUDS/nanobot` (`49,935★`) was read and **not filed** this pass: adoption is not relevance (`681-H`).
3. 🟢 **AI embedded in operations, not just teaching** — admissions, advising, student services, accessibility, administration and workforce preparation. The April 2026 ADA deadline is named as an accessibility driver.

🔴 **The adoption figure worth more than the market size, because it is the one that predicts engagement failure: `83%` of K-12 teachers report using generative AI tools — and only `15%` of students WITH Khanmigo access actually use it.** 🔵 **Teacher-side adoption is voluntary and high; student-side adoption is provisioned and low.** That asymmetry is the strongest argument on these shelves for weighting teacher-facing artefacts over learner-facing ones — and it is why this pass filed [`HKUDS/Paper2Slides`](https://github.com/HKUDS/Paper2Slides), [`instructure/canvas-rce-api`](https://github.com/instructure/canvas-rce-api) and [`edulinq/quiz-composer`](https://github.com/edulinq/quiz-composer): all three put the model's output where a teacher already works, rather than asking a student to visit something new.

🔴 **Source-quality caveat applied to this whole subsection:** of the sources behind the global leg, the market-size pages are vendor blogs or aggregators, and the `83%` / `15%` pair comes from a statistics round-up rather than the underlying study. **The three trend readings are corroborated across independent sources and the OECD recommendation is attributable; the numbers are not.** Treated accordingly above.

#### 🔵 p712 — Global: **`Global 107 → 111`, and for the first time the `Global` bucket is a MEASUREMENT rather than a residue**

`Global` remains the largest region on the agent shelf at `111 / 252`, and `p712` can finally say what it means. Four placement channels were run over `27` owners and `24` artifacts (`712-A`): a shipped locale catalogue or IANA timezone, the grant's copyright holder line, a named deployment or preset inside the artifact, and the owner's declared profile location. **`5` of this pass's `16` rows survived all four with nothing to place them on** — no i18n directory, no institutional holder, no named tenant, no declared profile location. For those rows `Global` is what the evidence supports.

🔵 **The practical reading for a studio: a `Global` row is not a worldwide product, it is an UNLOCATED one.** Most are single-author tools whose author chose not to say where they are. They are no less usable — `micho911/brightspace-mcp` (Go, one binary) and `Mattlo0546/classroom-mcp` (one dependency-free Python file) are among the cleanest artifacts filed this pass — but **a `Global` cell carries no evidence about the market that produced them**, and should never be read as evidence of reach.

🔵 **Cross-region structural finding, and it is a procurement fact rather than a technical one.** The whole `51`-repository Brightspace/D2L field authenticates by borrowing the user's own browser session, because **D2L issues no student API keys** (`712-E`). A Brightspace integration therefore needs **no tenant admin and no procurement** — the exact opposite of the Canvas constraint this base recorded at `p711`, where institutions switching off personal access tokens invalidates most integrations (`T179`). 🔴 **The same fact explains why the field is deep and commercially empty: `51` repositories, `9` substantive, `0` instructor-side, and nothing that can write a grade.** Anything that needs institutional authority cannot be built this way at all.

#### 🟡 p711 — Global: **the `2026` market series still does NOT bracket, adoption is near-saturation on the teacher side, and `Global 107` now holds a language-placed tail that is not global**

🔴 **The `2026` figures diverge again, and the divergence is definitional rather than resolvable.** `p705` recorded that the `2026` figures finally bracketed; p711's sources break that:
